# Ariva decision log

Product and architecture decisions in date order. Architecture decisions with consequences and alternatives are in [../architecture/adr/README.md](../architecture/adr/README.md). Numbers such as "decision 8" refer to the numbered scope decisions recorded in D1 to D3 and cited by D4 to D6. A later entry overrides an earlier one where they conflict; superseded entries are marked.

## September 2026 (D1 to D3 and the product owner; exact dates not recorded in the sources)

- Build the airport queue management system as a Dalil Tech product, with no current client. AUH is a likely first target; AMAN is not the immigration system at AUH.
- Separate product and codebase from AMAN, integrating through new aggregate-only contracts; hostable in AMAN's cluster or a separate one (decision 15). ADR-0001.
- Two separately sellable modules: the Border module for border authorities and the Airport Operations module for airport operators (decision 1). ADR-0002.
- Penalties are in scope: a handler SLA and penalty engine in v1.
- Officer-level data for border supervisors only (decision 8), and aggregate-only contracts from AMAN (decision 9). The conflict is resolved in D5: officer-level analytics stay in AMAN and never cross into Ariva. ADR-0010.
- Multi-sensor support is treated like e-gates: vendor-agnostic adapters, all ceiling heights (decisions 13 and 14). Hardware is bought on Claude's recommendation; a local integration partner installs and maintains.
- On-prem first. Separate border and airport deployments, with a one-way aggregate feed from border to airport. ADR-0012.
- AODB feeds (SITA, Amadeus and others) are mocked during development rather than waiting for real feed access (decision 11). ADR-0014.
- The developer works roughly half-time on the product until a pilot contract is signed (decision 17).
- Delivery team assumption: one developer using Claude Code, with the architect and product owner part-time.
- The first pilot is the Border module at one AMAN arrivals hall.
- AUH is approached as a component inside SITA's platform.

## 2026-09-28: D4 How It Works (approved)

- Sensors: overhead 3D stereo vision for ceilings from 2 to about 20 m; LiDAR above that, outdoors, in poor light, or where cameras need legal authorisation. Certify one product family of each before the pilot.
- Wi-Fi or BLE probing and CCTV analytics are not used for queue KPIs (CCTV only as a coarse overflow fallback).
- Continuity by overlapping coverage, never by re-identification. Track ids rotate at zone exit and never outlive the operating day. ADR-0011.
- Two wait numbers: realised wait (attributed to the entry interval, final only once the queue clears, used for reports and penalties) and nowcast (queue length and staffed-desk throughput, used for screens and alerts). ADR-0007, ADR-0008.
- Queue model: backlog recursion with Monte Carlo, not Erlang formulas. ADR-0009.
- Desk state is a state machine (Closed, Idle, Serving, Paused, plus Unknown) fed by signals in strict precedence; a passenger walking up is not a signal.
- Passenger screens show the nowcast in 5-minute bands with hysteresis, never a realised number.
- Validation protocol per site (manual counts, timed tracers, observer logs, at least five operating days including two peaks) and proposed acceptance targets, to agree contractually.

## 2026-09-28: D5 Technical Architecture (approved)

- .NET event-driven system on Kafka and PostgreSQL with TimescaleDB, deployed on-prem per authority per airport.
- Ariva ingests tracks and owns versioned zone geometry; vendor crossings are a fallback. ADR-0003.
- Nine bounded contexts; Border Integration and Flight Demand are anti-corruption layers.
- Stream processing in plain .NET consumer services with keyed state. ADR-0005.
- PostgreSQL with TimescaleDB Community Edition (licence checked for self-managed use); plain partitioning as fallback. ADR-0006.
- Four aggregate-only AMAN contracts: DeskSessionChanged, DeskIntervalStats, EGateIntervalStats, InboundFlightLaneDemand, with timestamps coarsened to the interval. ADR-0010.
- Python worker for model training and batch forecasts (v1); staffing optimiser in .NET. ADR-0013.
- Canonical flight model with AIDX and ACRIS adapters, mocks and a replay harness. ADR-0014.
- Signed, offline-verifiable licence file per deployment for module licensing.
- Hosted SaaS by Dalil is out of scope for now.
- Rebus for commands and sagas. Superseded on 2026-10-01 (Rebus not used).
- EF Core for relational schemas. Superseded on 2026-10-01 (NHibernate).
- Observability on AMAN's Prometheus, Grafana and Loki. Superseded on 2026-10-01 (Serilog and OpenTelemetry to SigNoz or Loki).
- OpenSearch optional for event and audit search. Narrowed on 2026-10-01 (not used in Phase 0 or the MVP).
- Unprefixed topic names (for example `tracks.samples.v1`). Superseded on 2026-10-01 (`ariva.<context>.<event>.v1`).

## 2026-09-28: D6 Roadmap and Delivery Estimate (approved)

- MVP: the Border module at one AMAN arrivals hall, validated against manual counts (23 to 38 developer-weeks with Claude Code).
- Four phases and three gates: Phase 0 demo core, gate pilot contract signed, Phase 1 pilot MVP, gate pilot accepted, v1 first sale, gate first commercial sale, v2 scale.
- Phase 0 builds only what needs no site, on simulated and lab data.
- First hire at the contract gate is a field engineer, before a second developer; the second developer joins at pilot acceptance.
- Pilot acceptance criteria as proposed in D6 (see the roadmap).
- Keep ADRs, domain definitions and a project guide for Claude Code in the repository from the first sprint.

## 2026-10-01: repository setup

- Product and code name: Ariva. ADR-0015.
- Repository on GitHub (`github.com/alsadderwork-coder/Ariva`, private; local clone `D:\DevOps\Ariva`), decided 2026-10-01, mirroring AMAN's layout: Backplane hosts Ariva.Api.Main (51001), Ariva.Api.Ingest (51002), Ariva.Api.Stream (51003), Ariva.Api.Cronz (51004), Ariva.Api.Integration (51005); Ariva.Business.Contracts; Ariva.Web (51010); Ariva.Simulation.Api (51020); Ariva.K8s (Helm and Helmfile); Ariva.Cicd (Azure DevOps YAML). ADR-0016.
- AMAN code conventions are inherited (Onion, `IStorageProvider`, `IUnitOfWork`, `SvcBase`, `Result<T>` and `Fx.Specification` from FluentX, AdminArea controllers, `Permission` attribute, FusionCache with Redis, Serilog, xUnit v3, Moq, FluentAssertions, Bogus). ADR-0016.
- ORM: NHibernate via `IStorageProvider`. `SchemaUpdate` in development only; production schema from reviewed SQL. TimescaleDB objects only from versioned scripts in `Ariva.Infra/Timescale/Scripts/NNNN_*.sql` with a `schema_version` table. Hot-path time-series writes use Npgsql binary COPY. ADR-0017.
- Kafka stays. Bus: MassTransit 8.5 with the Kafka Rider behind `ISvcMessageBus` (AMAN parity), decided 2026-10-01. Ariva adds the NHibernate outbox, inbox and dead-letter filters that AMAN's code lacks; the stateful Stream engine uses the raw Confluent consumer. ADR-0018.
- Rebus is not used. Long-running workflows are persisted state machines in Ariva.Core driven by Kafka events, with TickerQ jobs for time-based steps. ADR-0004 (amended).
- Topic naming `ariva.<context>.<event>.v1`, sensor events keyed by zone id; AMAN feed topics `aman.feed.<contract>.v1` produced by AMAN. ADR-0019.
- Background jobs: TickerQ in Ariva.Api.Cronz. ADR-0020.
- Live push: SignalR with Redis backplane and MessagePack. ADR-0021.
- Observability: Serilog plus OpenTelemetry to SigNoz or Loki. ADR-0022.
- OpenSearch is not used in Phase 0 or the MVP. ADR-0023.
- Front end: SvelteKit 2, Svelte 5, Tailwind 4, bits-ui, ECharts, svelte-i18n. ADR-0024.
- Ariva.Simulation.Api with sensor, AODB and AMAN emulators; reference scenario is the prototype's seeded day (seed 9303; scripted events at 18:05, 18:20 to 18:30 and 19:10). AODB mocks cover AIDX 22.1, ACRIS and SSIM import. ADR-0025.
- AMAN feed contracts V1 live in `Ariva.Business.Contracts`: DeskSessionChanged, DeskIntervalStats, EGateIntervalStats, InboundFlightLaneDemand.

- Alert email (ARV-040), decided 2026-10-03: a transactional outbox (`email_message`) written by the host that changes the alert and sent by Ariva.Api.Integration, the host with egress to the site's mail relay (wiki 05, flow 15), through MailKit; at least once; plain text from templates in Ariva.Resources; one recipient per message; limits per address per hour and per minute over all replicas. smtp4dev in development (compose) and in E2E as a pinned local .NET tool, so CI pulls no image for it.

- Flights (ARV-041), decided 2026-10-03: one flight model for every feed, keyed by site and the feed's flight key, applied by message time per field (schedule and each milestone), so ordering never matters; the stale-feed alarm is a system alarm (state in `feed_freshness`, a warning in the log, `Ariva.Flights` metrics for the monitoring stack, runbook section 3), judged per site against the flights due, not a user alert rule metric: alert rules watch zones, and a feed is not a zone.

- Integration clients (ARV-042), decided 2026-10-03: `X-TOTP-Code` on data calls is checked within the TOTP window but without the replay guard (the token exchange keeps it), so a client is not limited to one call per 30 seconds; a client has at least one allowed source network, and an exchange from outside them is refused before anything is counted, so only an address inside the client's networks can wear it down; per-client attempts over the limit (5 a minute) are refused without counting toward the lockout, which caps how fast even an inside attacker reaches ten failures; the failure count starts again at the lock and an expired lock is not renewed by a failure in the same call; a lock stops new token exchanges only, so tokens already held keep working until they expire (an attacker on the client's network cannot cut a running feed by failing on purpose); every change to a client (scopes, sites, networks, policy, secret, seed, disable) moves its token version, every token carries the version it was issued for, and a token of another version is refused on its next call; the scope check reads the client's scopes and sites from the database on every call, not from the token; clients are disabled, never deleted; two connectivity check endpoints let integrators test scope and site binding before the data endpoints of ARV-043 exist.
- Integration API batches (ARV-043), decided 2026-10-03: every Integration API path names the site (`/api/v1/integration/sites/{siteCode}/...`), so the site binding is checked before the action and never read from a body; answers follow Ariva's API convention (data, RFC 9457 problems) rather than AMAN's `Result<T>` envelope, and a batch answers 200 with per-item results even when items were refused; `Idempotency-Key` is required, kept 24 hours per client, and the same key with another body, endpoint or site is 422 (the IETF Idempotency-Key draft's choice); each client is its own feed (`api-` and its id), so freshness and the stale-feed alarm are per client and a body cannot write into another feed; batch bodies are read strictly (exact camelCase names, no unknown or repeated members, depth 8). batches are limited per host (8 at once and 16 waiting, before authentication and before any body is read, so a burst of 1 MB bodies cannot exhaust memory or the temporary volume) and per client (120 a minute per replica, counted only after the token is validated, so a forged token cannot spend another client's allowance), and a body within the limit is buffered in memory; the api-integration ingress takes at most 1 MB. Open: a unit of work disposed without being ended commits what was promised (pre-existing; the API's filter ends it explicitly, so the batch endpoints roll back on an exception).
- AIDX 22.1 inbound (ARV-044), decided 2026-10-03: validated against Ariva's own AIDX profile (typed elements Ariva reads, the rest accepted unread), because IATA's XSDs are licensed and not in the repository; customers licensed for them can add them (To confirm per deployment); the direction comes from the site's airports, so one AIDX feed can carry both arrivals and departures and legs of other airports are refused per leg; the flight key is airline, number, suffix, origin date and direction, shared with the JSON route's examples so both routes update one leg; the answer is an `IATA_AIDX_FlightLegRS` acknowledgement in the OTA pattern (shape To confirm per AODB); the root `TimeStamp` is required and is the message time, so a re-delivered old message is older than what it would overwrite and changes nothing (without it, a late re-delivery stamped with its receipt time would overwrite newer values), which is why `Idempotency-Key` can be optional for AIDX (many AODBs cannot set headers); check-in counters are not read from AIDX; operation times map onto the leg's milestone fields (with per-field message-time ordering, the same UTC and near-schedule rules and the same per-key lock) rather than separate `FlightEvent` rows, a deviation from the story's wording: what is lost is a per-milestone history row and the events route's "send the leg first" check, neither of which an AIDX leg needs since it carries its own identity. Order: ARV-044, ARV-045 and ARV-048 are built before the deferred emulators of ARV-029, with the test acting as the partner; ARV-029 then points its emulators at the finished endpoints.
- Outbound endpoints (ARV-045), decided 2026-10-03: the SSRF control is in the connection itself (`ConnectCallback` resolves, checks every address and dials only a checked one), not in a check before the request, so DNS rebinding cannot pass; no proxy for outbound calls (a proxy would hide the address actually reached; a deployment that requires one needs a design change); resilience is a small per-endpoint handler rather than a new library dependency; outbound endpoints reuse the integration permissions instead of new ones; the authentication kind of an endpoint is fixed for its life; the ACRIS flight profile follows the ACRIS Semantic Model names Ariva could confirm (To confirm per AODB); ARV-045 is built before the ARV-029 emulator, with the test's stand-in AODB; the two outbound constructions the scanner would flag (SEC-020 `new HttpClient` in OutboundClients, SEC-023 `new SocketsHttpHandler` in OutboundTransport) are allowlist entries awaiting the product owner's approval, not silent exemptions; moving an endpoint needs its secret again; TLS chains are built without certificate or revocation downloads.
- SSIM import (ARV-046), decided 2026-10-03: preview, then import only what was previewed, bound by a Data Protection preview token (file SHA-256, site, horizon, window, user, two hours; nothing is stored between the two); a schedule is a fallback feed by ownership, not by message time: each leg records the feed that set its schedule and whether it is a fallback (script 0029), a fallback creates legs and updates only fallback-owned ones, and a live feed always replaces a fallback's schedule (an earlier draft back-dated SSIM messages 29 days, which let an import overwrite a leg a live feed had pre-loaded more than 29 days earlier; replaced after the security review); a failed unit is rolled back and every import is audited in its own transaction with its outcome; the SSIM key of a time mode U arrival uses the UTC departure date while AIDX keys by the origin's local date, so near midnight one flight could become two legs (To confirm before pilot); legs expand within a 1 to 200 day horizon (default 60) rather than the whole season; units of 500 legs, each in its own transaction; a new `FlightSchedule` permission (System administrator); the arrival date is derived from the times rather than SSIM's date variation field (To confirm against airline files).
- AMAN feed and immigration endpoints (ARV-048), decided 2026-10-03: the immigration REST endpoints follow the Integration API pattern (`/api/v1/integration/sites/{siteCode}/immigration/...`, a batch with `items`, `Idempotency-Key`, per-record results) instead of the earlier bare arrays at `/api/v1/immigration/...`; the records are stored as received (script 0030) for the engines to use (ARV-047, ARV-049); Ariva.Core now references Ariva.Business.Contracts, a dependency-free package (the layering test allows exactly that); idempotency is by site and `SourceEventId` across both transports; both transports read the contracts with the same strict converters (enums only by their exact member name, as values and as dictionary keys, because `JsonStringEnumConverter` also accepts other cases, padding and comma-joined names; times only with an explicit offset), and Kafka refusals are logged and acknowledged rather than retried; the intake takes an explicit scope (one site for REST, the record's known site for AMAN's Kafka feed; an unchosen scope is refused); a desk code mapping counts only when its desk is at the same site; a refused record's key is its code only when the code has the mapping shape; AMAN site codes are Ariva site codes (To confirm per deployment); the demo seed maps AMAN's IN, OUT, EGIN and EGOUT codes; records may lie at most `Border:Feed:AheadMinutes` ahead (5, 1440 in dev and demo for the simulator).
- Arrival wave (ARV-047), decided 2026-10-03: in-block precedence on-block, then the later of landing plus taxi-in (5 minutes, Proposed) and the estimate, then the estimate, then the schedule; passengers from AMAN's lane demand, else the feed's estimate, else seats times 0.8 (Proposed), with the reference mix (To confirm per site; one mix per deployment until per-site settings exist); one delay for every stand (11 minutes) until stand types are known; the alert window is the 20 minutes from now + 5 to now + 25; a queue zone's lanes come from the desks its service zones stand at (the topology's own link, rather than names or a new zone attribute), each lane shared equally among the queue zones serving it; predicted-breach rules have no projection for the past (no projections are stored), so a backtest finds none (at most its last minute when it ends now); the lane split is border data, so airport roles (`TerminalDutyManager`) get flight and minute totals only and the split needs `ArrivalWaveLanes.View` (border shift supervisors, administrators); `ArrivalWave.View` is granted to border shift supervisors, terminal duty managers and administrators, not handler station managers (wiki 12: no arrival wave); the web strip is not built yet (the API is), and the predicted-breach evaluation now reads this projection instead of none.
- Desk sessions and e-gate coupling (ARV-049), decided 2026-10-03: Ariva.Api.Stream reads the AMAN records the immigration intake stored (by receipt time, with an overlap and the ids taken) rather than AMAN's topics or new `ariva.desk.signal.v1` events, so validation, desk code mapping and idempotency stay in one place (the intake) and no new topic is needed; one desk engine per site, under a per-site advisory lock, with its snapshot and read position saved with the minutes; AMAN's feed is the heartbeat of every AMAN desk (F10's T_stale on the feed), so a desk with no session stays Closed while AMAN lives and goes Unknown when AMAN stops; AMAN intervals are transactions completed, never a transaction in progress; e-gate rejects join one manual lane per deployment (VIS, To confirm per site) after one minute, with r measured over 30 minutes from 20 attempts up and the reference 0.07 below; the coupling is applied to the projected demand predicted-breach rules read, while the arrival-wave API keeps hall arrivals and shows r. Not in this story: staff and service zone readings into the desk engine (the stream drops non-queue zones), the desk term of the queue nowcast (it needs desk lanes joined to queue zones in the queue worker, and changes stored stream outputs), and a staffing plan for `DesksBelowPlan`.
- Outbound AMAN connector (ARV-050), decided 2026-10-03: a new outbound purpose `AmanFeed` (script 0032) rather than a separate connector record, so the endpoint registry's controls (step-up, sites, secret protection, the SSRF-safe transport, resilience) apply unchanged; `TotpClientCredentials` only, one site, the feed path ending in a slash; the pull runs in Ariva.Api.Integration beside the ACRIS pull and hands records to the same immigration intake as Kafka and REST (one validation, mapping and idempotency path; a record received twice is kept once); the position per contract is saved with the records it applied; the token handler tries at most one TOTP exchange per step, matching AMAN's replay guard, so a dropped token or a retry cannot spend AMAN's lockout; AMAN's paging (`after`, `limit`, `next`, the envelope) follows the simulator's mock AMAN and is To confirm against AMAN's Integration API; the status records counts, never AMAN's text.
- Web sign-in (ARV-051), decided 2026-10-03: Ariva.Api.Main is reached on the web origin (`Endpoints.main.baseUrl` empty; the ingress already routes `/api` and `/hubs` on the web host, and `vite dev` and `vite preview` proxy them), so the SameSite=Strict refresh cookie and the X-Ariva-Csrf check work without CORS credentials; the access token stays in memory, one tab refreshes under the Web Locks API and shares the token and sign-out over a BroadcastChannel, and a non-secret `ariva-signed-in` flag in local storage spares signed-out visits a refused refresh; `GET /api/auth/me` (allowed for a pending session) gives the web app the user's roles, permissions ("Entity.Action"), sites and first-login needs, and the sidebar filters by it while the server stays the authority; first sign-in changes the password, then offers TOTP enrolment (mandatory while the account is pending, which is every account where Auth:TotpRequired is on) and shows the recovery codes once; the step-up dialog answers RFC 9470 401s and resends the action once; an Account security screen holds password change and new recovery codes (the critical action the dialog is tested with).
- Live operations screen (ARV-055), decided 2026-10-03: the home screen joins the published profile's queue zones and the site's alerts on the live hub (WebSockets, token from memory, automatic reconnect, groups joined again after a reconnect); a zone is stale after 150 seconds without an update; near and over target use the reference thresholds of rule R-001 (10 and 15 minutes) until zones carry their own targets; the wait chart is the session's live minutes (realised waits and the forecast band come with v1); desk states come from a new `GET api/v1/sites/{siteCode}/desk-states` over `desk_minute`, and immigration and emigration desk states need a new permission `BorderDesks.View` (border shift supervisors and administrators), check-in counters and security lanes a new `AirportDesks.View` (terminal duty managers and administrators), so airport roles never see border desks, border roles never see check-in data, and handlers see no desks until handler tenancy lets them see their own counters; checkpoint codes become unique per site (script 0033) because desk minutes are keyed site/checkpoint/desk; `GET api/v1/admin/floor-plans?siteCode=` lists a site's plans so screens stop asking level by level. The Playwright test of the 18:05 alert plants what Ariva.Api.Stream writes (snapshots in Redis, the R-001 alert row and its notice), as the existing live hub and alert suites do, because Stream is not part of the E2E run; a run with Stream and the sensor emulator end to end is open (see the open decisions).
- Alert rules screen (ARV-056), decided 2026-10-03: the sidebar item becomes "Alert rules" at `/alert-rules` under `AlertRule.Search` (the open alerts stay on the live screen); the backtest preview takes its range in UTC, as the API does, defaulting to the 24 hours before the last whole minute, and shows times as "YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM UTC" in both languages; owner and escalation roles offered are the caller's own (every operational role and "every role of the site" for an administrator), a saved rule's roles stay selectable; the server now enforces that only an administrator creates a rule without an owner, gives a rule to every role or takes one from every role, since such a rule's alerts reach every role of the site, handlers included (the rule service only checked named roles before); enable and disable send the whole rule back (the API has no partial update). The story's "fires live at the same time" is proved where the live evaluation runs: an integration test ticks the screen test's rule live minute by minute and compares with its preview (Stream is not in the E2E run, see "E2E with Stream"); the Playwright test checks the preview against the saved rule's backtest through the API.
- Immigration screen (ARV-057), decided 2026-10-03: a queue zone carries an optional lane category in the zone profile (script 0034, versioned with the profile, frozen once published, outside the geometry hash), which links a lane to its queue for the wait per lane; the demo profile marks A-/D- CIT, RES, VIS, CRW and EG. A new permission `Immigration.View` (border shift supervisors, terminal duty managers, administrators) opens the screen; desk and gate rows need `BorderDesks.View` and are left out of other callers' answers by the server. The window is the last 15 whole minutes of AMAN's interval aggregates; staffed desks come from each desk's latest session event of the last day; desks and gates are shown by Ariva's codes through the AMAN code mapping, and unmapped AMAN codes are not shown. The extra manual load of e-gate rejects is an estimate (each reject at the manual desks' mean service time), labelled as such. For callers without desk data, a lane's service and cycle times from fewer than three desks are left out, like AMAN's small cells.
- Passenger displays (ARV-058), decided 2026-10-04: a display is a site's record (script 0035) with its settings and its entries as JSON (queue zone, label per language), audited without its credential; the player authenticates with its own display credential (`ardp_`, 256 random bits, prefix and SHA-256 stored like a device's, constant-time comparison) through a scheme of its own (`Ariva.Display`), never a user session, and polls every 10 seconds rather than joining the live hub (the hub serves users); the board answer carries each entry's nowcast with its age by the server's clock, and the band, the hysteresis, the wider band for a degraded nowcast and the stale fallback are the player's; the player's address carries the credential in the fragment. Permissions `Display.*` go to terminal duty managers and border shift supervisors (a border deployment has no duty manager) and administrators; creating a display and a new credential are critical actions (step-up MFA); changing a display's settings, labels or zones and deleting it are not, since neither reveals nor issues a credential and both are audited, and a deleted display's player is refused at once. Board languages are English, Arabic, Portuguese and Swahili; the Portuguese is European Portuguese wording and the Swahili is standard Swahili, both To confirm with a native reviewer per site.
- Users and access screen (ARV-059), decided 2026-10-04: one administrator screen at `/users` (`User.Search`) with an Accounts tab and an Audit log tab (`AuditEntry.Search`); roles change as their boxes change rather than through a save, since each grant and revoke is its own audited, step-up action on the server; an administrator's own account is read only on the screen (the server still lets an administrator correct its own name and email through the API). From the security review: an account is created with its sites in the same request (`CreateUserRequest.AllSites`, `SiteCodes`), a site-limited administrator must give one of its own sites, and an account without sites, whatever its roles, is administrable and visible in the audit only to all-sites administrators, because a site administrator who could reset an account during the step between creation and sites could sign in as it once it held wider sites; enabling a disabled account is a critical action (step-up), like the resets, since it gives access back. Account names are now one line of visible text (no control, invisible or broken characters except the zero-width non-joiner and joiner, at most 200), and the audit summary of an account quotes the name and email as JSON strings, so a name such as `x; roles=SystemAdministrator` cannot read as a field of the trail (CWE-117); summaries written before this keep their old form.
- Daily report and scheduled delivery (ARV-060), decided 2026-10-04: the report is computed on request from stored minutes, not from a continuous aggregate: `queue_minute` now keeps each minute's wait histogram (script 0036) and the report merges them per hour and day (F7); an hourly aggregate can come later without changing the answers. Its content is lane waits (every queue zone, with the lane category), the alerts the reader's roles see, and device uptime from the outage record; forecast against actual and staffing come with their stories. Report.View and ReportSchedule.* go to border shift supervisors, terminal duty managers and administrators; the handler's own-counter report waits for the handler module. Schedules send only to Ariva accounts allowed the site and the report, each getting its own roles' report, never to free addresses (the report is operational data). The day is the site's airport time zone (UTC for a site without an airport). TickerQ runs in Cronz in memory with the job's state in PostgreSQL (ADR-0020 implementation note), and its dashboard takes an operator key. CSV cells that start like a formula get a leading apostrophe, quoting per RFC 4180, UTF-8 with a byte order mark.
- Local orchestration (ARV-066), decided 2026-10-04: .NET Aspire 13.6 (dependency approved by Ahmad Al-Sadder 2026-10-04) as Ariva.AppHost in Platform/Cloud, development and test only, beside docker-compose.dev.yml, which CI keeps. The hosts keep their own settings (Database:*, Kafka:*, Redis:*, Email:*) and environment names; the AppHost passes them as variables rather than Aspire connection strings. Ariva.ServiceDefaults now owns OpenTelemetry, the health check service and HttpClientFactory resilience (no retry of unsafe methods) and maps no endpoint, so the security baseline is unchanged; readiness now checks the database (Unhealthy) and Redis (Degraded only, the cache is fail-safe). The Aspire MCP server runs from a pinned local tool.
- Load tests (ARV-071), decided 2026-10-04: a console harness of our own (`Platform/Testing/Ariva.LoadTests`) instead of NBomber, which the story named and the dependency approval covered: NBomber needs a paid licence for organisational use, so it was not added pending the product owner's call. The harness speaks the live hub's SignalR JSON protocol over a plain WebSocket and stands in for Ariva.Api.Stream through Redis, since Stream is not in the E2E run. The smoke size runs in every E2E run; the full run's numbers are in wiki 05 as lab measurements. If NBomber is licensed later, the scenarios map one to one. Its HTTP calls go through Apizr 6.4.2 (Refit interfaces) rather than a hand-built HttpClient, at Ahmad Al-Sadder's direction (2026-10-04); the Toxiproxy control client of the ARV-072 fault tests does the same. The harness calls each manager's Refit client directly, with logging, retries and caching off, so the client neither hides answers nor spends the measured machine's CPU on expression handling.
- Nowcast desk term (ARV-064), decided 2026-10-05 with the product owner ("go with your recommendation"): Stream joins AMAN's open desks and lane cycle time to each queue zone by level and lane (as the immigration screen does) and blends them with the exit rate (F8), live only (not in the zone snapshot, never in replays, so the golden replay and the bins are unchanged); the screens show "Data degraded" only for a stale zone or a degraded queue length, and mark an exit-rate-only nowcast as an estimate. The scripted demo's third event is the R-004 check-in P90 alert with the check-in nowcast; no SLA engine in Phase 0.
- Continuous health checks (ARV-114a), proposed 2026-10-06 by the implementation; its golden replay head and its role grants decided by the owner on 2026-10-06 (the next two entries): computed per queue zone and bin in the stream and stored with every bin result (`zone_health_bin`, script 0038). The occupancy for the conservation residual is the queue zone's reading plus the readings of the overflow bands heard so far (a band never heard counts as empty, so an unmeasured band shows in the residual), sampled at bin boundaries with the queue length's 2-minute freshness; the track completion rate is F18's literal ratio (exited over entered in the bin), with censored and still-open tracks reported apart. Physical capacity is an optional attribute of queue zones and overflow bands (1 to 5,000), outside the geometry hash (F22 unchanged); the demo profile v12 takes the scenario's snake capacities. A new permission `DataQuality.View` goes to border shift supervisors, terminal duty managers and administrators (the roles that own the site's zones and sensors and read the daily report; the checks are zone-level counts, no desk data, so the border per-desk rule does not apply), not to handler station managers (own counters only, no handler tenancy yet). The API reads one zone at a time over at most 31 days (the replay and recomputation range) and 3,000 bins, the latest revision of each bin. No screen and no alarms yet (alarms and degraded propagation are ARV-114b).
- Golden replay head after ARV-114a, decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-06: the seed 9303 reference replay's output hash becomes 3cce63edfb17660f53a3fb7eead6c8ae92b20c491135b406a7df8cb032199a6f over 3,202 outputs (was 8d13749079c0c0ca347dcd8c9e41f6708a7360b7145ad61af32c4d49cf0e0a2c over 2,716). The 486 new outputs are the bins' health rows; every earlier output is unchanged (left out of the chain, the health rows give the ARV-113 head back). Approved.
- `DataQuality.View` role grants (ARV-114a), decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-06: SystemAdministrator, BorderShiftSupervisor and TerminalDutyManager hold it; HandlerStationManager does not (own counters only, no handler tenancy yet). Approved as built.
- Overflow band occupancy and `OverflowDetected` (ARV-115), proposed 2026-10-06 by the implementation; the overflow minute rule, the locked replay value and the silent band decided by the owner on 2026-10-06 (the next three entries): (1) the overflow minute rule (TC-19): a minute of a queue zone counts when any of its overflow bands held anyone in it (the band's highest reading in the minute above zero); a minute without a band reading is not observed, never counted as empty; overflow minutes per bin are a plain view (`overflow_bin_15m`, 15-minute bins from UTC midnight) over `overflow_minute` (script 0039). (2) `OverflowDetected` is decided per closed minute, not per reading: once at a band's first occupied minute after an empty or unheard spell, once at its first minute with readings and no occupancy after an occupied one; a silent band keeps its state within the freshness window and becomes Unknown after it (see the silent band decision below). It carries site, queue zone, band, state, minute, the spell's start and peak people, and the profile version (aggregates only), keyed by the zone key, with an id derived from those values so a repeated checkpoint adds no second event. The stream writes it to the transactional outbox in its checkpoint transaction (the raw Npgsql transaction of `StreamStore`, not an NHibernate unit of work), and no consumer reads the topic yet (alert rules read `overflow_minute`). (3) `OverflowOccupied` for alert rules: per minute 1 when any watched band was occupied, 0 when the bands reported and held no one, nothing without a reading; a queue zone in scope watches all its bands, an overflow zone in scope its own band. R-002 is therefore evaluated, but only where a band has a sensor reporting occupancy; at DMO the reference evening never fills a band (A-VIS peaks at 148 of 190 people), so R-002 raises nothing there. (4) Seed 9303's reference evening never occupies a band, so the simulator gained a scenario case, `ScenarioConfig.OverflowEvening` (the reference day with the arrivals Visitors snake holding 60 people instead of 190; scripted events unchanged), and the sensor emulator now splits a queue at the run's snake capacity (the defaults unless a scenario sets others, so the reference traffic is unchanged). The golden replay of the reference evening is unchanged (3cce63edfb17660f53a3fb7eead6c8ae92b20c491135b406a7df8cb032199a6f over 3,202 outputs: no band reports in it, so no overflow output joins the chain). The overflow case's replay is locked in `Platform/Backplane/Ariva.UnitTests/Replay/replay-overflow.json` (d971a3170539758f5248bfcc01e181e3bfd9ab1aa51dfba638d8908be9948b9a over 1,692 outputs, of which 209 band minutes and 4 band changes), approved by the owner (next entries). The stream's store drops, with one warning per zone, a band minute or change the tables or the outbox would refuse, so a checkpoint never fails on one: in particular a zone key longer than the outbox's 200-character message key (a 17-character site and a 200-character zone name make 218) loses its `OverflowDetected` while its band minutes are still written (`OutboxLimits`; the NHibernate outbox now fails such a commit with the reason instead of a database error).
- Overflow minute rule TC-19 (ARV-115), decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-06: accepted as built. A minute of a queue zone is an overflow minute when any of its bands' highest reading in the minute is above zero; a minute without a band reading is not observed (never counted as empty). Formulas F17.
- Locked replay of the overflow scenario case (ARV-115), decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-06: approved, d971a3170539758f5248bfcc01e181e3bfd9ab1aa51dfba638d8908be9948b9a over 1,692 outputs (`Platform/Backplane/Ariva.UnitTests/Replay/replay-overflow.json`). The silent band rule below leaves it unchanged: in the case the band's sensor reports every minute of the range, so no band becomes Unknown in it, and the end of a replay's range is not a silence. The golden replay of the reference evening is unchanged too (3cce63edfb17660f53a3fb7eead6c8ae92b20c491135b406a7df8cb032199a6f over 3,202 outputs). Superseded on 2026-10-07: the owner re-approved the case at 8df69d9f46b3eb15d871b5e0e37c82f0c52ac62d2f1db6e022b0df0d7db569ba over 1,693 outputs after the F5 re-anchor fix (ARV-139b point 8).
- Silent overflow band (ARV-115), decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-06: "Unknown after timeout". A band occupied or empty whose sensor stays silent beyond the occupancy freshness window (2 minutes, the same `OccupancyFreshFor` as the queue length) becomes Unknown: with L its latest minute with readings, at minute L + 3 when no reading arrived in minutes L + 1 to L + 3, decided per closed minute against the watermark (pure, replay-safe). It raises `OverflowDetected` with state Unknown (the event's state is now an enum: Occupied, Emptied, Unknown); its next reading makes it occupied or empty again with a change. Unknown is neither occupied nor empty: `OverflowOccupied` and the overflow minutes count only bands with a reading in the minute, so an Unknown band adds neither a 1 nor a 0 (a zone whose only band is Unknown has no value, and a rule's sustain and clear runs neither grow nor break). The zone snapshot is version 6 (a version 5 snapshot restores its bands occupied or empty as written). Flagging zone health for a dead band sensor is deferred (not part of this rule).
- Zone keys that fit the outbox (ARV-114c), proposed 2026-10-06 by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner's review: the zone profile refuses a queue zone name whose zone key (the site code, a slash and the name) would be longer than 200 characters, rather than widening `outbox_message.message_key` with a new script. Why: one key limit then holds everywhere a key goes (`MessageKeys.MaxLength` in Core, which `OutboxLimits` in Infra follows): Kafka keys, the outbox's column, and the zone key columns of the stream's tables (220); widening the column alone would leave the limit to whichever store is smallest next, and a 200-character zone name is not a real need (the longest at DMO is 5). Characters are counted as PostgreSQL counts them (code points). Only queue zone names are bound: service, staff and overflow zones share their queue zone's key (`OverflowDetected` carries the band's name in its payload), so their names keep the 200-character name rule. The rule applies when a zone is added or renamed, and publishing validates it again, so a draft copied from a version published before the rule must rename such a zone first; site codes never change, so nothing else can push a key over. Validation messages are English, like the other zone profile rules (no localised validation resources yet), and never repeat the refused name. The device health sweep and report no longer fail on such a zone: it is skipped with one warning per zone per process and the metric `ariva.zones.not_keyable`, and the other zones and the device states commit (wiki 10, section 4.2a, says how to find and rename one). If the owner prefers the wider column instead, a new script widens `message_key` and `MessageKeys.MaxLength` follows it; the profile rule then relaxes to the new value.
- Staff and service zones in the desk engine (ARV-116), proposed 2026-10-06 by the implementation (the engineer). Owner decisions (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-07): (1) and the 95 percent bound accepted as the pilot criterion, to be re-measured on real counts in the pilot validation (ARV-104), since it is measured on Ariva's own simulator; (2) the 60-second sensor T1 kept as a Proposed, configurable value, to be confirmed on real sensor data in the pilot validation before go-live, because it was tuned against the simulator; (8) desk zones are modelled in the AUH Terminal A seed (ARV-139a), not added to the DMO demo. Points (3) to (7) stay proposed. (1) The agreement bound (Proposed in the story): from the staff and service zones alone, the desk engine's open desks (Idle or Serving for at least 30 seconds of a minute, as the nowcast's desk term counts them) are within one desk of the scenario's for at least 95 percent of the minutes of the reference evening. Measured: 206 of 209 minutes from 17:01 to 20:29 (98.6 percent), 182 exact (87.1 percent), the largest difference 5 desks at 19:30 when five Visitors desks close in the same minute (`DeskZoneScenarioTests`). (2) A sensor T1 (`Border:DeskFeed:SensorPauseSeconds`, Proposed 60 seconds, 10 to 180): a desk without a live login source is Paused once its staff zone has been empty this long, instead of after T1 (3 minutes). Why: without a login the staff zone is the only evidence the desk is staffed, and with the 3-minute T1 every short break and every closing counts as open for 3 minutes, which measures 93.7 percent within one desk (68.3 percent exact) and misses the bound; 60 seconds still keeps a sensor dropout under a minute from pausing the desk. With a live AMAN session T1 stays 3 minutes (unchanged). Agreement within one desk by sensor T1 (an exploration over 17:05 to 20:29): 15 s 100 percent, 30 s and 60 s 98.5 percent, 90 s and 120 s 95.6 percent, 180 s 93.7 percent. (3) A staff or service zone counts as a desk source only once it has been heard: a profile may draw a service zone to link a queue to its desk without a sensor over it (the XS2 probe does), and such a zone must not mark the desk Degraded for good; once heard, a silent zone is stale after T_stale like any other source (its desk Degraded, or Unknown when every source is silent). (4) Readings flagged by the sensing pipeline (a corrected or unreliable clock) make the desk's state Degraded while they are the zone's latest (F11). (5) `desk_zone_reading` (script 0040) keeps readings 7 days (Proposed, To confirm): they are an input, desk_minute keeps what they produced and the raw events stay 90 days in `sensing_event`. (6) Between the zone processor and the desk engine at most 60 readings per desk zone and minute pass, and a repeat of the latest count within 15 seconds is dropped (the source stays heard); counts above 50 are no desk. (7) The zone processor now keeps any occupancy reading of a zone that is neither the queue's nor a linked desk zone from the queue engine (counted), where a late one used to count as a late event of the queue's bins; no output of the golden replay changes. (8) The demo profile v12 is unchanged (no staff or service zones): the emulator's desk zone sensors S-18 to S-20 need a profile with zones named `<desk> staff` and `<desk> service` for AR-08 to AR-22, and are not registered in the demo; adding them to the demo (a v13 seed) would also give the arrival wave its service zones (Open decisions, "Zone lanes for the arrival wave"). Seed 9303's scripted events are unchanged, the queue zones' reference traffic is identical, and the golden replay is unchanged (3cce63edfb17660f53a3fb7eead6c8ae92b20c491135b406a7df8cb032199a6f over 3,202 outputs).
- Shadow nowcast without AMAN inputs (ARV-117), proposed 2026-10-07 by the implementation (the engineer); points (1) and (2) decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-07 (the next two entries); (3) and (4) as built, with the read-path checks widened after the first and second security reviews (wildcard and whole-row reads of `queue_minute` in code, through the table name or any alias declared for it, the `TABLE` command and `COPY` with a schema or quotes; and an exact allowlist of every view, rule, materialized view, continuous aggregate or SQL function that depends on `queue_minute` or its chunks at any column, checked through `pg_depend` after the migrations). (1) Cycle time of the sensor-only desk term: none. AMAN's interval statistics and the transactions counted in `desk_minute` both come from AMAN, and the staff and service zone readings (one count per change, at most 60 a minute, read at the minute's start by the emulator) give no reliable service starts, so the shadow nowcast takes the F8 fallback for an unknown cycle time: the exit term alone, flagged `Degraded`, and no service (`NothingOpen`) when the zones show every desk closed. The sensor-only n_open therefore decides only "nothing open" against "open", and the comparison measures, in effect, AMAN's desk term against the exit rate. Alternative for the owner: a sensor cycle time c = (sensor-derived open desk minutes over the exit window) / (queue exits over the same window), which makes mu_desk the exit rate scaled by the desks open now against the window's mean; it would react at once to a desk opening or closing, but for the "differ only through AMAN inputs" property it must then also become the published nowcast's fallback at sites without AMAN (a change to what screens show). (2) A desk open through AMAN only (open for at least half of the minute, less than half of it sensor-derived) is invisible to the shadow and treated as an Unknown desk: it flags the shadow, and with no desk open from the zones there is no sensor-only term (the exit term alone), never "nothing open" while AMAN shows desks open. Limitation: while AMAN's session is live the desk engine takes AMAN's rank, so `desk_minute` records no sensor-derived time for an AMAN desk even when its zones show staff; at a site where every desk has an AMAN code (AUH) the shadow is therefore the exit term alone whenever AMAN is live. A faithful sensor-only desk state at such sites needs a second, sensor-only desk engine per site in the desk feed (its own minutes and snapshot), a follow-up story if the owner wants the shadow to use the zones of AMAN desks. (3) The shadow is stored as three columns of `queue_minute` (script 0041: minutes, no-service reason, F11 flag), written by the stream in the same row and checkpoint as the published nowcast, computed always (no setting, on from the first day of burn-in), never serialised (not in the live snapshot or the replay ledger) and read by nothing until the validation comparison (ARV-104f); the golden replay is unchanged (3cce63edfb17660f53a3fb7eead6c8ae92b20c491135b406a7df8cb032199a6f over 3,202 outputs) because a replay has no desk term and its shadow equals the published nowcast in every minute. (4) Retention as `queue_minute` (no policy); the columns are lane aggregates without desk codes or identities. Column-level revocation of SELECT for the runtime role was not used (the role holds table-level SELECT for reports and alert inputs); the absence of a read path is enforced by architecture tests instead.
- Shadow nowcast cycle time without AMAN (ARV-117), decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-07: keep the F8 exit-term fallback for the sensor-only desk term (the exit term alone, flagged Degraded; NothingOpen when the zones show every desk closed). The sensor cycle time alternative (sensor open desk minutes over queue exits in the window) is not built; revisit with pilot data.
- AMAN-only desks in the shadow nowcast (ARV-117), decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-07: accepted for ARV-117 as built (a desk open through AMAN only is Unknown to the shadow), so that the shadow runs from the first day of burn-in. Follow-up ARV-117a (backlog/prd-phase1.json, priority 7): a second desk engine per site fed only by staff and service zones (never AMAN), so that sensor-only open seconds are recorded even while AMAN is live and the shadow's desk term reads them; at all-AMAN sites (AUH) the pilot proof then compares AMAN's desk term against the sensor desk term, not the exit rate. ARV-117a must land before burn-in at a site where every desk has an AMAN code; ARV-137 (dev cluster release, and through it the go-live gate ARV-138) depends on it.
- Shadow table and sensor-only desk engine (ARV-117a), built 2026-10-07 on the owner's decision of the same day (the shadow in its own table the runtime login cannot read, same transaction instead of same row), with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) Privileges, verified on PostgreSQL 17 with TimescaleDB 2.30: INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE needs SELECT on the conflict target's columns, and, contrary to the expectation recorded in the story, a reference to `EXCLUDED.<column>` needs SELECT on that column of the target table; so the runtime role gets INSERT, UPDATE of the value columns and SELECT of the two key columns (`zone_key`, `minute_utc`) of `queue_minute_shadow`, and the stream's upsert takes the new values from its staging table by key (`SET (...) = (SELECT ... FROM stage WHERE key = queue_minute_shadow.key)`), never reading a value column. The key columns and the row count are therefore readable by the runtime role (which minutes have a shadow, not what it is). (2) The read role is created now in script 0043, following 0001's pattern: `ariva_validation_reader`, NOLOGIN, SELECT on `queue_minute_shadow` only; ARV-104f grants it to the comparison's own login (since moved: ARV-104g grants it, lead decision 2026-10-08, the ARV-104f entry below) and adds whatever else that login reads (the published nowcast, realised waits and desk minutes are in tables the runtime role already reads, so ARV-104f decides whether its login also gets `ariva_runtime` or narrower grants). (3) A minute whose later checkpoint carries no shadow keeps the shadow row written earlier (the runtime role cannot delete); `Nowcast.Compute` returns a number or a reason on every path, so this does not arise in practice. (4) The sensor-only open seconds are a parallel table, `desk_sensor_minute` (script 0044: seconds per state and the F11 flag, no transactions), not columns of `desk_minute`, so that the published engine's rows and writes are unchanged and the table has its own exposure checks; it stays readable by the runtime role because the stream's desk term reads it. (5) Bounds (Proposed): at most 2,000 desks per site in the sensor-only engine (beyond, left out in key order and counted), the published engine's caps on buffered readings (1,000 per desk, 100,000 per engine), at most 120,000 minutes written per read. (6) An absent (a state saved before ARV-117a), invalid, AMAN-tainted or oversized sensor-only snapshot is rebuilt by replaying the stored zone readings of the last 15 minutes, so the first read after deployment rewrites up to 15 minutes of sensor-only minutes. (7) Effect on the ground-truth proof: with the owner's decision of 2026-10-07 to keep no sensor cycle time, the sensor-only desk term decides only "nothing open" against "open"; ARV-117a makes that decision come from the zones at all-AMAN sites while AMAN is live (before, the shadow had no desk term there), but the shadow's number while a desk is open is still the exit term alone, flagged. A comparison of AMAN's desk term against a sensor desk term with its own throughput needs the sensor cycle time alternative (sensor open desk minutes over queue exits in the window), which `desk_sensor_minute` now makes computable at all-AMAN sites; the owner may want to revisit that decision before burn-in at AUH. Golden replay unchanged (3cce63edfb17660f53a3fb7eead6c8ae92b20c491135b406a7df8cb032199a6f over 3,202 outputs).
- Residual risk on the shadow table (ARV-117a), recorded 2026-10-07 after the first security review of ARV-117a, accepted as low (pending the owner's acknowledgement): a runtime session can learn one bit per minute of `queue_minute_shadow`, whether the shadow holds a number or a no-service reason, by a deliberate probe: an UPDATE by key that sets only `no_service` (or only `nowcast_minutes`) trips `ck_queue_minute_shadow_one` (23514) exactly when the other one is held, and the prober rolls back. Why accepted: it needs a deliberate probe by a compromised host; the number, the reason and the flag are not revealed (PostgreSQL's row detail lists only the columns the session may read and those the statement set; verified with `Include Error Detail`); the shadow can be recomputed from inputs the runtime role reads anyway (queue length, exits, `desk_sensor_minute`); and the control's purpose is that the shadow never reaches what Ariva publishes (screens, displays, alerts, reports, AMAN), which the privileges and the exposure tests still ensure. Pinned by `QueueStreamTests.RuntimeLogin_Should_LearnOnlyWhetherANumberIsHeld_When_ItProbesTheShadowCheck`. Watch item: never enable TimescaleDB chunk skipping on a value column of this table (its per-chunk ranges are readable in the catalog). Removing the residual would need the check moved out of the table (for example a trigger owned by the migration role), which the owner may choose later.
- Sensor-derived cycle time for the shadow nowcast (ARV-117b), decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-07: added to backlog/prd-phase1.json right after ARV-117a (later priorities shifted by one), depending on ARV-117a, and made a dependency of ARV-137. The shadow's number is still the exit term whenever a desk is open because it has no cycle time; ARV-117b derives c for the shadow only from `desk_sensor_minute` (sensor open desk minutes over queue exits in the window, F8-style bounds and plausibility, Degraded when outside the bounds or with too few exits). The published nowcast and every screen stay unchanged, so at all-AMAN sites the pilot proof compares AMAN's desk term with the sensor desk term. This revisits, for the shadow only, the earlier decision of the same day to keep the exit-term fallback.
- Owner decisions on ARV-117c (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-07): ARV-117c is accepted with its tracking criterion measured while a lane runs at capacity (median ratio 0.95 to 1.03 per lane); the gap over all minutes is not the emulator's but the published desk term's, so a new story, ARV-117d, fixes F10 before the AUH seed: c per person (documents, not transactions) and idle gaps left out, with AMAN asked to confirm the semantics of MeanCycleSeconds and DocumentsProcessed.
- Owner decisions on the ARV-117b points (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-07): the Serving-based estimator is accepted as Proposed with the values of point (2), and the walk-up-gap bias is to be re-measured on pilot data (ARV-104); a site without AMAN keeps its published nowcast unchanged until the pilot shows the sensor cycle time is accurate; the AMAN emulator's constant 1.00-minute lane cycle time is an artifact that makes the published nowcast look worse in every demo and comparison, so it gets its own story (ARV-117c) before the AUH seed.
- Sensor cycle time of the shadow nowcast (ARV-117b), built 2026-10-07 on the owner's decision of the same day, with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) The estimator. The method offered with ARV-117 (sensor open desk minutes over queue exits in the window) collapses into the exit term: open desk time over exits is the throughput identity, so with the exit term's window n_open / c is the exit rate times n_open over the window's mean open desks; the blend then equals the exit term whenever staffing is steady, and the idle time of a short queue lowers the desk term (the realised throughput, not the capacity). On the reference evening it is worse than the exit term alone (mean absolute error 1.01 min against 0.81) and gets worse with a longer window (0.80 at 5 minutes, 0.93 at 10, 1.19 at 15, 1.67 at 30, against 0.73 for the exit term alone, in the engineer's exploratory run with a simpler alignment of the desk minutes). Built instead: the lane's sensor-only Serving desk minutes (staff present and the service zone occupied, F10 row 6, from `desk_sensor_minute`) over the queue's exits in the same window, the time a desk takes per passenger while it serves, so n_open / c is the capacity of the desks open now. It adds the desks open now and the busy share of their time; under saturation it is still the exit rate per busy desk, because the sensors see no service starts, so it measures no service speed of its own. Exits are the lane's (the exit line is shared; they cannot be attributed to a desk). Its known bias: service-zone time leaves out the walk-up gap, so where the gap is long against the service time the shadow is optimistic (D4: service time overstates throughput); the simulator does not model the gap, so this needs pilot data. A per-desk service time from staff presence and exits attributed to desks would need a desk exit line or the service zone's occupancy changes as service starts, which the zones as configured do not give. (2) Proposed values: window W = 10 whole minutes ending at the desk term's minute (from 2 to 10: the desk term reads 15 minutes and is fresh for 5; a longer window needs its own read, and in the same exploratory run 5, 10, 15 and 30 minutes gave 0.66, 0.66, 0.64 and 0.61 min mean absolute error: little to choose); at least 10 exits in the window; c from 0.2 to 10 minutes a passenger (12 s to 10 min; F8's 3-second floor for the published c stays); at most 5 percent of the window's desk time Unknown, missing or refused; at most 4,000 sensor-only desk minutes per lane and window (CWE-120). (3) Fallback and flag: without c the shadow falls back as F8 does, the exit term alone, which `Nowcast.Compute` always flags Degraded, or `NothingOpen` when the zones show every desk closed, which is not flagged for a cycle time it does not need (the story's "flagged Degraded" read as applying to the exit-term fallback; flagging every NothingOpen minute, which always has too few exits, would make the flag mean nothing there). A desk minute of the lane without a sensor-only minute (a desk without zones beside desks with zones) gives no c, because its busy time is missing while its exits count. (4) Stored: `queue_minute_shadow.sensor_cycle_minutes` (script 0045, null when the shadow fell back; check 0.05 to 60), written by the stream, UPDATE without SELECT for the runtime role; its check names that column alone, so it adds no probe bit to the accepted residual of 0043. (5) Settings: `ZoneProcessorSettings.SensorCycle`, code defaults like the other nowcast settings, left out of the replay's settings hash (a replay has no desk term, so they change no replay output); the golden replay is unchanged (3cce63edfb17660f53a3fb7eead6c8ae92b20c491135b406a7df8cb032199a6f over 3,202 outputs). (6) Consequence at a site without AMAN: the shadow now differs from the published nowcast in the minutes where it has a sensor cycle time, which the published nowcast never takes; whether such a site should publish a sensor cycle time (a change to what screens show) is a separate decision, not part of this story. (7) What the pilot proof would show, measured on Ariva's own simulator (reference evening, seed 9303, 17:20 to 20:25, Visitors desks all with AMAN codes and AMAN live; not field data): against the realised mean wait of the people who joined in the minute after the nowcast's minute, the published nowcast (AMAN's n_open and lane cycle time) has mean absolute error 1.17 min and bias -1.07 min; the shadow with the sensor cycle time 0.74 min and +0.53 min (sensor c in 151 of 186 minutes, median 1.59 min against the scenario's 1.58 min service time); the shadow without it (ARV-117a) 0.81 and +0.61. The published nowcast's error there is the emulator's: its one-minute AMAN interval statistics give a mean cycle of 60 s per transaction with at most one transaction a desk minute, so AMAN's lane cycle time reads 1.00 min all evening; field AMAN statistics will differ, and the simulator's waits are short (mean 6.4 min), so these numbers show how the comparison works, not what the pilot will find.
- Availability ledger per operating minute (ARV-118), proposed 2026-10-07 by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner's review and the client's answer to TC-83: (1) an available minute is one where every queue zone of the site's zone profile in force at the minute has a live snapshot in Redis younger than 150 seconds (the screens' staleness) whose minute has reached the minute and trails real time by at most 180 seconds, and a `queue_minute` row; availability is available operating minutes over operating minutes, per local day, week (Monday to Sunday) and range (at most 92 days), none without operating minutes. (2) Operating hours come from a site operating calendar: weekly hours in the site's local time (its airport's time zone), dated exceptions and maintenance windows; a site without a calendar version in force is open around the clock (every minute counts). (3) Entries count only when recorded before they take effect (server time): a week or an exception before its local day starts, a maintenance window before it starts. A window recorded at or after its start does not count at all, rather than from its recorded time, so an outage can never be excused by declaring maintenance once it has begun; entries change or are removed only before they take effect. (4) Catch-up after Cronz downtime uses the database alone: a missing minute is unavailable, every other minute is Unobserved and counts as not available (never available from today's Redis state). (5) The calendar is read with the site's view permission and changed with its edit permission (administrators); the availability read uses `DataQuality.View`. No screen in this story (API only; a calendar and availability screen is deferred). If the client defines availability differently, the rule changes (a new rule version), not the ledger rows already written.
- Availability ledger (ARV-118), decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-07: (1) the available-minute definition as built (every queue zone of the profile in force has a snapshot published within 150 s, its minute reached and trailing real time by at most 180 s, and a `queue_minute` row) is accepted as the Proposed KPI-annex definition and goes to the client as TC-83; (2) maintenance windows recorded at or after their start never count; (3) a site with no calendar is open around the clock. All three kept. With the first security review (2026-10-07) the read also reports a stricter ratio with maintenance minutes counted as unavailable (`availabilityMaintenanceAsUnavailable`), and TC-83 asks the client to cap declared maintenance minutes per month or use that ratio; a snapshot whose minute ends more than 60 s ahead of the job's clock counts as stale; a site whose airport time zone is unknown on the Cronz host is skipped (never decided in UTC). Accepted residual risk: the runtime role keeps UPDATE on the calendar tables (soft delete needs it), so a compromised runtime could alter an entry's columns; the ledger has already fixed every minute written, so this reaches only minutes not yet written. Amended by the owner on 2026-10-10 (F3 of the ARV-104j entry below; carried out in ARV-150): a site's ledger starts at the first whole minute of the first publication of its zone profile, so the minutes before the site existed are never in the denominator; the minute before the first push still counts, and a later republication does not restart the ledger (rule version 2).
- Realistic AMAN interval statistics in the simulator (ARV-117c), built 2026-10-07 on the owner's decision of the same day, with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer); the owner decided them on 2026-10-07 (the entry above: accepted at capacity, the F10 fix is ARV-117d): (1) Semantics the emulator follows (simulator README, `AmanDeskProcess`): the scenario's served people are worked through by each desk as discrete transactions (families of 1, 2 or 3, 1.25 on average; a service time variation of mean 1); a transaction is counted whole in the interval in which it completes, never spread over the minutes it spans, because `TransactionsProcessed` counts completions and Ariva's lane cycle time (F10) weights each interval's mean cycle by that count, so each completed transaction then counts once and the lane cycle time is the mean cycle per transaction; its cycle is from the previous transaction's start at the desk to its own start in open time (the contract's "start to next start while open": paused minutes left out, idle minutes in); the first transaction of a session, or after a gap over 3,600 s, has its own service time as its cycle (no zero beside a count, which the immigration view would average in). To confirm with AMAN when the contract is reconciled. (2) What it shows about Ariva, not about the emulator (reference evening, seed 9303, Visitors desks all with AMAN codes and AMAN live; Ariva's own simulator, not field data; `SensorCycleScenarioTests`): AMAN's lane cycle time as the published term reads it has median 2.11 min (1.02 to 23.13) against the scenario's 1.58 min per person, and the published nowcast's mean absolute error is 1.85 min with bias +1.85 (was 1.17 and -1.07 with the emulator's constant 1.00 min); the shadow is unchanged (0.74, +0.53). Two causes, both in how Ariva reads contract V1: (a) `LaneCycle` gives a cycle per transaction (an approach, 1.25 people on the simulator) where F8's c is per person, so the desk term's throughput is 20 percent low wherever families travel together; (b) a start-to-start cycle while open includes the idle time of a desk waiting for passengers, so after each lull the lane cycle time is several times the service time until the window refills (over all minutes 1.20 times 1.25 people's service time for A-VIS, up to 10 times for a mostly idle crew desk; within 10 percent in every lane while it runs at capacity, `AmanIntervalScenarioTests`). Measured alternatives, for the decision only (Ariva unchanged): the lane cycle time per person (cycle seconds over documents) gives median 1.70 min and published error 1.22 min, +1.11; per person in the desks' working time (idle left out, which AMAN does not publish under V1) 1.49 min and 0.63 min, +0.28, better than the shadow. Owner decisions needed: (i) whether F10's lane cycle time should be per person (sum of cycle seconds times transactions over the sum of documents), a change to the published nowcast and its own story; (ii) whether contract V1's `MeanCycleSeconds` should exclude idle time (only starts with a passenger waiting, or a cap on the gap), to be agreed with AMAN, or Ariva should bound the gap it takes; (iii) the acceptance criterion's "median within 10 percent" is met while a lane runs at capacity and not over all minutes, for the reasons in (2): accept it as measured at capacity, or wait for (i) and (ii). (3) The emulator change is the simulator's only: Ariva.Core, the contracts, the HTTP surface and the golden replay (3cce63edfb17660f53a3fb7eead6c8ae92b20c491135b406a7df8cb032199a6f over 3,202 outputs; a replay has no desk term) are unchanged.
- Owner decisions on ARV-117d (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-07): the mean service time per document method (point (1) of the entry below) is accepted as Proposed; the walk-up gap it leaves out (point (2)) is to be re-measured on pilot data (ARV-104), with the cycle capped at the P90 service and floored at the mean service (`LaneCycleMethod.CycleCappedAtP90`) kept in code as the switch (`DeskTermSource.CycleMethod`) if the gap is long. The fallback and flag rules (point (3)) were not part of the decision and stay proposed.
- Published lane cycle time per person in working time (ARV-117d), built 2026-10-07 on the owner's decision of the same day (the ARV-117c entry above), with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer); the owner decided points (1) and (2) on 2026-10-07 (the entry above): (1) The method that leaves idle time out. Built: each AMAN interval's mean service time (`MeanServiceSeconds`, transaction start to end) times its transactions, summed over the lane's intervals in the exit window and divided by their documents: c = sum(MeanService x Transactions) / sum(Documents) / 60 minutes a person (`LaneCycle.PerPerson`, `LaneCycleMethod.MeanService`). Service time holds no idle time by definition, and it is the one V1 field whose meaning does not depend on what a desk does between passengers. The alternative offered, each interval's mean cycle capped at its P90 service time, was measured and rejected: with one or two transactions in a one-minute interval (the usual case at 1 to 2 minutes of service a person) the interval's P90 is its own transaction's service time while its cycle runs from the previous transaction's start, so the cap takes the smaller of two consecutive transactions' times and reads c low whether or not there was a lull (reference evening: c median 1.14 min against the scenario's 1.58, published mean absolute error 0.82 min, bias -0.53). Floored at the mean service (max(MeanService, min(MeanCycle, max(P90, MeanService))), kept in code as `LaneCycleMethod.CycleCappedAtP90`, not used) it measured 0.65 min, +0.35, no better than the mean service and harder to explain. (2) What the built method gives up: the walk-up gap (D4: cycle time, service plus the walk-up gap, sets throughput; pure service time overstates it). The simulator has no walk-up gap, so on the reference evening the method equals the desks' working time (1.51 min against 1.49; 0.63 min and +0.31 against 0.63 and +0.28); in the field the published nowcast may read low by the gap's share of the cycle, as the shadow's sensor cycle time (ARV-117b) does, so the pilot's comparison measures both with the same bias. If the pilot shows a long gap, the floored cap keeps a gap shorter than the P90 service time (one constant, `DeskTermSource.CycleMethod`), or AMAN could publish a cycle without waiting time (pilot-to-confirm TC-84). (3) Fallback and flag: when AMAN's intervals for the lane were read, c comes from them or there is none (never the desk minutes' open time per transaction, which is per transaction and carries idle time); with no documents in the window (desks idle) there is no c and the nowcast falls back as F8 does, the exit term alone, flagged by `Nowcast.Compute`, without flagging the term itself (idle desks are not a fault); an interval outside contract V1's bounds (counts 0 to 10,000, transactions at most documents and both 0 or neither, every time finite and 0 to 3,600 s) or with transactions and a service time of 0 is left out, counted, logged with the site and the count only, and flags the published term (the screens show a band). Only a lane without any interval statistics keeps the desk minutes' fallback. (4) Measured on the reference evening (seed 9303, 17:20 to 20:25, Visitors desks all with AMAN codes and AMAN live; Ariva's own simulator, not field data; `SensorCycleScenarioTests`): published mean absolute error 0.63 min, bias +0.31 (was 1.85, +1.85); shadow with the sensor cycle time 0.74, +0.53 and the exit term alone 0.81, +0.61, both unchanged. Per lane over the reference day (`AmanIntervalScenarioTests`) the published lane cycle time over the scenario's service time has median 0.92 to 0.99 over all minutes and 0.96 to 1.03 at capacity. (5) Golden replay unchanged (3cce63edfb17660f53a3fb7eead6c8ae92b20c491135b406a7df8cb032199a6f over 3,202 outputs): a replay has no desk term. (6) Operators see lower published waits at AMAN sites (wiki 15, Nowcast); sites without AMAN are unchanged.
- Owner decisions on the AUH Terminal A illustrative seed (ARV-139a; Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-07): (1) the assumed lane split of the 38 counters is kept (CRW 2, DIP 1, CIT 4, RES 6, GCC 6, VIS 17, TRF 2) and all 34 smart gates serve lane EG only; both stay labelled assumptions in the seed and in docs/demo/auh-terminal-a.md (the real split is not public), accepted by the owner for the demo. (2) The "Illustrative, not surveyed" note on the site's passenger displays stays in English and Arabic only (no further languages for the demo). The other points stay as proposed in the seed and its page: every cited source's URL is to confirm before the page is shown outside Dalil; the operational demo users stay on DMO (only the demo administrator reaches AUH-TA); the flag can never be cleared (a real, surveyed site is a new site created through the admin API).

- AUH Terminal A reference scenario (ARV-139b), built 2026-10-07, with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) The engine's parameterisation stayed in this story (no ARV-139c split): `ScenarioSite` (Ariva.Simulation.Api/Scenarios/Engine/ScenarioSite.cs) holds what is site specific (queues and servers, staffing areas, arrival lanes and the split of a flight's passengers, minimum staffing, sensors and their roles, bands, outages, roster lines, servers out of service, seeded rules, snake capacities, the flights a seed draws), the reference site takes every value from the existing model, and seed 9303's outputs are bit for bit unchanged (the sim.js parity fingerprints, the golden replay 3cce63edfb17660f53a3fb7eead6c8ae92b20c491135b406a7df8cb032199a6f over 3,202 outputs; the overflow case's lock changed only through the engine fix of point 8). (2) One simulator plays both sites on one demo clock (no second deployment): each scenario endpoint takes `site` (DMO when not given), sensor emulator devices name their site, seeds are `Simulation:Sites:{site}:Seed` (DMO also `Simulation:Seed`), and `Simulation:SiteCode` is gone (a summary names its day's site). (3) The AUH-TA evening's assumptions (flight waves, fictional carriers HC, RG and LV and their mixes, smart-gate shares, service times, roster and shift handover, the smart gate fault, the Q-RES-04 outage), all labelled in the source and the simulator's Scenarios/README.md; among them snake capacities of 300 people for A-VIS and 250 for A-EG in front of their bands, far below the seed's area capacities (947 and 994), which no plausible evening fills; the seed and Ariva's zone capacities are unchanged. (4) The scripted events with seed 9304: A-VIS above 15 minutes at 18:12 in the scenario and in Ariva's own nowcast from the exits (an earlier draft said 18:09 for Ariva: that value came from the re-anchoring fault of point 8 and depended on the devices' ids), Q-RES-04 offline 18:25 to 18:35 (heard again at 18:36), A-EG above its snake 19:12 to 19:24 (band A-EG-OV occupied 19:13 to 19:26 in Ariva); the scenario's fingerprints are locked in `Platform/Backplane/Ariva.UnitTests/Simulation/auh-terminal-a-golden.json`, to be approved like the reference values. (5) How the demo registers and commissions the seeded sensors: `scripts/demo-local.mjs start` (and `run-ariva.ps1 -Demo`) issues each the credential it plays with (`POST devices/{id}/credential`, a critical action needing the recent second factor) and records its calibration (`POST devices/{id}/calibrations`), and gives AUH-TA the scenario's three alert rules through the alert rules API once while the site has none; the seed itself still creates no credential, calibration or rule, and device authentication is never relaxed. (6) The AMAN emulator does not play AUH-TA: the site has no AMAN desk codes, its counters' states come from their staff and service zones (the owner's ARV-116 decision of 2026-10-07), an AMAN desk session would take precedence over those sensors, and contract V1's lanes have no GCC, DIP or TRF (ARV-130). (7) The AUH-TA AODB has an integration client of its own, so a refused call on one site never drops the other site's token; like DMO's, it is not configured by the local demo. (8) Queue engine fix found by the AUH-TA replay (2026-10-07): F5 re-anchoring was checked as each occupancy reading was applied, so when a queue zone and its overflow band were read at the same instant by two devices, a band's 0 applied before the zone's new reading was added to the zone's previous reading; at the start of a wave (zone 0 then 10, band 0) the engine took the queue for empty, dropped the people it held and counted their exits as duplicates, which lowered the exit throughput and raised the nowcast. Which reading came first depended on the batch ids, which derive from the device ids, so the AUH-TA replay's first breach moved between 18:09 and 18:12 from run to run (eight runs gave eight output hashes). The check now runs once every event of the instant has been applied (`QueueStateEngine.Reanchor`), and the outputs no longer depend on the order of ties between devices (12 runs with different ids, one output hash; `AuhTerminalAReplayTests.Replay_Should_GiveTheSameOutputs_When_TheDevicesHaveOtherIds`). The golden replay of seed 9303 is unchanged (its devices report no band). The overflow case's locked replay changed, approved by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-07 (ARV-139b re-approval): d971a3170539758f5248bfcc01e181e3bfd9ab1aa51dfba638d8908be9948b9a over 1,692 outputs (approved 2026-10-06) became 8df69d9f46b3eb15d871b5e0e37c82f0c52ac62d2f1db6e022b0df0d7db569ba over 1,693 outputs: at 17:59 and 19:28 its band's reading was ordered before the zone's, so the exits at the start of each wave now count (minutes, bins, bin health and 11 live minutes at 17:59 to 18:03 and 19:28 to 19:33 change; queue lengths, every overflow minute and change, the line minutes and the outage are byte-identical). By type: 567 minute (566 before), 224 bin, 224 health, 211 live, 253 line, 1 outage, 209 overflow, 4 overflow-change. The one added output is a further provisional revision of minute 19:27 (18 entries, 8 exits, 18 waits): the 10 people who entered 19:27:27 to 19:27:58, dropped at 19:28:00 before, now exit 19:28:01 to 19:29:06 across two engine steps, so the minute's waits go from 8 to 13 and then 18 with a provisional row each, where they stayed at 8.
