Alternative: learn each device's interval from its receive times, more exact for devices that push faster than the setting but misled by retries. (3) CWE-501 bound: an event counts on its zone's clock at most up to its own batch's receive time (itself capped at the host's clock plus `Stream:MaxAheadSeconds`), even when the host has ticked the idle zone on to a later time since, so a device whose clock runs ahead cannot move the watermark past the receive time less the lateness, and the live path gives what a replay of the same records gives; its events are still held until the watermark reaches them, as before. Alternative: refuse events more than a short bound ahead. (4) The events of one instant are applied in a fixed order of their content (interval readings first, then crossings, readings and track samples; then line or zone, direction, track and counts) rather than in arrival order, so the batches and topics that carried them never change a result. It also mends a defect of the receive clock: a T1 device's interval reading was spread over nothing (all its people at the interval's end) when the device's occupancy reading of the same instant was applied first, which the stream's topic order did. (5) Device liveness in the stream: the devices are observed once for each new receive time, before any record of it, so a silence beyond the limit is an outage even when the silent device's own record is the first of that receive time (before, the outage then went unrecorded); open outages are marked at each new receive time through the minutes known to be out, one mark per device and minute (before, every 5 minutes through the receive time); and a bin is final only once the receive clock less the push interval has passed its end, so an outage never finds a bin already final except at its start. With a lateness of at least the push interval, which the stream requires, the watermark itself never passes a bin's end before that, so this rule delays no bin; it holds the engine to the rule under settings below it too. (6) A zone's clock starts with its first event: a zone that has had only health reports since its state started publishes no minutes or bins until its first event (before, empty ones from the receive clock), and the zone's first watermark may publish an empty bin and a live minute just before its first data (the golden replay's 16:45 bin and 16:58 minute). (7) The watermark moves with each batch of a push rather than once per receive time, so more provisional revisions are written: the golden replay writes 652 bin and 652 health rows (486 before) and 1,756 minute and live rows (1,596), about 15 percent more provisional upserts; final results are unchanged by it. (8) The replays regenerated, confirmed by the owner 2026-10-10: seed 9303's golden replay 2b047dfce6d587d26ac5f99e4dd9c0817627b3b63d21792caf4f0a1cc6abd05e over 3,694 outputs (was 3cce63edfb17660f53a3fb7eead6c8ae92b20c491135b406a7df8cb032199a6f over 3,202), S-17's outage 18:20 to 18:30 with only the 18:15 bin Degraded; its overflow case (seed 9303, the 60-person snake) 0a38ac8833154f3810780e1360f77996b417fc8c3b04ce422e8bed7dd1ff12a8 over 1,966 (was 8df69d9f46b3eb15d871b5e0e37c82f0c52ac62d2f1db6e022b0df0d7db569ba over 1,693), confirmed by the owner 2026-10-10 as covered by the same approval. The AUH-TA replay (seed 9304, ARV-139b) has no locked value, but its outputs change the same way and Q-RES-04's outage now ends at 18:35 (was 18:36), confirmed by the owner 2026-10-10. (9) Outside the owner's decision and still open, as before ARV-148: the recomputations that late events ask for are not carried out in the live path; with event time an event is late only when it arrives more than the lateness behind its zone's latest event.

- Desk state from AMAN's transaction rhythm (ARV-149), built 2026-10-10 on the owner's decision of the same day (F2 of the ARV-104j entry above), with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner (formulas F10, "AMAN-only desks: the transaction rhythm"; wiki 15, Desk utilisation): (1) Nobody waiting is read from the queue sensors: Q is the people in the queue zones the desk serves (the desk term's match, F8: the queue zones of its level whose lane is one of its lanes), from each zone's latest live minute of the last 5 minutes; logged in with nobody waiting a desk is `Idle` however long it is silent, with people waiting `Paused` after a silence of P, with the queue not measured `Paused` after P as before. So a desk without zones now reads aggregate queue lengths, never a sensor's view of the desk itself. Alternatives: judge from the lane's other desks (the lane transacting while this desk is silent is a pause; no sensors, but wrong in light demand, where some logged-in desks rightly idle), or `Paused` after P whatever the queue (an empty hall reads `Paused` again, the rehearsal's largest disagreement). (2) Between the rhythm window and P, with people waiting, the desk is `Serving` (a long transaction, a family, calling the next traveller); with nobody waiting it is `Idle`. The decision's words leave this band open; alternative: `Idle` there, which disagrees with an observer whenever a transaction runs longer than s plus a minute. (3) The rhythm window is s + I (I = AMAN's one-minute interval: a transaction is reported at the end of the interval it ends in), and P = max(T1, min(k x s, 30 minutes)) with k = 4 (`Border:DeskFeed:RhythmPauseMultiple`, 2 to 20): never sooner than before (T1), never longer than the longest transaction. Alternatives: k = 3 or 6; s per transaction rather than per person. (4) s is the lane cycle time per person of ARV-117d over AMAN's intervals of the last 15 minutes (`Border:DeskFeed:RhythmWindowMinutes`, 5 to 60) and the 2 they may arrive late by, a steadier window than the nowcast's 5. (5) With people waiting the silence counts from the last transaction, the login or the start of the waiting, whichever is latest (the waiting starts again after a context with nobody waiting, an unknown queue or a gap of T_stale), so a desk idle through a quiet spell has P to start. (6) A degraded queue length (a sensor outage) is used and flags the desk state `Degraded` when it decided it; alternative: treat it as not measured (desks would pause after P during an outage). (7) The lane context reaches the engine once per whole minute and applies from the engine's watermark at the read (the read's time less the feed's 90-second lateness), the earliest moment it can apply without revising a closed minute; the queue it carries, each zone's latest live minute, is a minute or two behind that, so the state follows the start and end of a queue that much later (the rhythm covers a wave's start from its first reported transaction). The first design applied it from the minute of the read, a minute and a half later. (8) The state is AMAN's (logged in), never sensor-derived: the shadow nowcast's code is unchanged and still counts a desk open through AMAN alone as Unknown (ARV-117), but its outputs change, because such desks are now `Idle` or `Serving` far more often: in minutes it used to read them as known closed (`Paused`), the shadow now flags its desk term or has none; the published nowcast counts them open while they are `Idle` or `Serving`, and desk minutes stay border data for border roles only. (9) Measured on the reference evening (`DeskRhythmScenarioTests`, formulas F10): the Visitors desks without zones agree with the simulator's observers in 0.907 of desk minutes from 17:20 to 20:25 and 0.901 from 18:00 to 19:30 (0.272 and 0.247 without the lane context; open or not open 0.996), meeting 0.95 with a queue (0.989) and short of it in the empty hall (0.925) and in light demand (0.640). Light demand is a simulator finding for the owner: the scenario's truth makes its lowest-numbered open desks serve and the others idle while its AMAN emulator spreads the people served over every open desk, so the ARV-104j rehearsal will judge desks without zones against a truth their AMAN feed cannot show; options: make the AMAN emulator serve only with the desks its truth calls serving (a simulator story; it moves the measured AMAN figures of F8 and F10), have the truth spread service over the open desks as the emulator does, or judge desks without zones at the throughput level (open or not, 0.996), one of F2's own options.

- Availability ledger from profile publication (ARV-150), built 2026-10-10 on the owner's decision of the same day (F3 of the ARV-104j entry above), with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner (formulas F18, "Availability during the pilot", start of a site's ledger): (1) The first minute is the first whole minute at or after the first publication (a profile published at 06:37:30 starts the ledger at 06:38, one published at 06:37:00 at 06:37): the profile in force at a minute is the one published at or before its start, so the minute of the publication was not wholly the site's, and counting it would put one NoPublishedZones minute back. Alternative: count the minute of the publication and take the profile as in force for all of it. (2) A site the job sees while its first minute is still live or ahead starts there and stores that start in a pass of its own (before its time zone is resolved, committed with nothing else, the minutes decided from the next run, so a first minute at the very edge of the live window is caught up as Unobserved), so neither a missed run, an unknown time zone nor a pass that fails later can move it (security review of 2026-10-10, finding 1); a stored cursor is never moved. A site published before the job ever ran for it (its first minute already past the live window: published before ARV-118, or while Cronz was down) starts at the latest decidable minute, as ARV-118 decided, so no history before the ledger existed is filled as unobserved (that downtime is left out; downtime after the start counts as Unobserved or MissingMinute). Alternative: always catch up from the first publication, so that the minutes between a publication and the job's first pass count (as Unobserved or MissingMinute) even when Cronz was down at the publication; existing environments would then fill their sites' history once. (3) Rule version 2 from this release, as the runbook asks of a rule change (wiki 10, 4.8k): no minute is decided differently, but a site's first rows now never precede its publication, except as in point (4) (a publication stored far ahead and not yet trusted) and from a cursor kept from version 1. Rows already written keep version 1 and are never rewritten, and the reads count what the ledger recorded, so a site whose ledger started under version 1 keeps its first minutes (at most 3 NoPublishedZones; none in production, which has not started; the rehearsal runs on fresh services). Alternatives: keep version 1, or leave rows before the first publication out of the reads. (4) A first publication stored more than 60 s ahead of the Cronz clock (the snapshot tolerance of ARV-118) is not trusted (CWE-501): the ledger starts as if it were not there, goes on from its cursor and records the minutes before the profile is in force as NoPublishedZones, so a stored time cannot keep a site out of its ledger and no minute is left unwritten once it is trusted; Cronz warns once per site, with the site code only, so the runbook's clock check finds it. (5) No script: the start is derived from `zone_profile.published_on` rather than stored, and the cursor holds it once a pass has seen the site. The 0012 trigger (BEFORE UPDATE OR DELETE) refuses any change to a published or retired version and its deletion, so the first publication can only move earlier (an insert can add an earlier version), which never moves a stored start; an integration test runs these statements as the runtime login (security review of 2026-10-10, finding 2). (6) A site and its measurement start belong in the KPI annex: a new site id for an existing hall starts a new ledger (TC-83).

- Simulator truth spreads service over the open desks (ARV-151), built 2026-10-10 on the owner's decision of the same day (the light-demand finding, point (9) of the ARV-149 entry above), in the simulator only, Ariva's rules unchanged, with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) The rule: the people a lane serves in a minute are shared among its working desks (open and not paused, or hidden from the sensors: the desks the AMAN emulator works) by speed, D x (1 / svc_k) / sum of (1 / svc), so every working desk works the same seconds, D / sum of (1 / svc); a desk is `serving` in the minute when it works at least half of it (30 seconds, `ScenarioDay.ServingSeconds`) or people wait, and `idle` otherwise, so a lane's working desks serve or idle together. Two implementations, sim.js (`day.work`, `day.servers`) and its C# port (`ScenarioDay.WorkSeconds`, `ServerStates`), held to each other by the golden fingerprints and to the AMAN emulator (`AmanDeskProcess`) by `DeskTruthTests`: the same seconds for every desk and minute of every immigration lane on seeds 9303 and 2026 (about 43,000 desk minutes with people served on each), every transaction completing at a desk the truth has working, and over the day each desk's documents within 7 percent of the people the truth gives it (each transaction's service varies around its people's; the test allows 15). Alternatives: serving at any work in the minute (F18 compares a minute's dominant state, hence half the minute), or the AMAN emulator following the old truth (it would move the measured AMAN figures of F8 and F10). (2) The reference scenario's golden fingerprints are regenerated for this reason, with the owner's approval of 2026-10-10: only `serversAt` changes, in all six cases (`scripts/simulation/reference-golden.mjs`, `reference-golden.json`); Ariva's golden and overflow replays and the AUH-TA fingerprints are unchanged. (3) What follows the truth: the observers' desk states of the ARV-104i truth endpoint (a desk hidden from the sensors is seen `Serving` when it serves by the same rule), the service zones S-18 to S-20 report in the desk zone case (its open desk counts unchanged) and with them the shadow nowcast's sensor cycle time measured there (`SensorCycleScenarioTests`, its assertions unchanged). (4) ARV-149's rows re-measured (formulas F10, `DeskRhythmScenarioTests`): 0.914 of desk minutes on the reference evening and 0.909 on the held-out evening (seed 2026, used for no choice), with a queue 0.989 and 0.986, the empty hall 0.925 and 0.909, light demand 0.676 on both; the held-out evening agrees within 0.005 of the tuned one, and the light-demand shortfall from 0.95 is the rule's, not the simulator's (row 5a reads a desk `Serving` for s + I after each transaction however little of the minute it worked). For the owner: judge desks without zones at the throughput level (open or not, 0.99 on both evenings), change rows 5a and 5b to read each interval's service seconds (formulas F10), or accept the Proposed rows below 0.95 for desks without zones.
- Owner decisions on ARV-151's points (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-10): (1) the truth's serving threshold stays at half the minute's work (30 seconds, `ScenarioDay.ServingSeconds`), the rule F18 already judges by (a minute's dominant state), so observers, the truth and Ariva share one definition. (4) For desks without staff or service zones, F2 closes by reading each AMAN interval's service seconds, not by the throughput level and not by accepting the Proposed rows below 0.95: rows 5a and 5b change so that Ariva estimates the seconds a desk worked in each minute from the interval statistics it already receives (`TransactionsProcessed` and `MeanServiceSeconds` of `DeskIntervalStats` V1, spread back over the minutes the transactions span) and reads `Serving` when that is at least half the minute, no contract change. Carried out in ARV-149a, measured on seeds 9303 and 2026 before the ARV-104j rehearsal runs again; if the held-out evening still falls short of 0.95, the shortfall comes back to the owner with the minutes behind it, never a loosened test.

- Desk state from the seconds AMAN's desks worked (ARV-149a), measured 2026-10-10 and stopped before rows 5a and 5b were changed, as the owner's decision above provides when the evenings fall short of 0.95; the shortfall comes back to the owner (the engineer's measurement, pending the owner): (1) What was measured: the rule as decided (each AMAN interval's work, `TransactionsProcessed` x `MeanServiceSeconds`, spread back over the minutes its transactions span, no minute above 60 s, `Serving` at 30 s or more; rows 5b to 5f as they are) for the Visitors desks AR-08 to AR-22 from 17:20 to 20:25 on the reference evening (seed 9303) and the held-out one (seed 2026), against the simulator's truth, from the AMAN emulator's own interval figures and the truth exported per desk and minute. The rows were modelled outside the engine; the same model gives ARV-149's rows 0.913 and 0.912 where `DeskRhythmScenarioTests` measures 0.914 and 0.909. (2) The closed-minute options: (a) desks without zones decide their minute one interval later (one more minute on their live state and on the nowcast's n_open; the spread reaches the interval's own minute and the one before, work for earlier minutes is not counted): 0.927 on 9303 (18:00 to 19:30: 0.918; with a queue 0.987, empty hall 1.000, light demand 0.653) and 0.933 on 2026 (0.945; 0.974, 0.998, 0.728); (b) no extra delay, work for a closed minute counted in the interval's own minute: 0.918 (0.907; light demand 0.617) and 0.926 (0.948; 0.707). Even with every minute decided in hindsight and the scenario's own queue without lag the rule reaches at most 0.940 on 9303 (0.944; light demand 0.693) and 0.960 on 2026, over three spread shapes (filled back from the interval's end, over the transactions' cycle time, evenly since the desk's previous transaction). So 0.95 is out of reach on the tuned evening itself, and nothing was loosened or built. (3) Why: a Visitors transaction takes about two minutes of work, so in light demand a desk completes one every two to four minutes, while the work per desk changes from minute to minute at the start and end of each arrival wave (9303, 17:28 to 17:30: 10, 21 and 33 s); one desk's completions cannot show which of those minutes crossed 30 s. The disagreements sit in those ramps, mostly several desks at once (with option (a): on 2026 17:28 to 17:32, 17:46 to 17:49, 17:54 to 17:58, 18:39, 19:01 to 19:05, 19:19 to 19:21, 19:28 to 19:29, 19:46, 20:00 to 20:03 and 20:16 to 20:19; on 9303 17:29 to 17:31, 17:53 to 17:58, 18:39, 19:00 to 19:11, 19:23 to 19:27, 20:04 to 20:06 and 20:14 to 20:15). (4) Options for the owner, none built: (i) the per-desk rule with option (a) as an improvement on ARV-149's rows (about +0.014 and +0.021) and its Proposed bounds below 0.95; (ii) the lane's AMAN work pooled over its working desks (the simulator shares the work equally, ARV-151): at most 0.944 on 9303 even in hindsight; (iii) the lane's work from the queue sensors' exits (exits x s / n_open), each desk minute decided once the same minute's queue row is published: about 0.99 on both evenings in the model, 0.945 and 0.947 when the exits are a minute behind; it is sensor-derived and rests on a lane's desks sharing the work, which the pilot would have to show; (iv) judge desks without zones at the throughput level (open or not). The review recommendations of ARV-149 and ARV-151 were applied in the same story.
- Owner decision on ARV-149a (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-10), on the measurement above: for a border desk without a staff or service zone, the desk-state agreement (D6, F18) is judged at the throughput level, open (`Idle` or `Serving`, F8's n_open) against not open (`Paused` or `Closed`); its target stays 0.95. The agreement on `Serving` against `Idle` is reported beside it for such desks, with no target. Reason: AMAN's interval statistics give a desk's completed transactions per minute, not when within the minutes its work fell, so no rule on them reaches 0.95 desk by desk (0.940 on the reference evening even in hindsight), while open or not open agrees in 0.996 and 0.992 of desk minutes on the two evenings with ARV-149's rows. A desk with a staff or service zone is judged on its full state as before. The KPI annex says so: 0.95 on `Serving` against `Idle` needs a staff or service zone at each desk (a line of the bill of quantities), and a site whose desks have none commits to the throughput level. This replaces the earlier choice of the same day (rows 5a and 5b on the intervals' service seconds), which the measurement showed cannot meet the target; ARV-149's rows 5a to 5f stay as built. The queue-exit option was set aside: its 0.99 in the simulator rests on the truth sharing a lane's work evenly over its desks, the same assumption the rule would make, and a real hall is unproven. Carried out in ARV-149a (rescoped to the comparison engine).

- Desk-state agreement at the throughput level for desks without zones (ARV-149a as rescoped), built 2026-10-10 on the owner's decision above, in the comparison engine (ARV-104f) and its service (ARV-104g), the desk engine unchanged, with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner (formulas F18, The level of a judged minute; wiki 15 section 8; TC-85): (1) Where the level comes from: the zone profile version in force at each judged minute (published at or before it and not retired by then, as the availability ledger reads versions), at the full state when that version has a staff or service zone naming the desk and a queue zone (as the desk feed gives a desk its zones), at the throughput level when it has none; read by the results service from `zone_profile` and `zone` (configuration, immutable once published), never from the observers, with no new column or script. Alternative: the desk feed's own record of the minute, a `desk_sensor_minute` row written beside the desk minute for desks with zones; not chosen, because a missing sensor minute (an engine rebuilt after a restart, a desk beyond the sensor-only engine's cap) would then judge a desk with zones at the easier level. (2) Fail closed: a minute no version covers (before the site's first publication) or covers only in part (the minute within which a version is published, either way), and every minute of a desk whose level row the engine refuses, are judged on the full state; where two versions would overlap the full state wins. (3) Serving against Idle (no target): of the throughput-level minutes the observer and the stored minute both call open, the share naming the same state; the existing confusion of observed against dominant states stays beside it. (4) Frozen results: the result's shape grows by fields only (each desk summary's minutes at the throughput level, Serving against Idle, and the level computed from them; the desk view's left-out level rows), so a document frozen before ARV-149a is served from its own bytes with its content hash unchanged and reads as judged on the full state, which it was; no version field is added, since the absence of the counts says the same. (5) A campaign's observations end when its profile version retires (ARV-104b), so within one campaign the level changes only for minutes observed before its version's publication; the rule handles any number of changes all the same.

- Health-check alarms and degraded propagation (ARV-114b), built 2026-10-11, with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner (formulas F11, F18 and F20; wiki 11 section 8, wiki 15 sections 2, 3 and 8): (1) F11 rule: a bin whose track completion (tracks exited over tracks that entered, F18) is below 0.90 is `Degraded`, judged only once the bin is final (a provisional bin's open tracks are people still queuing; judging them would flag every bin while it fills), never for a bin without tracks (no rate), compared with F18's 1e-9 tolerance (18 of 20 is not below 0.90); the threshold is the pilot's track completion target (`BinSettings.TrackCompletionDegradedBelow`, 0 disables). The flag is part of the bin result, so it reaches `queue_bin.quality` and every reader of the stored bins; bins made final before the change keep their flag until a recomputation revises them. (2) The conservation residual and occupancy out of range raise alarms but do not flag a bin: they compare two measurements without saying which is wrong (alternative: flag above a residual tolerance, which would need its own threshold). (3) Units and values of the three alert metrics: `ConservationResidual` is the residual's size in people, whichever its sign (0 to 100,000, the queue length's bound); `TrackCompletion` is percent, 100 x exited / entered (0 to 100); `OccupancyOutOfRange` is the minutes of the bin outside 0 to the capacity (0 to 1,440, a bin's longest), with no value when no minute was checked against a capacity. (4) Granularity follows ARV-114a's checks: per queue zone and bin, never per line or per minute; each stored queue minute takes the value of the bin that ended last by the minute's end (its latest revision), so a bin's value holds for the 15 minutes after it ends (alternative: the worst bin within a look-back, as `BinP90` takes 150 minutes, which would keep an alarm up long after the measurement recovered). (5) Settled values and the live wait: a value counts once it can no longer change except through a recomputation (residual once both boundaries are measured, occupancy once the end boundary is sampled, completion once the bin is final or has ended with no track or every track exited), and the live evaluation stops before the first minute of a bin not yet settled, so live equals the backtest over the same stored rows; completion alarms come up to about T_censor after their bin, and a bin unsettled 180 minutes after its end (the evaluation's catch-up) no longer holds the rule back. Reads are bounded (security review, CWE-120): zones and targets are read in clusters of nearby times, each only the bins that can govern its minutes, at most 50,000 rows per read, refused beyond it (the tick skips the rule with one warning naming the site and the rule code, a backtest answers 400). As for `BinP90`, a revision written after its minutes were judged can make a later backtest differ. (6) Health-check rules watch queue zones only (the checks are kept per queue zone, an overflow band's readings counting in its queue's); a rule naming an overflow zone is refused with its own message, and the screen lists queue zones only for them. (7) Decided by the lead in the ARV-114b security review as the fail-closed choice, Proposed for the owner (option a): a bin set apart only because of its own F11 track-completion flag is set apart by no criterion; count accuracy, the wait error and bias, the nowcast error and track completion judge it like a Good bin. The stored bin carries the reason (`queue_bin.degraded_by_completion_only`, script 0051; the comparison refuses the flag beside anything but a Final, Degraded bin, and believes it only when the bin's own health row is final, counted tracks and fell below the threshold; a recomputation that changes only the flag writes a revision), so outages, Unknown, Provisional, other versions and a bin Degraded for another cause as well are still set apart; track completion also judges every final bin below its target whatever its flag (fail closed). The case behind it: ten bins at 99 percent and one at 80 percent with 15 of 20 tracks exited failed count accuracy before ARV-114b and would have passed once the self-flagged bin was set apart. The two thresholds are one constant (`HealthChecks.TrackCompletionTarget`, 0.90; neither setting is configured per host, and a test pins them equal). Results frozen before keep their figures; a recomputation (a new results revision: Validation.Manage with a second factor, audited) applies the rule. (8) No health-check rule is seeded for the demo airport (the seed's R-001 to R-005 are the prototype's); whether sites get default health rules (for example track completion below 90 on every tracking queue) is the owner's call. (9) Not done here: zone health for a dead overflow band sensor (a band going Unknown, ARV-115) and the functional test that R-002 neither clears nor grows through an Unknown spell (ARV-115's review), both left as noted there.
- Owner decision on sign-in (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-11): no change to sign-in for now. The installation will use an identity provider later (ARV-134a, site identity provider sign-in with OIDC and account linking; ARV-134b, step-up and MFA through the provider), and the first System administrator will come through it. ARV-121 (first administrator bootstrap with a one-time setup code, a forced password change and TOTP enrolment) is therefore not built now: it waits behind ARV-134a and is re-scoped then. No sign-in code changes until the identity provider work.
- Weekly summary figures (ARV-119), built 2026-10-11, computation only (no endpoint, template, document, CSV, schedule or delivery: those are ARV-146, the owner's decision of 2026-10-08), with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner (formulas "Weekly summary figures", wiki 15 section 2): (1) The week: Monday 00:00 to the next Monday 00:00 in the site's airport time zone, resolved in .NET (`SiteTimeZones`, TimeZoneConverter) and handed to the database as hour and day bounds, so PostgreSQL's time zone data never decides a local day; the days are the daily report's (`DailyReports.DayBounds`), so a week with a clock change has 167 or 169 hours, a skipped midnight starts its day at the first valid minute and a half-hour change leaves a short last hour. A site without an airport is summarised in UTC (as the daily report); a site whose airport names a zone the host does not know is refused (`TimeZoneUnknown`), never summarised in UTC (as the availability ledger). Alternative: a Sunday or Saturday start where the client's week starts there. (2) Which weeks: the date of a Monday, from the current local week (still running: the figures run to the request, `complete` is false and days not yet started are marked) back 57 weeks (about the daily report's 400 days); any other date is refused before any read. A week before a site's data, or partly before it, lists those days empty: no passengers, no percentile (null, never 0), no peak, no recorded ledger minute and no ratio. (3) Lanes and waits: every queue zone of the published profile and every zone with minutes in the week, as the daily report; per lane and local day the daily report's figures (passengers, waits, P50 and P90 from the merged minute histograms, the longest queue, provisional and missing-histogram flags) and every local hour, so a lane's day reads exactly as that day's report (an integration test compares all seven days); per lane over the week the same from all its hours merged. The minute histograms are merged per lane, hour and bucket in the database (the continuous aggregate `queue_minute_15m` holds no histograms, so it cannot give percentiles), at most one row per lane and hour. (4) Peak hours: per lane and day the daily report's rule (the hour with the highest P90 among hours with at least 20 waits, the earlier label on a tie); per lane over the week the same over its hours (the earlier hour on a tie); and the site's ten lane-hours with the highest P90 of the week (then the earlier hour, then the zone). Alternative: the busiest hours by passengers, or the hours above the 15-minute target. (5) Alerts and time to acknowledge: the alerts raised in the week (by raise time, on the local day raised) that the reader's roles see, the alert screens' rule as in the daily report; counted by severity, by rule (with its latest name and severity) and by day, escalations counted. Time to acknowledge is raised to acknowledged, in minutes, over the acknowledged alerts only and whenever acknowledged (an alert raised late on Sunday and acknowledged on Monday counts with its real time): nearest-rank P50 and P90, mean and longest. Alerts never acknowledged are counted apart, still open or closed without an acknowledgement (cleared by themselves, withdrawn or resolved by hand), never as zero minutes or as the time to the week's end. Alternative: count an unacknowledged alert as acknowledged at its clearing or at the week's end (censored), which would lower the figures for alerts nobody answered. (6) Device uptime: the daily report's rule over the week: the share of the week's elapsed minutes (up to the request for a running week) with no outage of the device in the outage record (`zone_outage`), overlapping outages once, and a device the registry has offline counted out from its last heartbeat until the stream records its outage; the devices of the site that are neither retired nor deleted at the request, so a device added mid-week counts as up before it existed. Alternative: count from each device's commissioning. (7) Data quality: the latest revision of every stored bin (`queue_bin`) and health bin (`zone_health_bin`, ARV-114a) whose start falls in the week; bins by status and quality, with the bins Degraded for their own track completion alone (ARV-114b) apart; track completion over final bins as summed exited tracks (each bin's capped at its entered tracks) over summed entered tracks, never an average of bin rates; final bins below the 0.90 target with F18's tolerance (F11's rule); the conservation residual's size over the bins where it was measured (count, sum, largest, mean); occupancy minutes checked against a physical capacity and those outside it. (8) Overflow minutes: TC-19 as `overflow_bin_15m` counts them (distinct minutes in which any band of the queue held anyone), read from `overflow_minute` by its own time column with the same rule, because the view groups before it filters and would make the hypertable read every chunk of the site; per lane and day, with the minutes a band reading was observed and the most people one band held. (9) Availability: the ledger's minutes per local date of the week (ARV-118, ARV-150) through one query shared with the availability read (`AvailabilityLedgerReader`, moved out of `SvcAvailability` unchanged), so both read the same counts. (10) Time in each desk state: seconds closed, idle, serving, paused and unknown summed from `desk_minute`, with the desk minutes they came from, per day and week; nothing called utilisation (TC-45 is open). Per-desk rows are border desks (Immigration and Emigration checkpoints) and only for `BorderDesks.View` holders; lane aggregates per checkpoint kind and lane go to every reader with the desks that had open time; for a reader without `BorderDesks.View` each border cell (a day, or the week) is shown only where at least 3 desks had open time in it (idle, serving or paused above 0; a desk with closed or unknown minutes only never counts, since the desk engine writes every desk's minute), the days all or none (a lane whose every day with open time had 3 shows every day, otherwise none, so the week less the days shown never gives a withheld day) and the desk count only with the week; corrected after the security review of 2026-10-11, which found that a weekly count of desks with any minute let one open desk's figures through; a small-cell rule like the immigration screen's (ARV-057), which counts desks with transactions per window instead; check-in and security lanes go only to `AirportDesks.View` holders and never per desk (the handler's own counters wait for its module); a desk key shared by checkpoints of more than one kind, and any other checkpoint kind, are left out (fail closed). (11) Who may read: a caller that reaches the site (ISiteScope) and whose roles read reports (`Report.View`); the service checks the permission itself because no endpoint carries it yet, and answers NotFound otherwise, as for a site that does not exist. A pending account (a temporary password, or TOTP required and not enrolled) or a disabled one holds no role, as the permission resolver treats a pending principal; the service has no principal to hand the resolver, so it reads the stored account with `User.IsPending`, the rule that gives a token the pending scope (sign-in itself unchanged). Data quality and availability go only to `DataQuality.View` holders (today the same roles). ARV-146 decides how the figures reach the report tool; the reader (`WeeklySummaryReader`) takes the recipient's roles, as the daily report's deliveries do. (12) Bounds (CWE-120): one week per request; at most 200 lanes, 30,000 desk, lane and day rows, 20,000 alerts, 2,000 devices and 20,000 outages (`WeeklySummaryLimits`); a read beyond one is refused (`TooLarge`), never cut short. (13) Nothing is stored and no script is added: the summary is computed on request like the daily report; percentiles and minutes are rounded to 2 decimals, track completion to 4, desk seconds to whole seconds. Accepted residual, Proposed for the owner (security review 2026-10-11): a desk with any open time in a cell counts toward the three, so a cell can still be almost entirely one desk's, the same weakness as the immigration screen's count of desks with one transaction; a later rule may require a minimum open time per desk per cell (for example one minute).
- Overflow and data-quality views (ARV-120), built 2026-10-11, with these points proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner (wiki 12, Live operations, Immigration and Devices): (1) A read endpoint was added, since none gave the screens the bands' state: `GET api/v1/sites/{siteCode}/overflow-bands` with `LiveQueue.View`, site-scoped, as the live hub's zone snapshots: zone-level occupancy only, so every role of the live screen reads it, handler station managers included (they see every queue zone of their site there). An answer holds at most 500 bands, every band whole: beyond that the read is refused with 409 and a fixed text, never cut short (the project's bound rule, corrected after the security review of 2026-10-11, which found a cut answer with a truncated flag), and the screens mark no queue and say so. Alternative: carry the bands' state in the live snapshot, which needs a change to the stream and the snapshot contract. (2) A band's latest minute with a reading counts for 6 minutes on Ariva's clock (`ISvcOverflowBands.FreshMinutes`): the stream calls a band Unknown three minutes after its last reading, and a band minute reaches `overflow_minute` about two and a half minutes after it starts (its minute, then the 90-second lateness). Without a reading in the window a band is NotHeard, never Empty (TC-19's rule that a minute without a reading is not observed); a queue is Occupied when any band is, Empty only when every band reported empty. (3) The live and immigration screens mark only an occupied band (Overflow in use, with each band, its people and its minute in the marker's text); Empty and NotHeard show nothing, since a silent band sensor is a device matter for the Devices screen and a site whose bands have no sensor would carry a mark on every queue. Alternative: a grey Overflow not heard mark. (4) The mark is in the zones table and the lanes table, not on the floor plan (the prototype hatches the band on the plan; Ariva's plan draws queue zones only). (5) The data-quality panel judges each bin by F18's Proposed targets: a conservation residual of 0 (any other value amber, Not measured grey), track completion of at least 90 percent judged on final bins only (a provisional bin shows its share so far in grey, since its open tracks are people still queuing, F11), and any minute outside 0 to the zone's physical capacity amber (No capacity set grey). Alternative: a tolerance of one or two people on the residual for sensor noise, to agree in the KPI annex. (6) What the panel shows: on the live screen the selected zone's bins of the last 2 hours, newest first (up to 8); on the immigration screen the latest bin of each lane queue of the hall shown; refreshed every minute through the existing per-zone read (`zone-health`, one request per zone, at most 12 zones per panel), with no site-wide endpoint. Alternative: a site-wide latest-bin read if a hall grows beyond 12 lane queues. (7) Who sees it: `DataQuality.View` holders only (border shift supervisors, terminal duty managers and administrators, the owner's grants of 2026-10-06), on the immigration screen too: the checks are zone-level counts, not desk data, so the terminal duty manager reads the lanes' data quality while the desk grid stays border data. The screen hides the panel from anyone else and asks nothing; the server refuses the read (403). (8) Health-check alarms on the Devices screen: the open alerts of the three health-check metrics from the existing alert search (one page of the site's 500 newest open alerts, filtered on the screen, with a note when the site has more), grouped by the alert's zone, under the alert screens' rule of who sees an alert (owner role, escalation role once escalated, every role without an owner, administrators); open alarms only, no history; they are acknowledged on the live screen's Alerts panel. Alternative: a metric filter on the alert search, which the screen would use if a site keeps more than 500 open alerts. (9) Refresh: the bands every 30 seconds (the stream writes a band minute about every minute), the panels and the alarms every 60 seconds.
- Site configuration export and import (ARV-122a), built 2026-10-11 (wiki 11 section 2d, formulas F22). Owner decisions (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-11): (O1) F22 v1 stays exactly as it is; a portable geometry hash (`ariva-zone-profile-portable-v1`), computed on request and never stored, names each level by its terminal and level codes and each desk by its checkpoint and desk codes, everything else as v1; the export answers the published profile's v1 and portable hashes, and the round trip compares the portable hash once the copy is published (formulas F22). (O2) For ARV-122b: imported alert rules and displays are checked against the imported draft's zones with the same rules, and act only once it is published. (O3) For ARV-122b: an alert rule's escalation contact is never exported; the import answer lists the rules that need one. The lead's calls, Proposed: (L1) a display code already taken in the target database answers 409 (ARV-122b); (L2) the airport must already exist on the target and is never created; (L3) device calibrations are never exported (ARV-122b). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) The bundle, format version 1: one JSON document `{ formatVersion, sourceSite, exportedAtUtc, manifest: { part: sha256 }, parts: { part: base64 } }`; each part is the base64 of its own bytes (UTF-8 JSON for `topology`, `deskCodeMappings`, `zoneProfile` and `floorPlans`, the raw bytes of each `floorPlanFile.N`, N being the plan's row from 1, without a gap) and the manifest hashes the decoded bytes, so no canonical JSON is needed. The four configuration parts are always present, an empty list when the site has none (`zoneProfile` holds none or one), so a part can never be dropped silently; an unknown or extra part, a manifest entry without its part and a part without its entry are refused. ARV-122b adds `devices`, `alertRules` and `displays` by name without a new format version; until then they are refused as unknown. Open for ARV-122b: whether a bundle exported before it (without its three parts) is still imported or refused as missing them. (2) The bundle hash, answered by the export and the import as `bundleSha256` for an out-of-band comparison: the SHA-256 of a canonical text (`ariva-site-configuration-v1`, then the format version, the source site and the export time, UTC to the second, then one line per manifest entry ordered by part name), so the envelope cannot change either without changing the hash (the brief named the manifest only; the envelope is added because the audit records the source site). `sourceSite` is the bundle's claimed source, not authenticated: like the rest of a bundle it proves nothing about who made it, and the audit and the import's answer record it as claimed. (3) Trust (CWE-501): the manifest proves that the parts were not changed or cut after export; it does not prove who made the bundle, since anyone can recompute SHA-256. The trust comes from the import being a step-up critical action of a system administrator, and every part is decoded and checked as if an administrator had typed it into the existing endpoints: request rules with the endpoints' limits (Fx.Specification, `SiteConfigurationRules`), then the entities' own rules, the ARV-018 type check, SVG sanitiser and raster bounds on every file and the upload's file name check. Nothing in a bundle is an id: every record is created with a new id and references are resolved through the bundle's codes and names. Every refusal is a fixed text that repeats nothing from the bundle or the request. (4) The endpoints: `GET` (export) and `POST` (import) `api/v1/admin/sites/{siteCode}/configuration`, the AdminArea's route prefix (the brief wrote `api/v1/sites/...`, the OpsArea's), with a new permission `SiteConfiguration.Transfer` (a new action, Transfer) that only system administrators hold through the role seed's every-permission rule (grants are stored as role codes, so no seed or migration change), `[RequiresRecentMfa]` on both (the export hands over a site's whole configuration), `[SiteScoped]` and the service's own site check (404 without a detail), the upload concurrency policy (one at a time per host with the floor plan and SSIM uploads) and `Cache-Control: no-store`. The import answers 201. (5) Bounds (CWE-120): the import body is at most 20 MB (`[RequestSizeLimit]`, the file-import class, the floor plan limit), within the existing ingress `proxy-body-size` of 21m on api-main, so no ingress change; JSON at most 32 levels deep for the body and for each part; a configuration part at most 8 MB decoded and a file part at most the floor plan limit, each checked on its base64 length before decoding and again on the decoded length; at most 204 parts; per kind at most 50 terminals, 200 levels, 1,000 checkpoints, 5,000 desks, 10,000 desk code mappings, one floor plan per level (200), and the profile's own 500 zones and 1,000 lines (`ZoneProfile.MaxZones`, `MaxLines`); names 200 characters, file names 200 code points (the column's). The export reads each kind at its bound plus one and refuses beyond it, never cutting a site short, reads the plan files one at a time against the bundle's budget, and refuses a site whose bundle would pass 20 MB (409), so every export can be imported. (6) Empty means no live terminal, level, checkpoint, desk, floor plan, device (a retired one included), desk code mapping, alert rule or display, and no zone profile of any status (409 otherwise). The import runs in one transaction with the site row locked (`SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`, the zone profile service's lock) before the emptiness check, so of two imports one sees the site empty; a unique index refusing a row at the flush is 409; any other failure keeps nothing (files stored before the failure are deleted after the rollback), is audited as failed and answers 500. Residual: topology endpoints do not take the site lock, so a record created at the target by hand while an import runs is not refused by the emptiness check (a code collision still refuses the import at its flush). Closed 2026-10-24 by ARV-154: every create that adds configuration to a site (terminal, level, checkpoint, desk and desk range, desk code mapping, floor plan, alert rule, display; the zone profile draft and the device already took the row for update) takes the site's row in share mode after its own checks and before its first write or any other lock (`SvcDb.ShareSiteAsync`), and holds it to its commit, so an import waits for the creates in flight and they wait for it; creates do not wait for each other. Before, such a create's insert locked the row (by its foreign key) only at its flush, so an import could find the site empty between the create's checks and its commit. (7) The airport: a site has no airport of its own (each terminal names one), so an empty target says nothing about where its terminals go. The bundle names each terminal's airport by IATA code, which must exist on the target (never created, L2). The optional query `airportCode` places every terminal at that existing airport instead (an IATA code, checked to exist, after the site scope check; airports are deployment-wide reference data, as for creating a terminal). It is needed because terminal codes are unique per airport: a copy within one deployment at the same airport collides (409), which is also how the API suite and the integration tests run the round trip. (8) Export: the published zone profile only; a site without one is exported without it, with `zoneProfileIncluded` false and a note. Refused (409, fixed texts): a site with two live terminals of one code at different airports (neither the bundle nor the portable hash could tell them apart), a published profile on a removed level or desk, a kind beyond its bound, a plan file missing from storage or not the bytes its record hashed. The reads share one repeatable-read snapshot. (9) Import: the zone profile arrives as a draft, published with the existing step-up action; the draft must reproduce the bundle's portable hash (otherwise 400); the parts carry no site code (only the envelope's source), so everything is bound to the target site, and a queue zone name too long for the target's zone keys (ARV-114c) is refused with its own text; kinds are read by their exact names only (stricter than the endpoints' parsing, which reads `Immigration, CheckIn` as a combination of flags). (10) The audit: one entry per export (`SiteConfiguration.Exported` or `ExportRefused`) and per import past the site check (`Imported`, in the import's transaction; `ImportRefused` for a refusal and `ImportFailed` for any unexpected failure, the database, storage or the file checks, each in a transaction of its own; and `ImportFailed` by a post-rollback action of the unit of work when the import is rolled back after its records were written, a failed commit included) with the outcome, a refusal's fixed text, the bundle hash when the manifest checked out, the format version, the claimed source site, the number of parts and the counts; never a part's content or a name. Limits: an audit entry that cannot be written (the database itself unreachable) is logged as an error, never silent; an attempt on a site out of the caller's reach is not audited (as everywhere, it answers like a site that does not exist); a cancelled request (the caller went away) is not audited and keeps nothing; a commit that reaches the database while its acknowledgement is lost is rolled back in the unit of work, so its files are removed and `ImportFailed` stands beside a committed `Imported` (the floor plan upload shares this; security review round 2, accepted residual). The per-record entries the topology endpoints write are not repeated for an import (security review 2026-10-11, N4). (11) Never exported: users, roles, integration clients and connections, outbound endpoints, secrets, audit entries, time series, validation data, report schedules and device calibrations (L3), and in ARV-122a also devices, alert rules and displays (ARV-122b). Nothing about officers, travellers or documents is held by these parts. (12) Floor plans: the type comes from the file's bytes, never from the bundle, and an SVG is sanitised again (an export's already sanitised SVG comes back byte for byte in practice, an integration test shows it, but nothing relies on it).
- Site configuration export and import: devices, alert rules and displays (ARV-122b), built 2026-10-11 (wiki 11 section 2d, wiki 04 section 10). Owner decisions (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-11): (O1) imported alert rules and displays are checked against the imported draft's zones with the same rules as the existing endpoints: queue zones and overflow bands for rules (a health check watches queue zones only), queue zones only for displays; they act only once the draft is published. (O2) An alert rule's escalation contact is never exported; the import answer lists the rules that need one typed again, by rule code only (`rulesNeedingEscalationContact`). The lead's calls, Proposed: (L1) a display code already taken by a live display in the target database answers 409 with a fixed text that names no code; (L2) device calibrations are never exported; (L3) the three new parts `devices`, `alertRules` and `displays` are required from ARV-122b on and always written (an empty list when there is nothing); a bundle without them is refused like any missing part, and the format version stays 1 (this settles ARV-122a's open point: a bundle exported before ARV-122b is refused; export it again); (L4, security review of 2026-10-11, N3) an imported rule code above `R-100000` is refused with 400 and its own fixed text (`RuleCodeTooHigh`), since codes are kept and the rule service numbers a new rule after the highest code ever used, so a bundle with `R-999999` would leave the target site unable to create a rule; this leaves the target at least 899,999 codes, and an export refuses (409, `RuleCodesBeyondImportBound`) a site with a code above the bound, so every export can be imported. Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) Devices are imported through the registration path itself (`ISvcDevices.RegisterAsync`, inside the import's transaction once the draft is flushed): its rules (kinds by exact name, the supported transports, the mapping catalog, the queue zone in the imported draft on the device's level and within its footprint's reach, the code unique at the site), its credential issuing and its own audit entry per device (`Device.Registered`, which, as for any registration, holds the new credential's prefix, the model and the zone name, never the credential). Each arrives in Commissioning with a new credential returned once in the import's answer (`deviceCredentials`: code and credential) and is calibrated again. A device the registration path refuses refuses the whole import with the devices part's fixed text. (2) Never exported: credentials (prefix, hash, issue time), calibrations, a device's state, its access settings (source networks and certificate pin name the source's networks and certificate, which a copy should not inherit), alert rule states, alerts, report schedules, and display credentials and pairing state. As the review asked (N1), each device row says only whether its access settings were withheld (`accessSettingsWithheld`, never which networks or how many), and the import's answer lists those devices by code (`devicesNeedingAccessSettings`, as `rulesNeedingEscalationContact`), so a site rebuilt after a loss knows which devices to secure again. Retired devices (the source's history) are not exported either. A vendor footprint travels; an assumed one is computed again by the registration path from the family and mounting height. (3) Bounds: 2,000 devices (the weekly summary's bound on a site's devices), 500 alert rules (`SvcAlertRules.MaxRulesPerSite`) and 200 displays (`SvcDisplays.MaxDisplaysPerSite`), each refused one beyond at export (409) and at import (400); the part bound becomes 207 (seven configuration parts and 200 files). (4) The export refuses (409, fixed texts) a device on a level that no longer exists, and a device, rule or display that names a zone the published profile does not offer it (any of them when no profile is published), since the import could not place it; a site is never cut short. (5) Rule codes are kept (the canonical `R-nnn` of a number from 1 to 100,000, L4, once each), so the rules listed for a contact read the same at both sites, and the rule service gives the next rule the number after the highest. An import goes only into a site without any zone profile, and a rule needs a published profile, so a kept code cannot meet a deleted rule's code at the target; were it to, the unique index answers `SiteNotEmpty`. (6) Roles: as the rule service checks a created rule, a caller who is not a system administrator may not import a rule without an owner or naming a role it does not hold (Transfer is held by system administrators only, so today every caller passes). (7) Displays arrive unpaired (no credential) and are paired with the existing step-up action `POST api/v1/admin/displays/{id}/credential`; a display's settings name only queue zones and the built-in languages, nothing outside the bundle. (8) Order of an import's checks (corrected after the security review, N4): the airport code's shape; the envelope, manifest and part sizes; each part read strictly; the request rules; the airports; the site row lock and the emptiness check (409); the plan (a terminal code taken at its airport, 409; the entities' rules for the topology, mappings, profile, alert rules and displays, then the rules' and displays' zones against the draft, 400); the rule service's role check (400); the floor plan files (400); the display code check (409); then the writes (records, then files; a unique index met meanwhile answers 409) and, last, each device through the registration path, whose own rules (supported transport, mapping catalog, zone in the draft on the device's level and within reach, code) run only then (400). So a bundle with a taken display code answers 409 even when the registration path would refuse one of its devices, and a site that is not empty answers 409 before any check of the plan. (9) Every member of a row is required (`RespectRequiredConstructorParameters`, for all seven parts): an export writes every member, null when empty, so a row that leaves one out (a withheld flag included) is unreadable rather than read as a default.
- CSV imports of devices and desk code mappings: API (ARV-123a), built 2026-10-12 (wiki 07 section 2, wiki 11 section 2e; the screens are ARV-123b). The lead's calls (2026-10-12): (L1) CsvHelper 33.1.0, already pinned in `Directory.Packages.props` (from AMAN's list, ARV-041) but referenced by no project until now, is referenced from Ariva.Infra only, with no version attribute; only its low-level `CsvParser` is used (no `CsvReader` record mapping, dynamic types or class maps), with its limits set explicitly. NuGet lists no vulnerability for 33.1.0, its latest version, and no package dependency on net8.0 or net9.0; licence MS-PL or Apache-2.0. (L2) Accepted as proposed, Proposed: a preview token of 15 minutes, Data Protection with one purpose per kind of import; no second factor on either preview and none on the mapping commit, consistent with the single mapping endpoint; the limits below; the columns below, with a header row of exact names. (L3) Number columns: a leading minus is read only as a sign and the existing ranges apply; `+`, `=` and `@` never in a number. (L4) The commit sends the file again with the token, and its hash is checked against the token before anything else is parsed. Owner question (flagged by the lead): should a bulk mapping import be a critical action (a second factor), although the single mapping endpoint is not? Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) Endpoints: `POST api/v1/admin/sites/{siteCode}/device-imports/preview` and `/commit` with `CreateDevice`; `POST api/v1/admin/sites/{siteCode}/desk-code-mapping-imports/preview` and `/commit` with `CreateDeskCodeMapping` (the single endpoints' create permissions); all `[SiteScoped]` with the service's own site check (404 outside, before anything about the upload), the `upload` concurrency policy, multipart field `file` (and `previewToken` on commit), answers never cached. (2) The device preview needs a second factor too, unlike L2: `CreateDevice` is a critical permission (`security/critical-actions.json`), and `CriticalActionsTests` requires every endpoint it guards to carry `[RequiresRecentMfa]`; keeping that rule whole was preferred to a preview under another permission. In practice one second factor covers the preview and its commit (15 minutes each). Alternative: a separate, non-critical permission for device previews. (3) A preview is a dry run of the service itself: every row that passes the reader's checks goes through `ISvcDevices.RegisterAsync` or `ISvcDeskCodeMappings.CreateAsync`, unchanged, under the site row lock, and the transaction is rolled back; the dry run's credentials are dropped in the service and never answered, logged or audited. So the preview refuses exactly what the commit would (the mapping catalog, the supported transports, the zone and its reach, a code taken, a mapping that does not fit its desk), unless something changes in between, which the commit checks again. (4) The token binds the kind, the file's SHA-256, the site, the user and its issue time (Ariva's clock, 5 minutes of skew between hosts allowed); it is issued only for a file without a refused row; it is not single use, since a second commit of the same file finds every code taken and keeps nothing. Refused for another user, site, file or kind, an altered token or after 15 minutes: 409 with a fixed text (as the SSIM import's). (5) One unit: the whole file in one transaction, all or nothing, with the site row lock serialising imports of a site. Measured in the integration tests (Testcontainers PostgreSQL on the development container): 500 devices, preview 2.5 to 3.1 s and commit 3.4 to 4.1 s; 10,000 mappings, preview 11.8 s and commit 14.3 s. Both well within a request's time, so the bounds stay. (6) Limits (CWE-120): a file of at most 1 MB (hashed first, refused one byte beyond with 413); cells of at most 200 characters, and CsvHelper's `MaxFieldSize` at 800 as the parser's own bound (beyond it the file is refused); 500 device rows and 10,000 mapping rows (one more refuses the file); at most 100 refusals listed in row and column order, with the total; UTF-8 only, a byte order mark optional (UTF-16 and UTF-32 refused). (7) Columns: devices `code, family, model, transport, dialect, clockSource, terminal, level, x, y, mountingHeightMetres, orientationDegrees, queueZone`, optional `mappingName, footprintLengthMetres, footprintWidthMetres, footprintRadiusMetres`; mappings `externalSystem, externalCode, checkpoint, desk`. Any order; each name once; an optional column may be absent or its cell empty. Kinds by their exact names; codes by the topology pattern (the import takes any code that passes it, TC-40); a level by its terminal and level code (a site with one terminal code at two airports cannot tell them apart and the row is refused); a desk by its checkpoint and desk code. (8) Cells (CWE-79, CSV injection): a text cell may not start with `=`, `+`, `-`, `@`, a tab or a carriage return, also after leading spaces; no cell holds a control character (a line break inside quotes is valid CSV, but no column takes one); a number is digits with an optional leading minus and decimal point, nothing else (no `+`, exponent, spaces, NaN or infinity), within the registration request's range (x and y from -1 to 2001 m, height 0 to 100 m, orientation 0 to 360 degrees, footprints 0 to 1000 m); a minus that does not start a number is a formula prefix. (9) Rows are numbered as a spreadsheet shows them: the header is row 1 and every record counts (a blank line counts and is skipped; a quoted line break does not start a row); columns count from 1 in the file's own order. (10) Answers: each refusal is a row, a column (none for the whole row) and a fixed text; the services' own fixed texts keep their meaning, and any other message (an entity's, which may name a value) becomes one fixed text. A refused commit answers `Row N:` and the fixed text, 400; a file over 1 MB 413; a token that is not the preview's 409. (11) Audit: `DeviceImport.Previewed`, `.Committed`, `.Refused` and `.Failed`, and the same for `DeskCodeMappingImport`, each with the kind, the outcome, a fixed reason, the file's SHA-256 and the counts, never a cell, a code from the file or a credential; previews and refusals in a transaction of their own, a commit with its records, and a commit rolled back after it was written as `.Failed` by a post-rollback action. Not audited (security review, residual): the controller's refusals before the service runs (no file, a file over 1 MB, a token of the wrong shape) and a cancelled request. The registration's own `Device.Registered` and the mapping service's `DeskCodeMapping.Created` entries come with the commit only (a preview's are rolled back).
- Floor plan file names bounded in code points (ARV-153), built 2026-10-12, a defect of ARV-018 found in the ARV-122a security review (N5): the upload cut a name at 200 text elements while `floor_plan.original_file_name` is varchar(200), which PostgreSQL counts in code points, so one letter with 300 combining marks passed the cut and failed the insert at the commit (22001, a 500). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) Refuse, not cut: a name longer than 200 code points after trimming is refused with 400 and a fixed text (`TopologyErrors.FileNameTooLong`), before anything is read or stored, as the site configuration import already refuses it (ARV-122a), so both paths follow one rule (`FloorPlan.FileNameFits`, also used by `SiteConfigurationRules.IsFileName`). A cut would have had to avoid splitting surrogate pairs and leaving a lone combining mark, and would store a name the person never chose. (2) Code points as PostgreSQL counts them: a surrogate pair (an emoji) counts once, each combining mark once; the UTF-16 length is checked first (more than 400 units cannot fit), so a long name is never walked. (3) The existing checks run first: a path, a backslash, `..`, a colon and a lone surrogate as before, and control and format characters now judged by code point rather than by UTF-16 unit (security review, 2026-10-12), so invisible format characters outside the basic plane (tag characters, shorthand format controls, musical formatting) are refused like a right-to-left override; they answer the existing fixed text; a blank name is still stored as "floor-plan". (4) The entity refuses a longer name too (`FloorPlan`'s constructor), the last line before the column, so no caller can reach the insert with one; the upload answers its fixed text, never a 500. (5) A change for people: a long ordinary name (more than 200 letters), which was cut and stored before, is now refused with 400; shorten the name and upload again. No schema change.
- CSV import screens (ARV-123b), built 2026-10-12 on the ARV-123a API (wiki 11 section 2e, wiki 07 section 2, wiki 12 Devices and Topology); the prototype has no import screen, so these points are proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) One dialog for both imports, a native modal `<dialog>` like the step-up dialog (Ariva.Web has no shadcn-svelte Dialog installed; its one dialog is native), with the file, the check, the refusals, the rows that pass and the commit in one place; the device import is a header action of the Devices screen beside Register a device, for `Device.Create` holders; the mapping import sits in the Topology screen's desk code panel, shown once a desk is selected, for `DeskCodeMapping.Create` holders, and imports for the whole site (the dialog says so). Alternative: a header action on the Topology screen, easier to find. (2) The file is checked in the browser before anything is sent: a `.csv` name with a CSV-like or empty type (Windows browsers name it as Excel's), not empty, at most 1 MB; the server checks again. (3) Wording: Check the file (the preview), Register N devices and Create N mappings (the commit), so the user reads what the step does. (4) Refusals show the row, the column as its number and spreadsheet letter (`12 (L)`, as a spreadsheet shows the file), or Whole row for none (and for a column 0, ARV-155 N5), and the reason; the server's fixed texts are translated by an exact match against a table of the CSV import, device and topology texts (`src/lib/core/csvImports.ts`), and a text the table does not hold is shown as the server wrote it, so a changed server text reads in English until the table follows. Alternative: error codes in the answer. (5) At most 500 rows that pass are listed (a mapping file may pass 10,000), with a note; the summary gives every count. (6) The preview's file SHA-256 is shown, to match the audit entry; the preview token is never shown, and the expiry is shown as a time in the reader's language from the device's clock. (7) A commit refused for any reason but a busy server (429) drops the preview, so the file is checked again before another commit. (8) New credentials: shown in the dialog only, in a table with Copy per device and Copy all as CSV (`code,credential` lines, safe as CSV: codes are upper case letters, digits and hyphens, credentials start `ardk_`); the dialog closes only once I have copied every credential is ticked (Escape and the close button show a reminder; the browser's enforced close on a repeated Escape reopens it; no close while the commit is on its way). Leaving the page (pagehide) or the screen drops them without a prompt: they are lost then, and each device's credential is replaced with Issue a new credential. (9) The devices list refreshes after a device commit; after a mapping commit the dialog closes and the desk's panel reloads. (10) The E2E suite has its own account (`e2e.webcsvimport`, all sites, TOTP), since a TOTP code counts once per account and every other TOTP account belongs to another suite, and its own site per run (`CIW` and a random tail).
- Display texts judged per code point (ARV-158), built 2026-10-12, a defect found while building ARV-153 and confirmed by its security review: `DisplayText.IsClean` stepped over every valid surrogate pair before its control and format test, so the invisible format characters outside the basic plane (the tag characters U+E0001 and U+E0020 to U+E007F, U+1BCA0 to U+1BCA3, U+1D173 to U+1D17A) passed every text it guards. Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) One pass with `Rune.DecodeFromUtf16`: a lone surrogate is refused first (a high surrogate at the end included), then any control or format character per code point (`Rune.IsControl`, category Format); line feeds and carriage returns only where the text allows line breaks, as before; in the basic plane a rune and a char are judged alike, so every text without a supplementary character keeps its result. (2) A change for people: such a character is now refused with the field's existing fixed text in every consumer (alert rules, displays, devices, user display names, integration clients, outbound endpoints, report schedules, calendar reasons, validation texts, alert notes, sensing zone names, the email sender name). That includes the subdivision flag emoji (England, Scotland, Wales), which are tag sequences; every other emoji is still taken (country flags, keycaps, skin tones), and sequences joined by a zero-width joiner stay refused as before, except in user display names, which drop the joiner and non-joiner before the check. (3) No migration: stored rows are not rewritten; a row holding such a character (none is expected, the pilot has not started) keeps it until it is edited, when it must be cleaned, and a sensing or health batch whose queue zone name holds one is no longer archived, as for a format character of the basic plane. (4) Database parity: the CHECK classes of scripts 0020 (alert rule name, scope zones, escalation contact) and 0022 (alert notes) list basic plane characters only, and scripts are immutable; parity would need a new script, left to ARV-157 or a later story; the entity and service checks are the control. (5) Per-character checks outside `DisplayText` are reported and left as they are, since none guards a stored display text with this gap: `AmanPull` and `SvcAcrisPull` strip control characters from `outbound_endpoint.last_status`, which holds texts Ariva builds (every control character is in the basic plane, so a check per UTF-16 unit is complete); `Email.Clean` drops every surrogate; `CsvImportReader` refuses control characters only and leaves format characters to the registration and mapping services (a new integration test proves a model cell with U+E0041 is refused through the registration); `ZoneHealthCriteria` filters a query; the simulator's observer settings are configuration of an emulator. (6) Found, not changed here: topology names (airport, terminal, level, checkpoint, desk, zone, line, zone profile) and site names are trimmed and bounded only (`TopologyCodes.RequireName`, `Site`, `SiteConfigurationRules.IsName`), with no control or format check in any plane; a follow-up story is proposed rather than widening this one, since it changes what the topology, site and site configuration import endpoints accept. No schema change.
- Topology and site names judged per code point (ARV-159), built 2026-10-12, found while building ARV-158 and confirmed by its security review: site and topology names (sites, airports, terminals, levels, checkpoints, desks, zone profiles, zones, lines) were only trimmed and bounded, so a right-to-left override that reorders what operators read was accepted, while Ingest and the archive refused the same name in sensing data. Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) One rule, not a copy: `TopologyCodes.IsName` (1 to 200 characters after trimming, then `DisplayText.IsClean`), applied by `TopologyCodes.RequireName` (every topology and zone profile entity), by `Site`'s constructor and `Rename` (which so also gain the 200 bound the request already had) and by `SiteConfigurationRules.IsName` (the import). (2) The new refusals, each a fixed text that never repeats the name: the topology and zone profile endpoints (airports, terminals, levels, checkpoints, desks, drafts, zones, lines, creates and renames) answer 400 "A name holds no control characters, invisible format characters or broken characters." (`TopologyErrors.UnsafeName`); the site endpoints answer 400 "The site name is at most 200 characters, without control, invisible or broken characters." (`AdministrationErrors.InvalidSiteName`; a site outside the caller's reach still answers 404 first); the site configuration import refuses the bundle with its part's text (`InvalidTopology`, `InvalidZoneProfile`, `InvalidDevices` for a device's queue zone). (3) Arabic and every other script, their combining marks, digits and emoji are names; the zero-width joiner and non-joiner are not, unlike in user display names (which drop them before the check), because a zone name must be one the sensing path takes: a Persian name written with a non-joiner is saved without it. (4) No migration: names stored before are kept; an edit of such a record (a rename, an airport or level update) needs a clean name; a new zone profile draft copies stored zone and line names as they are, so they can be renamed in the draft (the draft's own name follows the rule: give a new one when the published name breaks it); the export of a site holding such a name gives a bundle the import refuses until it is renamed. (5) The pre-pilot check is a one-shot command of Ariva.Api.Main, `--check-stored-names`, like `--migrate`: it reads the covered names through the hosts' runtime login (which reads these tables already) with `default_transaction_read_only` and in a READ ONLY transaction that is rolled back, one constant SELECT, judges them with the same rule and prints the failures escaped (exit code 0, 3 when some fail, 1 on failure). Neither existing read-only path fitted: the validation reader login may read the shadow nowcast and nothing else (script 0049, CWE-269), so it was not widened, and a script under scripts/ would have needed a login of its own and a second copy of the rule in another language. (6) Archive skips are a metric rather than a health-check alarm: `ariva.sensing.archive_skipped` (meter `Ariva.Sensing`, exported by every host) counts skipped sensing batches, sensing events and device health reports by `kind`, with no site or zone label (the skipped data failed the checks, and its values would make unbounded label sets); the health archive's warning no longer counts a report repeated in the same list as skipped. (7) No database CHECK on these names here: the scripts are immutable, and parity with the rule would take a new script, left to ARV-157 or a later story. No schema change.
- Segment intersection independent of point order (ARV-161), built 2026-10-12, found by `FormulaPropertyTests` (CsCheck seed `cCcbgdLcNjA6`) in the ARV-159 story gate and confirmed by its security review: `Geometry.SegmentsIntersect` took an orientation as collinear only when the cross product was exactly 0 (below 1e-12 m2), so at an exact touch or collinear overlap the order and direction of the points decided the answer. Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner (formulas F4 and F22; wiki 07): (1) An exact touch is a touch, whatever the order: the predicate answers true when an end of either segment lies on the other segment, in all eight orders and directions of its arguments, so `IsSimplePolygon` refuses a zone vertex on an edge that is not its own and `Overlaps` counts a footprint that touches its zone as reaching it, in any vertex order. Why: it is the predicate's own contract (closed segments, touching included) and `Overlaps`' (any shared boundary point), as `ContainsPoint` counts its boundary; a zone that touches itself has no well-defined inside for the ray casting and area rules, so refusing it is the safe side; a footprint that touches its zone reaches it at a point, which changes nothing a sensor counts. (2) The existing 1 cm `Tolerance` decides a touch: an end closer than 1 cm to the other segment touches it (the class's rule that a point that close to a segment lies on it; strict, as two vertices closer than 1 cm are one). It is compared as a distance in metres: `DistanceToSegment` for a touch, and for a side the cross product against Tolerance times the segment's length, never a cross product (a length squared) against a length. Consequences: a zone vertex 1 to 9 mm beside an edge that is not its own, simple before, is refused when the zone is added, moved or imported; a footprint within 1 cm of its zone now reaches it. (3) Order independence by construction rather than by tolerance alone: before any arithmetic each segment's ends are sorted by x then y (negative zero made zero), then the two segments, so the eight orders compute with the same numbers bit for bit. Tolerance alone would still leave orders rounding differently at exactly 1 cm. An end within 1 cm of the other's line but beyond its end has side 0 and gives no crossing; segments that cross then always have a touch, because the part between that end and the crossing passes within 1 cm of the other segment's end, so no crossing is lost. (4) The neighbour fold-back check of `IsSimplePolygon` (an exactly zero cross product of two neighbouring edges) is unchanged: it was already order-independent (swapping the neighbours negates the cross product exactly), and when two neighbours fold back, the far end of the shorter lies on the longer and starts the next edge, which is not a neighbour of the longer once the ring has four vertices (three that fold have no area), so the predicate refuses that fold too, within 1 cm.
