(5) Tests: the segment properties run at least 10,000 cases in every build (`Math.Max(10_000, Iter)`), the seed is kept as a fixed case beside the property and its points as a row of `GeometryTests`, and a property compares the answer on a 10 cm grid with exact integer arithmetic. (6) F4's future track-crossing code uses F4's own side-of-line rule (a point exactly on the line keeps the previous side; a touch without passing is not a crossing), not this predicate. (7) No migration: published versions are immutable, and a new draft copies their zones as they are; publication does not re-check simplicity, so a stored zone with such a vertex stays publishable until it is moved; the site configuration export of such a zone gives a bundle the import refuses. The seeds (DMO, NBJ-BC1, AUH Terminal A) and the reference scenario are unchanged; no schema, API or golden replay change.
- Legacy names and outlines blocked at publication, and the pre-pilot check widened (ARV-162), built 2026-10-12, from the ARV-159 security review (R1, R2) and ARV-161 (decisions point 7). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) `ZoneProfile.Validate`, and so publication, refuses a profile, zone or line name the name rule refuses and a zone outline `Geometry.IsSimplePolygon` refuses (ARV-161's rule: a vertex within 1 cm of an edge it does not end). A draft still copies zones and lines as stored, so they are fixed in the draft. (2) Such a name is shown escaped in every problem and edit message (`DisplayText.Escape`, moved from the check into Ariva.Core so both use one escape; `ZoneProfile.Label` shows a clean name as it is unless it holds a backslash, so a clean name that spells an escape cannot pass for an escaped one; security review recommendation 2), never by its position: the escaped form identifies the zone exactly. (3) Lines keep no rename: a legacy line is removed and added again (the problem says so); the existing endpoints do it, and a line's identity is its name in the geometry hash anyway. (4) Every export can be imported: the site configuration export answers 409 with a fixed text naming the kind of record (`TopologyNamesNotImportable`, `ProfileNotImportable`, `TextsNotImportable`, and since the security review (R1) `FloorPlanNamesNotImportable`) when a stored topology name, the published profile's name, a zone or line name or a zone outline, a floor plan file name (stored before ARV-153, which judged it per UTF-16 unit; one rule now, `FloorPlan.IsSafeFileName`, called by the upload, the export and the check), or a device model, alert rule name or display text would be refused by the import, instead of giving a bundle the import refuses; a published version from before ARV-161 with a touching outline is so exported only after a corrected version is published. This replaces the export part of ARV-159 point (4). (5) `--check-stored-names` stays one command, one constant statement streamed in a read-only session and transaction, and now also reads every stored column the display text rule guards, each by its entity's own rule (one line; line breaks in alert and calibration notes; a person's name, which may hold the zero-width joiner and non-joiner; an alert rule's list of zones; a display's labels and messages in their JSON, refused as unreadable when the JSON does not read), every zone outline and every floor plan file name. A personal text (a user's display name, an escalation contact, an alert or calibration note, a manual count or desk observation reason) is never printed: its finding names only the offending code points and their positions (`DisplayText.Offenders`, the walk `IsClean` itself uses; CWE-532), and the output must not be pasted into tickets or CI logs. Not read: the archives, replay runs, audit entries, emails, alerts' copies of rule and zone names and validation campaigns' copies of zone, line and queue zone names (evidence nobody can rename), and validation result revisions, which only the results service may name (ValidationResultsExposureTests); the email sender name is configuration, refused at host start (`EmailSettings.Problems`). Each finding carries a state (live, deleted, disabled, the profile's status or the alert's state) instead of a deleted flag. No schema change.
- Producing outside the rider (ARV-166, ARV-167), on the owner's decision of 2026-10-13 (the next entry, point 1): MassTransit 8.5's Kafka rider disposes a topic's shared producer when any send on it fails or is cancelled, and the sends still outstanding on it are then never reported, so a produce could wait for ever (ADR-0018, note of 2026-10-13; evidence in backlog/progress.md). ARV-166 ships the bounded mitigation: every awaited produce ends within 45 seconds, the caller's cancellation never reaches the rider, a relay pass produces for at most 60 seconds, and the dead-letter loops end when their endpoint stops and leave the message unacknowledged. ARV-167 then gives Ariva its own Confluent producer per host for sends and writes the ADR-0018 amendment and the CLAUDE.md and kafka-streaming skill updates. Until it ships, a failed send still strands the sends outstanding on its topic's producer (they fail at the 45 second bound and are sent again). Not changed by ARV-167, since it is in the rider's consumer: a record left unacknowledged when a host stops while its dead letter is refused can be committed past by a later record of its partition (overview, Producing; wiki 10 section 4.5a says how to find and replay it).
- Owner decisions (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-13, in the session), resolving open points: (1) Kafka sends (ARV-166): bound the damage now (ARV-166), then Ariva owns its sends with one Confluent producer per host behind ISvcMessageBus, keeping MassTransit's rider for consuming, with an ADR-0018 amendment (ARV-167). (2) The England, Scotland and Wales flag emoji are allowed: only the three well-formed RGI subdivision sequences, every other tag character still refused (ARV-169, reversing ARV-158 point 2). (3) A touch is within 1 cm, for zone outlines and device footprint reach (ARV-161 points 1 and 2 accepted). (4) Zone outlines have a minimum width of 1 cm (ARV-170). (5) The desk code mapping CSV import's commit asks for step-up; single mapping changes do not (ARV-155). (6) A Degraded message bus raises an alert; readiness does not fail on it (ARV-125). (7) After a site import, a device's source networks and certificate pin are taken from the site's commissioning as-built pack (its network or IP plan and certificate register; wiki 11 section 2d). (8) The Testcontainers.Kafka start race found in ARV-156 is not reported upstream; the shared builder's workaround stays. (9) The allowlist entries for the raw bidirectional characters in scripts 0020 and 0022 (ARV-157) are approved for exactly those lines and SEC-130. (10) ARV-166's bounded stop makes a narrow message skip reachable (a dead letter Kafka refuses at a stop while commits still succeed, for example a missing dead-letter topic or permission during a rolling deploy): accepted for ARV-166, since a frozen outbox is the larger risk, and closed before the pilot by ARV-171 (a refused dead letter written durably first, as an outbox row).
- Repeated sensing batches in the queue engine (ARV-168), built 2026-10-13, a defect found while finishing ARV-166: the engine applied a resent interval-count batch again. These points are proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner (formulas F5; sensor-adapters.md; cwe-controls.md CWE-501): (1) The window: a batch is remembered until the zone's watermark is a lateness past its latest event (about three minutes after receipt with the defaults), one lateness more than strictly needed, so that a resend whose times Ingest corrected differently (another replica's clock estimate) by up to the lateness is still dropped, and a resend between the two ends is dropped rather than counted as late (which would ask for a needless recomputation). Alternative: exactly the watermark (the batch's latest event), which halves the memory. (2) The key is the device code and the batch id within the zone, not the kind (Ingest's id already includes the kind) and not the device id (the engine works by device code throughout: liveness, tracks). The archive's own check stays by batch id alone (`sensing_batch`), which a device cannot exploit since the device id is part of the id; only a producer writing to Kafka directly could, which the broker's ACLs are for (ARV-007). (3) The bounds: 20,000 keys per zone (as the 20,000 resolved tracks) and 1,000 per device, settings of the zone (`MaxRememberedBatches`, `MaxRememberedBatchesPerDevice`, not in `Stream:` configuration), so they enter the replay's settings hash (a replay's genesis changes; its outputs do not). Beyond a bound the key whose window ends first is forgotten, counted as `ForgottenBatches`; a resend of it counts again. (4) A dropped resend still says its device was heard and moves the receive clock (it is exactly a health report at its receive time), since the push did arrive; the alternative, dropping it before liveness, would match a replay of the archive (which never stores the resend) where no health report was archived for it. (5) Every kind is dropped, not only interval counts: a vendor's anonymous crossings had the same defect, and occupancy, track samples and desk zone readings repeat harmlessly. Tracked crossings keep the track rule as well (first entry counts), since it covers more than resends. (6) Snapshot version 10; a version 9 snapshot restores remembering nothing, so within one window of the upgrade a resend of a batch applied before it counts again. (7) Not fixed here, for the coordinator: two interval readings that end at the same instant (the entry and exit lines of one T1 push) are applied one after the other, and the second is spread over nothing, so its counts land in the next minute (totals right, the later line's minutes shifted; backlog/progress.md, ARV-168).
- Refused dead letter kept durably at a stop (ARV-171), built 2026-10-13 on the owner's decision of 2026-10-13 (the ARV-166 security review, condition C1: accept for ARV-166, close before the pilot): the skip recorded with ARV-166 above is closed. A dead letter Kafka has not taken when its endpoint stops is first written as an outbox row for the dead-letter topic and only then does the record complete, so MassTransit's checkpointer can no longer commit past a record that was neither processed nor dead-lettered while the database is there; with the database away as well, ARV-166's path stays (the record left unacknowledged, the warning naming it for a replay, wiki 10 section 4.5a). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) at a stop the letter goes to the outbox without a last Kafka attempt, so the stop is not held for the 45 second produce bound; (2) the outbox write is bounded at 10 seconds; (3) every host registers the dead-letter producer of every topic, since any host can lead the relay.
- Interval readings ending at the same instant (ARV-173), built 2026-10-13, a defect found while building ARV-168 (its point 7): the second of two interval readings ending at one instant (the entry and exit lines of one T1 push) was spread over nothing, so its people landed at the instant itself, the next minute (each minute's exits counted a minute late; totals right). Decided by the lead (2026-10-13, technical, open to the owner's overrule): (A) the instant's entry-line readings are applied before its other interval readings by the zone's geometry, then by content, for interval readings only (crossings, occupancy and every other kind keep their order, so no locked replay can move), so that a zone's waits never depend on what an administrator named its lines; (B) the ARV-168 review's recommendation 2 (remember only interval and crossing batches) is considered and declined: the memory saved is small at pilot scale, and resent track and occupancy batches behind the watermark would again count as late events and ask for recomputations of final bins; `RecentBatches` stays as ARV-168 left it. Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner (formulas F5; sensor-adapters.md): (1) The rule: an interval reading ending at T is spread over the part of it after the last event processed before T's instant and after the watermark, so no event of its own instant narrows it, whatever the order of the readings in a push, across pushes or topics; the same principle as ARV-148's ordering for occupancy, made independent of order. Not taken: a time-ordered merge of an instant's readings (exact interleaving of entries and exits), which needs state across a step that fills inside the instant (a snapshot field) and bounded work. (2) A counted exit whose FIFO partner entered after it is unmatched (counted as an exit, no wait; the person stays held), which with (A) gives exactly the pairs time order gives; without it, spreading exits inside the minute would have turned an exit that comes before the minute's entries into a negative wait and a Rejected person. (3) An entrant is counted in the minute its reading places it, while the entry time FIFO uses never goes before a person already held (only a second entry-line reading of the same instant, longer than a minute, meets this), so a zone's minute entries always equal its entry lines' minute counts. (4) A step that fills inside an instant leaves the rest of the instant's readings over the tick before it, still in their own last minute; within the minute their positions differ from an unsplit step (a step holds 100,000 records), and spread points are capped one tick before the reading's end (a span of a tick would otherwise round its last person up to the end). (5) No snapshot field or version: the floor of an instant is rebuilt from the persisted cursor between steps, and inside a split step the watermark dominates it. (6) Replays of archived interval data recorded before the fix do not reproduce their output hash after it; the manifest names the engine build, and finals change only through a recorded recomputation (F6). Every locked replay (golden 9303, overflow, desk zones, AUH-TA 9304) uses the canonical dialect, which sends no interval readings: their output heads were checked identical before and after. (7) Residual, pre-existing, not fixed: an Out count on an entry line resolves the latest anonymous entrant, which may be one placed later in the same minute by the same reading (resolved at its entry time).
- Ariva owns its Kafka sends (ARV-167), built 2026-10-13 on the owner's decision of 2026-10-13 (point 1 of the owner decisions above): each host has one Confluent producer of its own (`KafkaProduce`) behind `ISvcMessageBus`, built at start, flushed for at most 5 seconds at stop and disposed only with the host; the relay, Ingest's direct publish and every dead letter go through it, and MassTransit's rider only consumes (ADR-0018, note of 2026-10-13 for ARV-167; overview, Producing). It removes the residual recorded with ARV-166 above: a send takes no cancellation token, so neither a failed nor a cancelled send can dispose the producer and strand the others (StuckProduceTests: both sends now end by their own delivery report). ARV-171's point 3 is moot: no host registers any producer with the rider, and the one producer sends to every topic Ariva produces, whichever host leads the relay. The wire format is the rider's (contract test against a rider producer). Decided by the lead (2026-10-13, technical, open to the owner's overrule): (A) A fatal producer error recreates the producer in place, at most once per `Kafka:Producer:RecreateSeconds` (30, configurable from 5 to 3600, validated at start) counted from the failed client's creation; sends in the gap fail at once; each fatal error and recreation is logged Critical (at most one of each per client) and counted on the `Ariva.Kafka` meter (`ariva.kafka.producer_fatal`) for ARV-125's alerts; the swap is race free (a send reads the current client once; the replaced client's own reports end their own waiters, its bounded retirement off the send path fails the rest with `Local_Destroy`, senders resend); a failed or cancelled send still never disposes or recreates the producer; no readiness change, consistent with the owner's ARV-125 decision. Why: Main also serves the screens, so stopping the host or failing liveness over a Kafka ACL mistake would crash loop the UI while the outbox can hold the events. (B) The relay waits for the producer (started, not in that gap) plus the database, no longer for MassTransit's bus, which sending does not need; one endpoint that cannot consume no longer stops the outbox of its host. Consequence: during a broker outage the relay's passes run, each ending at its first failed row, which backs off (up to 5 minutes), so after a long outage a key can wait up to its backoff; a host without consumers (Ingest) already relayed this way. (C) The flush order is accepted as built: hosted services a host registers before its messaging stop after the flush, their late sends work until disposal, and `Local_Destroy` fails the rest for their senders to resend (overview, Producing). (D) StuckProduceTests reads each send's delivery report from its send span, timed from that send's own produce against the 30 second message timeout plus a 30 second margin of the test's own, because the earlier assertion (the wait ended by a `KafkaException`, not the 45 second bound) depended on a margin of about 8 seconds (B's report came 36.7 and 38.5 seconds after its produce in two runs); no production bound changed. (E) No change for the dropped headers: Kafka between AMAN and Ariva is inbound only (`aman.feed.*`, docs/architecture/integration.md), so no reader outside Ariva consumes Ariva's topics. Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) The producer's queue is bounded for every host by the same constants, `queue.buffering.max.kbytes` 32768 and `queue.buffering.max.messages` 10,000, sized to the smallest pod memory limit (750 Mi; about 60 MB in the worst case, arithmetic in the overview); a full queue refuses at once, which Ingest answers 503. Alternative: a setting per host. (2) The flush at stop waits at most 5 seconds; nothing acknowledged depends on it (every caller awaits its own report). (3) The rider's `ConversationId`, `DestinationAddress` and `SourceAddress` headers are no longer written, since nothing in Ariva reads them; `MessageId`, Ariva's four headers and, when traced, `MT-Activity-Id` (trace context, from Ariva's own `Ariva.Kafka` activity source) are written as before. (4) Superseded by the lead's (A). (5) The producer refuses a topic that is neither Ariva's own nor a dead-letter topic (AMAN's included), and the relay refuses an event row whose topic is not its event type's topic (only that key waits). (6) Superseded by the lead's (B). The matching wording for CLAUDE.md rule 4 and the kafka-streaming skill is drafted for the owner and not changed by the implementation.
- Owner decisions (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-14, in the session), resolving open points: (1) refused dead letter kept at a stop (ARV-171): points 1 and 2 accepted (at a stop the letter goes straight to the outbox, without a last Kafka attempt that could hold the stop for the 45 second produce bound; the outbox write is bounded at 10 seconds); point 3 (every host registering every dead-letter producer) is moot since ARV-167, where no host registers a producer on the rider and one producer per host sends to any dead-letter topic. (2) Interval readings ending together (ARV-173): the lead's decisions (A) (an instant's entry-line interval readings first, by the zone's geometry, never by line names) and (B) (recommendation 2 of the ARV-168 review declined) confirmed. (3) Ariva's own Kafka producer (ARV-167): the wording of CLAUDE.md rule 4, Platform/Backplane/CLAUDE.md (Messaging and streaming), the kafka-streaming skill and the stream-engineer agent approved as drafted (with the three corrections from the security review), and applied the same day.
- Broker outage during an offset commit (ARV-165), built 2026-10-14, found in ARV-156 and proven again by the ARV-165 baseline (backlog/progress.md): MassTransit's consumer, held by an offset commit while the broker is away, kept its endpoint Ready, the bus check Healthy and the host's stop waiting for 45 seconds; and a host stopped while an endpoint was between restarts left a consumer running that held its partition from the next host (ARV-166's residual (b)). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) Readiness gets a `kafka` check of its own: a metadata request through the producer's handle within 3 seconds, failure status Degraded (consistent with the owner's ARV-125 decision: an outage raises the alert, readiness answers 200), one probe at a time and its result reused for a second. Alternative: folding a broker signal into MassTransit's bus check, which would need MassTransit internals. (2) The bus's stop wait is the host's shutdown timeout less 15 seconds (at least 5), derived rather than configured, so ARV-175's longer shutdown timeout gives the bus the time it adds. (3) Endpoints the bus's stop skipped are stopped after it, within 5 seconds; one still held by its commit ends with the process. (4) The relay does not count a broker or producer outage against a row (librdkafka's message timeout, all brokers down, a transport error, a full queue, the producer's or the pass's bound, the producer's fatal gap, a retired or stopping client, a swept report): the row is due again after `Kafka:Outbox:BrokerRetrySeconds` (5, from 1 to 30, validated at start), so every key resumes within about 25 seconds of the broker's return; a row's own failure keeps the exponential backoff and the attempt count. Alternative: resetting the waiting rows' next attempt at the first successful send after failures, which needs a write per key on recovery. (5) The producer's recreation catches every exception but running out of memory, logs Critical and tries again after the interval; a sweep every 45 seconds ends a waiter whose report is more than 90 seconds late (`Local_TimedOut`, logged as an error). Decided by the lead (2026-10-14, technical, open to the owner's overrule): (A) The shutdown budget comes into this story, since point (2) took 15 of the default 30 seconds and the clean stop during a message's retries (up to 3 x 5 seconds and the 10 second keep, 25 seconds) no longer fitted: every API pod gets `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` 60 (all five hosts, not only the consumers Main, Stream and Integration: Ingest and Cronz lead the relay as often as any host, every host flushes its producer, one validated budget is simpler, and a grace period is only a maximum), every host a shutdown timeout of 50 seconds (`Hosting:ShutdownSeconds`), so the bus gets 35; a host refuses to start unless the bus's share covers the retry schedule (`RetryCount` times `RetryMaxMilliseconds`) and the keep, and the shutdown ends 5 seconds before the grace period, which the chart passes to the host (`Hosting__GracePeriodSeconds`); chart render tests assert both. ARV-175 (Phase 3) keeps its other two items. (B) The relay during an outage is accepted: each attempt holds the pass's transaction, its claimed rows (`FOR UPDATE`) and the leader's advisory lock for up to the 30 second message timeout, every 5 seconds. Harmless because new outbox rows are inserts under new ids (the domain outbox, the stream's overflow rows) or `ON CONFLICT (id) DO NOTHING`, which does not wait on a locked row (a kept dead letter); nothing but the relay updates or deletes outbox rows; the other hosts' relays skip the pass (`pg_try_advisory_xact_lock`) instead of waiting; readers (the runbook's queries, health) are plain reads, which row locks do not block; the leader's housekeeping (old sent rows and inbox entries, hourly) runs after the sends, just before the commit, so its delete locks are not held through a send. It costs one database connection held per leader and a vacuum horizon 30 seconds older, both negligible. If a database sets `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` below 30 seconds (none of Ariva's does), the pass's connection is ended instead: the pass fails, is logged and is tried again 5 seconds later.
- Phase 1 scope (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-14, in the session): low-severity follow-ups from the security reviews leave Phase 1 for a new Phase 3 (backlog/prd-phase3.json, built after the pilot go-live gate ARV-138 unless the owner pulls a story back): ARV-147 (a web request kept to the account that started it; low, the server still authorises and audits it), ARV-154 (creates take the site lock during an import; not a security gap today), ARV-155 (CSV import lock and upload contention, tokens out of test messages, absent optional columns, and the bulk mapping commit's step-up decided on 2026-10-13), ARV-160 (story gate scope), ARV-163 (linear rather than timed regular expressions; low, fails closed), ARV-164 (test determinism), ARV-169 (subdivision flag emoji), ARV-170 (1 cm minimum zone width) and ARV-175 (dead-letter text PostgreSQL accepts, a per-topic relay hold, the shutdown budget; non-blocking in the ARV-171 review). Phase 1 keeps the pre-pilot fixes ARV-165 (broker outage during an offset commit), ARV-174 (resent receipt-timed crossings counted once, a double count the validation campaigns would see) and ARV-176 (a missing consumed topic stalling the AMAN feeds), with the go-live stories. Rule from now on: a review finding becomes a Phase 1 story only when it blocks or is needed before the pilot; anything else goes to Phase 3.
- Consumed topics that do not exist (ARV-176), built 2026-10-14 from the ARV-167 security review: one missing consumed topic faulted MassTransit's bus start and stopped every consumer of the host for good (proven by `MissingTopicTests`; overview section 5; ADR-0018 note of 2026-10-14). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) Topic endpoints are connected to the rider after the bus has started, each once its topic exists (`KafkaConsumerEndpoints`), instead of being configured with the bus; the bus's start is now waited for (`WaitUntilStarted`, at most 15 seconds, lowered from 60 in the re-review so that the start budget holds even when it times out). Alternatives considered: registering only the topics that exist when the bus is configured (the bus is built before any wait can see the topics) and a bus of its own for AMAN's feed (more machinery, and an Ariva topic deleted by mistake would still stop a whole bus). (2) A host's start waits (at most `StartWaitSeconds`, 60, lowered from 300 in the security review) for the consumed Ariva topics, which the topics job creates, and never for AMAN's feed topics, which AMAN creates (before, Ariva.Api.Integration held its start up to 300 seconds for a missing AMAN topic), then at most 30 seconds for the connected consumers to be ready. The start holds the web server, so the host refuses to start unless the two waits and a 30 second margin (the bus's start, at most 15 seconds, and a metadata request's overrun, at most 12) fit in the pod's startup allowance (`Hosting:StartupAllowanceSeconds`, 130, which the render test checks against the probe); 300 seconds would have had a pod restarted by its probe in a loop while it waited. The allowance is the probe's worst case, `initialDelaySeconds` plus `periodSeconds` times (`failureThreshold` less 1), because the kubelet's probe ticks run from before the container starts; the first build took the best case (10 + 15 x 8 = 130 against a real 115), so each API startup probe's `failureThreshold` went from 8 to 9 (10 + 15 x (9 - 1) = 130 at the earliest), keeping the value and the defaults (the re-review's R4). Since a topic missing at start is connected when it appears, the wait only spares a fresh cluster a host that starts before its topics. (3) A missing topic is looked for every 10 seconds (`Kafka:Consumers:TopicWatchSeconds`, 1 to 300, validated at start). (4) The signal is a gauge, `ariva.kafka.consumed_topic_missing` (1 per missing consumed topic, tags `topic` and `group`), plus a Warning log naming the topic; readiness does not change, since a missing external topic is a configuration matter for the alert, not a reason to take the host out of service. While Kafka cannot be read the host logs "Kafka not reachable" once, never "does not exist", and the gauge keeps the state it last read; a topic never read has no value (the `kafka` readiness check, Degraded, is the outage's signal). A topic that exists but whose consumer's connect failed reads 0 (the failure is logged with its group and topic and retried at each check). (5) Before go-live the four `aman.feed.*.v1` topics must exist (wiki 08 section 10, checklist row 16), the e-gate topic included at a site without e-gates.
- Resent receipt-timed crossings and re-stamped retries (ARV-174), built 2026-10-14, from the ARV-168 security review: a batch id hashed the push body, plus the receipt time for a kind stamped at receipt, so a device that stamps its send time again on a retry, or any resend of a receipt-timed kind, got new ids and was counted again. Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner (docs/architecture/sensor-adapters.md, formulas F5, cwe-controls.md CWE-501): (1) A kind whose events carry their own times is identified by the device, the kind, the vendor's package ids and its events as sent, in every dialect (the criterion named the canonical push; Xovis and declarative sections with a time path follow the same rule). Equal ids need the same package ids and the same events to the precision of the device's clock, which makes them one report only when the events have an identity of their own (a track id, a line's interval from one device, a zone's reading at a time); corrected after the security review (R1): anonymous crossings have none, see point (7). Whether Xovis keeps `package_info.id` on a retry is still to confirm with Xovis (sensor-adapters.md). (2) The package ids stay part of that id, so a device that gives a retry a new package id is counted again (new data is assumed, the safe side). (3) The events are compared as a set with multiplicity, in any order. (4) A kind stamped at receipt keeps the body in its id, with the package ids in place of the receipt time, rather than its events without times: a restarted counter repeating a package number then merges only when the whole message is byte-identical; the cost is that a send time in such a body (`sentTime`), stamped again on a retry, defeats recognition. Without a package such a kind keeps its earlier id (body and receipt time), so any resend is counted again: the conformance kit warns about crossings in that case (an xUnit warning, not a failure, since a vendor format may allow nothing better), and the mapping guidance states it. Not taken: listing the warning in `GET api/v1/admin/devices/mappings` or logging it at start-up. (5) Upgrade: ids of every kind with times change, so a resend straddling the upgrade is counted again once; archived batches keep their ids (replays read them). The locked replays (golden 9303, overflow, AUH-TA) are unchanged; the desk zone replay's head moves with the same outputs, 4,298 desk readings of S-18 to S-20 being listed in another order, since a replay orders records of one receive time and kind by batch id. (6) Residual, for Phase 3 (no shipped mapping stamps crossings at receipt): the queue engine remembers a batch until its events are a lateness behind the watermark, so a message stamped at receipt and resent later than about three minutes is applied again live (its events at the new receipt) while the archive keeps it once, and live and replayed counts then differ. (7) After the security review (R1, 2026-10-14): in a push without a package whose crossings include one without a track id, the crossings' id also hashes the device's send time, so two people crossing one line in the same second, sent as two messages, are not merged unless their send times are equal at the device's precision; a retry of such a push that stamps its send time again is counted again (the state before ARV-174). When such a push has no send time either, the receipt time is used, not the review's suggested marker for its absence: with a marker two genuine pushes without `sentUtc` would still merge, while the receipt time never merges them and counts such a resend again, the side R1 prefers. Two messages with the same anonymous crossings and the same send time are still merged, as before ARV-174 (byte-identical messages). The conformance kit also warns about declarative crossings with a time but neither a track id nor a package, and wiki 09 asks vendors to send a `packageId` or track ids with anonymous crossings. (8) Interval counts keep the events rule: one report per line and interval per device; a gateway forwarding two sensors counting one line under one credential would merge identical reports, so each sensor is registered as a device of its own (no real case found to change the id). (9) Package ids are sorted before hashing (review rec 2), so the same envelopes in another order (Xovis) are the same identity.
- System health metrics (ARV-125a), built 2026-10-14, split from ARV-125 (the rules and dashboards are ARV-125b; owner decision 2026-10-14: SigNoz JSON in ConfigMaps an operator imports; lead decisions 2026-10-14: a gauge for a consumer that cannot connect, external metrics only for disk and the ingress certificate, the AMAN feed alert on `ariva.border.feed.records` not growing for T_stale). Every meter Ariva creates is now exported (Ariva.Border, Ariva.Flights and Ariva.Integration were not), with a reflection test and a source scan; the readiness body says "Degraded" while a check is Degraded (still 200, no check named). Proposed by the implementation (the devops engineer), pending the owner (wiki 10 section 2a): (1) The readiness checks are reported one by one as `ariva.health.status` (0 Healthy, 1 Degraded, 2 Unhealthy, tag `check`) by each host's health check publisher, which runs the checks tagged `ready` every 15 s, besides the kubelet's probes; a host whose last run is older than 60 s reports nothing. (2) Totals one replica computes are reported only within two sweep intervals of its sweep (devices 30 s, feeds 120 s), so other replicas and hosts report nothing rather than zeros or stale copies; copies of a shared figure (device and feed totals, the outbox) are combined by their largest value, the stream's lag by its sum (instances own different partitions). (3) Every relay measures the outbox (one statement on script 0011's partial index, every 30 s, reported for 90 s), not only the leader, so the backlog is known whichever host leads and while the producer is down; no new script. (4) The stream's lag is the high watermark the consumer last fetched less the next offset it reads, every checkpoint interval, with no broker call; while reading is paused (a zone at its bound with the database away) the fetched watermark does not move, so the lag stays flat rather than growing, and the database alert covers that case. (5) A dead letter is counted once by how it ended (`sent`, `kept`, `failed`); a refused attempt that is retried is not counted; a topic that is not a constant or a group that is not an Ariva group reads `unknown`. (6) Certificate expiry covers the Data Protection certificate in use and Ingest's MQTT server certificate, as each pod loaded them at start (a renewed secret shows after the restart); previous Data Protection certificates, device, partner and border-to-airport certificates are not included. (7) The tag keys allowed are `check`, `certificate`, `contract`, `feed`, `feed_kind`, `group`, `kind`, `outcome`, `site` and `topic`; `feed` names a feed by the client's credential (`api-` and the client id) or the outbound endpoint's code, never a person.
- System monitoring alerts and dashboards (ARV-125b), built 2026-10-14 on the owner's decision of 2026-10-14 (target SigNoz; rules and dashboards as SigNoz JSON in ConfigMaps an operator imports) and the lead's (external metrics only for disk and the ingress certificate, in a reviewed list; the AMAN feed rule on `ariva.border.feed.records` not growing for T_stale, off by default; a gauge rule for a consumer that cannot connect). Proposed by the implementation (the devops engineer), pending the owner (wiki 10 sections 1.1 and 3.1, wiki 04): (1) SigNoz v0.146.0, the latest release on Docker Hub on 2026-10-14; alert rules in its `version` v5, `schemaVersion` v2alpha1 shape and dashboards in its v2 (Perses) format, `schemaVersion` v6, checked against SigNoz's source at that tag (its rule examples and validation, its dashboard types and an integration dashboard), not against a running SigNoz; builder queries on the OTel metric names, so no name translation. (2) The operator imports through `POST /api/v2/rules` and `POST /api/v2/dashboards`. The import runs as a SigNoz user kept for it (Editor role), with an API key created for the import and revoked after it. The key is read without echo, passed to curl as a header file through a process substitution (never on a command line), and unset at the end; it is never written to a file (security review R1, CWE-214 and CWE-522). The chart has no Job, no SigNoz address and no key, and a second import creates copies. (3) Thresholds are Helm values substituted into the rule sources at render (`monitoring.alerts.<rule>`, guarded in validate.yaml); channel names in `monitoring.channels`. (4) Host down is one rule per host on the absence of `ariva.health.status` for 5 minutes: a host whose every pod is gone or silent; one pod down is Kubernetes' to restart. (5) "Lag growing for several minutes" is read as more than 1,000 records behind for 5 minutes, and "growing backlog" as the oldest unsent outbox row older than 5 minutes. (6) The ingress certificate rule reads the soonest end among the certificates cert-manager manages in the release's namespaces (`namespaces`, empty for the release's own). The label that carries the namespace is a value, `namespaceLabel`: `namespace` as cert-manager exports it, or `exported_namespace` when the scrape adds its own. The rule is silent where cert-manager is not scraped. The disk rule likewise reads its claims only in `disk.namespaces` (empty: the release's), so a claim of the same name in another namespace is neither read nor named in Ariva's alert (security review recommendation 1). (7) Defaults not given by wiki 10 are Proposed: host down and readiness 5 minutes, Kafka, database and bus 2 minutes, dead letters any in 15 minutes, devices offline above a share of 0.2 for 5 minutes. (8) The section 3 alarms that have no rule yet (sensor degraded, zone Unknown, clock drift, stale AODB feed, unknown AMAN codes, bin maturity backlog, a failed retention job, display stale, a locked client) stay on the dashboards and logs. (9) Every query of an Ariva metric, in the rules and the dashboards, reads its release's `deployment.environment` only: `monitoring.environment`, or the release's `environment` when empty, which must equal the hosts' `Application:Environment`. With that filter, one SigNoz can take several environments without mixing their alerts, and a host down in one environment is not masked by another environment's pods. Rule and dashboard names end with the environment, and each rule carries the label `environment` (security review recommendation 2). A mismatch makes every host down rule fire after the import, which is the check.
- Xovis package ids on retry (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-14, in the session), answering the open point of the ARV-174 entry: Xovis devices keep `package_info.id` when they retry a push, so a Xovis retry gets the batch id of the push it repeats and is counted once (ARV-174). Recorded as the owner's statement; the pilot capture in ARV-132 confirms it on the pilot's firmware.
- Release smoke suite (ARV-128, TC-56), built 2026-10-14 under the owner's freeze on sign-in changes (no sign-in, login, password, TOTP or authentication change until the site identity provider arrives; ARV-121, ARV-134a and ARV-134b parked). Proposed by the implementation (the test engineer), pending the owner (wiki 04 sections 8 and 8.1, wiki 16 section 4): (1) TC-56's answer: the end-to-end suite is never pointed at a deployed environment. Its suites write, attack and load, and need the development accounts only vm-local seeds, so `playwright.config.ts` refuses to load when any target it calls or writes to (the hosts, the web app, Stream, smtp4dev, Redis, Kafka, the database) is not local, naming the target and never its address. A deployed environment gets the read-only release smoke suite (`npm run test:smoke`, `playwright.smoke.config.ts`), at go-live and after each upgrade; a failing check is a reason to roll back. (2) Local means `localhost`, `127.0.0.0/8` or `[::1]` after WHATWG URL parsing; anything else is non-local, including private addresses, `*.localhost`, `localhost.` and IPv4-mapped IPv6 addresses. A second machine on the same network is non-local. (3) The dedicated smoke account needs read-only permissions, and rights come only from the fixed roles, none of which is read-only, so a sixth fixed role, `SmokeReader` ("Smoke check (read only)"), made of existing permissions only: the handler's topology reads, `ZoneProfile.View` and `Search`, `LiveQueue.View` and `Report.View`. Rank 1 with the operational roles, airport-side for desk data (it holds no desk permission), not an alert role, and, holding `Report.View`, a possible recipient of report schedules like every role that reads the report. No new permission, no script, no authentication change. The server does not force named sites on it (it does for the validation observer); the suite checks at every run that the account holds this role alone, named sites including the smoke site, View and Search permissions only and none of `security/critical-actions.json`'s critical permissions. (4) The account signs in through the existing sign-in with its authenticator (every committed environment requires one); the operator keeps its password and its authenticator's base32 secret in the operations secret store as automation secrets, and gives them to the run as environment variables only. (5) A smoke site of its own (for example `SMOKE`) with one published queue zone, a test device (it never pushes, so it shows Offline on that site and counts once in the overall offline share of the monitoring rules) and a display. The alternative, the pilot site, would check real data but puts the test device and display in its device list, uptime and reports. (6) The 11 checks of wiki 04 section 8.1; readiness must be `Healthy` (`Degraded` fails the smoke), and the Ingest clock must be within 5 minutes of the machine running the suite. (7) The guard: every smoke test declares `@read`, `@write`, `@attack` or `@load`; against a non-local target anything but `@read`, or no kind, stops the run before a test starts; a smoke spec may import only `tests/smoke/support`; the smoke client and the browser send GET and HEAD only, plus the sign-in, the web app's session refresh and the sign-outs; the client calls the configured origins only and follows no redirect. (8) Credentials: environment only; Node's fetch, not Playwright's request fixture (its call log prints request headers); errors masked; fields filled through `evaluate` (Playwright 1.63 titles fill steps with their text); the display key in the player's session storage, never in an address; traces, screenshots and videos off; the run fails when a smoke credential, an access token, a device or display key, a bearer credential or an `access_token` value is found in its report or output folder. (9) The live screen reads alerts only with `Alert.View` (it asked `GET /api/v1/alerts` for every `LiveQueue.View` holder; the smoke reader is the first such role without alerts). (10) The suite runs from the operator's machine, not from a pipeline; the first run against a cluster is part of ARV-137 (owner). A release pipeline step with the secrets in the pipeline's secret store, and a server rule that keeps a smoke reader at named sites, are Phase 3 candidates.
- Release smoke suite security review (ARV-128), 2026-10-14: the security-reviewer's FAIL on R1 (CWE-269, CWE-863) is built: the server keeps an account whose only roles are the smoke reader, the validation observer or both at named sites (`RoleCodes.NamedSitesOnly`, `NeedsNamedSites`), refusing with 400 and writing nothing a creation, a site change, a grant or a revoke that would give it every site; the refusal text names no role. This replaces the suite-only check of point (3); the suite still checks the account at every run. Recommendations built the same day, Proposed pending the owner: (11) a report (the daily report, its CSV and email, and the weekly summary) lists alerts only for roles holding `Alert.View`, so the smoke reader's lists none, not even ownerless ones; a caller with another role keeps that role's alerts. (12) A smoke test gets the guarded page and its guards only (no request, browser, context or playwright fixture, by type and at run time), and a smoke spec using `fetch(`, `WebSocket(`, `XMLHttpRequest`, `newContext`, `newPage` or `.request.` stops the config from loading. (13) E2E global setup refuses to start unless every host answers as a vm-local host of this run, a CORS preflight from `http://localhost:51011` (allowed by vm-local's settings alone) for the API hosts and the web app's `/api` proxy and this run's simulator read key for the simulator, so a deployed host behind a port-forward or tunnel on a local port is never written to; the docs say never to run the suites with one open. No host reports its environment anonymously, and adding that was judged a wider change than the check needs. (14) The smoke run's report folder, output folder and JSON file stay inside `playwright-report-smoke`, `test-results-smoke` or `test-results-smoke-runs` of the E2E project. (15) The smoke account's password and authenticator and the device's and display's credentials are rotated together when an operator with access leaves (wiki 04 section 8.1). The owner has not yet decided whether the `SmokeReader` role stays. Recommendation 5 (scrubbing `#key=` from the published CI report) and findings on the docs rule reading `playwright-report`, the `CommitRaceTests` time limit under load and a release pipeline smoke step go to Phase 3 (filed by the lead).
- Release smoke suite, owner decision (ARV-128, Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-15, through the lead): no new role. The `SmokeReader` role of the 2026-10-14 entry is removed from code, tests and docs; point (3) of that entry and R1 and (11) of its security review note are superseded. The smoke account is a dedicated Handler station manager, the least privileged existing role: topology reads, zone profile reads, `LiveQueue.View`, alert rule reads and alerts (View, Search, Edit); none of its permissions is critical, and its one write, acknowledging its own alerts, is never sent by the smoke client, which sends reads only. The daily report check becomes a 403: the smoke account asks for the smoke site's daily report and must be refused (a Handler station manager does not read reports), which proves the route and its authorization; what a report holds stays covered by the E2E reports specs. Reverted with the role: the server rule that kept a smoke reader at named sites (R1; the named-sites rule is the validation observer's alone again) and the rule that lists report alerts only for roles holding `Alert.View` (recommendation 1), which no remaining role can reach (every role with `Report.View` holds `Alert.View`); the lead files that rule in Phase 3 as a guard for any future role. Proposed by the implementation, pending the owner: (16) the account holds the Handler station manager role alone and exactly the smoke site or sites, never every site and no other site; the server cannot enforce that for this role, so it is the administrator's rule, and the suite checks at every run, before anything else uses the account, that it holds that role alone, exactly that role's permissions (a list in `tests/smoke/support/role.ts`, which the E2E run compares with the role as `GET /api/v1/admin/roles` reports it), none of the critical permissions and exactly the configured sites (`ARIVA_SMOKE_SITE`, one code or several separated by commas), and refuses to run otherwise. (17) Points (1), (2) and (4) to (10) of the 2026-10-14 entry and (12) to (15) of its security review note stand, with (6) reading "the 11 checks of wiki 04 section 8.1" as now written (the report check a 403). (18) The live screen asks for alerts only with `Alert.View`, which every role with `LiveQueue.View` holds today, so it changes nothing for them. (19) `scripts/e2e-apphost.mjs` now draws the simulator read key (ARV-104i) as it draws the operator key, which global setup's host check needs and the read scope's tests needed already.

- Backup and restore (ARV-126, TC-34, TC-26, TC-50, TC-51), built 2026-10-15 under the owner's freeze on sign-in changes (the restore test signs in and reads restored seeds through existing code; nothing in authentication changed). Proposed by the implementation (the devops engineer), pending the owner (wiki 04 sections 10 and 11, wiki 10 section 4.13, scripts/ops/README.md): (1) A logical backup: `pg_dump --format=custom` inside the TimescaleDB pod by `scripts/ops/ariva-db.sh`, as the migration login over 127.0.0.1 with the password the pod already holds; physical backups with point-in-time recovery wait for TC-26. (2) The restore runs every Ariva step as the migration login (the checks, Ariva's group roles, statistics, the migration job afterwards) and TimescaleDB's own three steps (`timescaledb_pre_restore`, pg_restore, `timescaledb_post_restore`) as the database superuser over the pod's local socket, with peer authentication and no password: verified on 2026-10-15 that TimescaleDB refuses them to a non-superuser (its background workers can be stopped only by a superuser, and pg_restore must write its catalog and create chunks in `_timescaledb_internal`, which belong to the extension owner). Every object keeps the owner the dump records, the migration login, and `ariva_runtime` stays DML only (`ariva-db.sh check`, `BackupRestoreTests`). This is the closest the criterion "the restore runs with the migration role" can be met; making the migration login a superuser for the restore was rejected (an application login raised to superuser, left so by a failed restore). (3) A restore goes into an empty database only, on the same TimescaleDB version, with the same migration login name; before the superuser touches it, the dump's table of contents is checked: its kinds, names and owners, not the SQL of each entry (only Ariva's objects owned by the migration login, TimescaleDB's catalog data and the two TimescaleDB extensions; no other extension, language, event trigger, foreign server or owner). This check is defence in depth, not the gate (point 16). (4) Every backup file is encrypted with age (X25519, streamed, authenticated) to a recipient; the identity stays offline in a custody apart from the Data Protection certificate's, so a stolen backup with its identity still reveals no protected secret. age gives confidentiality and integrity, not origin: anyone with the public recipient can make a valid file. The origin comes from the signature on the backup's manifest (point 16); a SHA-256 the operator gives from their own operations log is the only alternative at restore. (5) Roles, passwords and verifiers are never in a backup; the migration job recreates the runtime and validation reader logins with the deployment's own passwords. (6) TC-50 answered: the key ring is in the database, wrapped under the certificate, so it travels in the database backup; the certificate is never in a backup and stays in the site's secret store; `ariva-ops.mjs keyring` checks before a restore is relied on that the certificate held opens every unexpired key. (7) TC-51 answered: the outbox relay now keeps the newest sent event of every key of a compacted topic (`TopicCatalog.CompactedTopics`) however old, older rows going as before (script 0052 adds the index); after a Kafka loss, with the topics recreated and api-stream stopped, `after-kafka-loss` marks those events unsent and clears `stream_offset`, and the relay sends them with their original event ids. Rebuilding the events from the source tables in a host command was rejected (logic in a host, a second way to build each event). Keys unchanged for more than 7 days before the upgrade are sent again only after their next change. (8) After a restore while Kafka survived, every MassTransit consumer group is reset to the backup time and the stream worker reads again from its restored positions, within the sensing topics' 3 days. (9) A restore brings back revoked access and old credentials: the procedure re-applies the security changes made since the backup from the operations log. (10) TC-34, Proposed until the customer answers: a daily backup with 14 kept and one before every upgrade; recovery point 24 hours for configuration, accounts and audit, and no loss of interval data while Kafka keeps the sensing topics; recovery time 4 hours at pilot scale, replaced by the go-live restore test's measurement; Kafka recovered within 1 hour; no standby site for the pilot. (11) Files are a tar stream from api-main, restored with `--no-overwrite-dir` (the volume root belongs to another user). (12) Nothing schedules the backup yet: an operator runs it daily until a scheduled backup exists (a follow-up for the lead to place). (13) Ariva.IntegrationTests now references Ariva.Api.Main and Ariva.Api.Integration and Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.Testing, to start the hosts on the restored database as a cluster runs them. (14) Owner decision (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-15, through the lead): point (2) is accepted; TimescaleDB's three restore steps run as the database superuser over the pod's local socket. (15) Owner decision (Ahmad Al-Sadder, 2026-10-15, through the lead): operator-run backups are enough for the pilot; the scheduled backup of point (12) goes to Phase 3, filed by the lead. (16) Security review of 2026-10-15 (security-reviewer; CWE-269, CWE-345, CWE-367): FAIL on R1, the archive's origin was not the gate. A restore runs the dump's SQL as the superuser, check-dump reads only the table of contents, age proves integrity and not origin, and the recorded SHA-256 sat unsigned in the manifest beside the dump, the file being hashed once and read twice more. Built: the backup signs its manifest with an operator's SSH key (`ssh-keygen -Y sign`, namespace `ariva-backup`; an Ed25519 key with a passphrase or in ssh-agent), kept apart from the backup store, the age identity and the certificate. A restore and a files restore verify that signature with `ssh-keygen -Y verify` against an allowed-signers file kept outside the backup store before any hash in the manifest is used, and refuse a missing or invalid signature, a signer not in the file (or limited to another namespace), a manifest that does not name the file and a file under another label; the only alternative is `--sha256` given by the operator from their own log. The file is copied into a private 0700 folder, the copy is hashed, and both decryption passes read only the copy; the folder is removed afterwards, also on failure. Every refusal comes before any kubectl step, which the wrapper's self-test proves with stand-ins for kubectl and age and the real ssh-keygen (a file replaced after the check is never read). OpenSSH 8.1 or later is now needed wherever `verify security` runs: the dev container's base image and CI's runners have it, and this session installed `openssh-client`. (17) The review's recommendation 1, taken: `ariva-db.sh check` also fails on a migration login that is superuser, replication or bypassrls; on members of Ariva's group roles other than the migration login and the logins named with `--runtime-login` and `--reader-login` (a restore takes the same options and refuses other members before anything changes); and on what a forged dump could leave for the superuser to run: C, untrusted-language or SECURITY DEFINER functions, languages other than plpgsql, extensions other than plpgsql and the TimescaleDB ones, and event triggers outside the extensions (proven by BackupRestoreTests). Recommendation 4 (the NuGet packages) is a dependency story of the lead's. (18) Proposed by the implementation, pending the owner: `allowed_signers` lives in the operations repository or the secret store, never in the backup store; a signing key rotates when its operator leaves, its line staying only as long as the backups it signed; the backups of a withdrawn key are restored with `--sha256` from the operations log.
- Desk plan per lane (ARV-129), decided by Ahmad Al-Sadder on 2026-10-15 in the session: not in the MVP. The pilot measures how many desks are open from AMAN's desk sessions or from desk sensors; it does not need a planned number of desks per lane. So the DesksBelowPlan alert rule keeps having no data: it stays listed and marked "not evaluated" on the alert rules screen and never fires. A staffing plan, entered or from the v1 staffing recommendations, can bring it back later.
- Recommended open desks (ARV-181), decided by Ahmad Al-Sadder on 2026-10-15 in the session: no staffing plan is entered (ARV-129 closed), but the pilot should tell the supervisor how many desks to open. An indicative version of the staffing recommendation (formulas F16, planned for v1) is added to Phase 1: per lane and 15 minutes for the next 3 hours, the fewest open desks that keep the predicted wait at or below the target, from the current queue, the arrival wave and the measured throughput per desk; no roster, shift or break rules, no Monte Carlo P90 and no show-up or lane-mix forecasting (all v1). It is shown as indicative on the immigration screen, and the DesksBelowPlan alert compares open desks with it.
- Recommended open desks, engine, stored minutes and API (ARV-181a), built 2026-10-15 (the plan of the same day split ARV-181 into ARV-181a, this, and ARV-181b, the immigration screen and the DesksBelowPlan alert). Owner decisions of 2026-10-15, through the coordinator: (1) the recommendation also keeps each desk at most 85 percent busy over the interval, configurable per site (`Staffing:BusyCap`, 0.5 to 1), a margin for the variability the deterministic recursion leaves out until v1's P90; (2) a flight without a passenger estimate (SSIM only, no AMAN inbound lane demand, no AODB estimate or seats) gives no recommendation for the intervals it affects, with the reason shown, never a guess. Lead decisions of 2026-10-15 (the plan's proposals, confirmed by the coordinator): (3) the predicted wait is max(Q_k, Q_(k+1)) over the interval's capacity, not F9's (Q_k + A_k / 2) / mu_k, which leaves out what the desks serve during the interval and cannot express F16's own test (formulas F16, with a note in F9); (4) the busy cap is measured on arrivals: each interval's arrivals must fit within the cap's share of the recommended desks' capacity (the reading of (1) confirmed; the backlog stays the wait's business); every interval records whether the wait, the cap or both set its number, stored and answered by the API, and F16 states that the cap can call for desks for a single heavy interval the wait target alone would absorb as a queue; (5) a flight without passengers gives `FlightWithoutPassengers` to every interval holding one of its hall minutes and `BacklogUnknown` to every interval after them (the backlog carried into them depends on it); (6) the target wait is configuration now (`Staffing:TargetMinutes`, default 10, per-site and per-lane overrides on the ARV-148 pattern), an audited site setting later; (7) no fallback cycle time is shipped: a lane without a measured throughput has no recommendation until the site configures its fallback at commissioning (wiki 07); (8) 13 rows: the current quarter hour from the computed minute and 12 more, reaching now plus 3 hours; (9) `ArrivalWave.MaxProjectionWindow` is 195 minutes, used only by `ArrivalWave.Project` for this projection (13 rows reach up to 194 minutes ahead); the arrival-wave API keeps 5 to 120 and the predicted-breach source 120; (10) "open now" is the desk-state count the nowcast uses (F8 n_open from the desks' latest closed minute, F10 Idle or Serving), labelled as such on the screen (ARV-181b), distinct from the lanes table's AMAN sessions; (11) `lane_staffing` (script 0053) is kept 90 days by a TimescaleDB retention policy; it becomes a migration parameter per environment with ARV-111a, and whichever of ARV-111a and ARV-181a lands second adds the table to the data inventory. Proposed by the implementation (the domain modeler), pending the owner: (12) the measured throughput per open desk is the lane queue's exits over its desks' open desk-minutes in the busy minutes (a person waiting for each open desk) of the 60 minutes ending at the lane's latest live queue minute, with at least 30 open desk-minutes, 20 exits and a cycle of 0.2 to 10 minutes; a desk never heard in the window is not waited for; (13) the queue now is used while its live minute is at most 3 minutes old and not degraded, the desks open now while their latest closed minute is at most 5 minutes old, and the API shows a lane's minute while at most 3 minutes old (`Stale` after); (14) a lane is a queue zone with a lane category on a level with an Immigration checkpoint, its desks the nowcast desk term's match (a desk serving several lanes counts in each, so recommendations of lanes sharing desks are not additive), its arrivals shared equally among the arrivals-hall queue zones of its category; the departures hall has none in this version (F13 is v1), and e-gate lanes have none (e-gates are not desks); (15) at least one desk while anyone waits or arrives, however small the fluid amounts (found by the property tests, seeds in F16); (16) the job runs in Ariva.Api.Cronz every minute at second 45 under a per-site advisory lock (class 51), inserts each lane's minute once (binary COPY, first computation stands), and refuses a site beyond its read bounds (100 lanes, 20,000 lane desks, 30,000 queue minutes, 120,000 desk minutes) with nothing written; a minute it missed stays empty; (17) the API (`GET api/v1/sites/{siteCode}/staffing-recommendation`) needs `ArrivalWaveLanes.View` (no new permission: border shift supervisors and administrators already hold it), answers NotFound outside the caller's sites, refuses more than 100 lanes with 409, and shows a lane whose stored rows fail script 0053's bounds as read as `Stale` (CWE-501). Phase 3 candidates (not needed before the pilot, to the coordinator): the predicted-breach source's queue-to-lane mapping (service zones' desks) differs from the one the immigration screen, the desk term and this recommendation use (`zone.lane_category`); a shared queue for all categories (the NBJ seed's lane `ALL`) gets `NoLaneDemand`, where its demand would be the sum of the manual lanes; the simulator's look-back (raising an earlier interval to the desk limit when a later one cannot be met).
- Recommended open desks, security review (ARV-181a), 2026-10-15: the security-reviewer's FAIL is built, decided by the implementation within the owner's rules. (1) CWE-501: the API's read-back (`SvcStaffingRecommendations.View`, `Plausible`, its row type) is internal and checked by a table-driven unit test, column by column (`StaffingReadBackTests`); it now holds every pairing of script 0053 and the engine's own (the lane-wide reason from the desks, throughput and queue; a bound exactly when a desk is needed; a wait exactly with a recommendation; a wait with the desks open now only when they are known; arrivals known where there is a recommendation), and a lane's minute only as 13 rows of one minute and the lane's category on that minute's plan; otherwise the lane reads Stale with no numbers. (2) CWE-120, CWE-400: the lane-desk bound is a validated setting, `Staffing:MaxLaneDeskRows` (20,000, 1 to 100,000), like `MaxDeskMinuteRows`; the queue-minute cap is derived, the lanes times the minutes read (one row per zone and minute, so at most 100 x 256 = 25,600; the earlier fixed 30,000 could never be reached), and pinned in a test. Integration tests plant 101 and 100 arrivals-hall lanes, a small lane-desk bound, a 1,001-desk lane and a failing site; an endpoint test pins 409 with the fixed text only and 404 "Not found" for anything else. (3) One bad lane no longer blanks the site: a queue length outside 0 to 1,000,000 gives that lane `NoQueueLength`; arrivals that are not a finite number of at most 1,000,000 give that interval a new reason, `ArrivalsOutOfRange` (script 0053's gap check takes it; the script is not yet merged, so its checksum is updated rather than a new script added), with `BacklogUnknown` after it; a minute's exits above a million are left out of the throughput; a lane with more than 1,000 desks is refused on its own (`SiteStaffing.Takes`, no row, still counted in its category's sharing, one warning with the site and count) and the site's other lanes are written. (4) The CWE-120 evidence is corrected: arriving legs at their cap give written `ArrivalsTruncated` rows with no numbers, not a refusal with nothing written. ASVS V15.2.2 cites the job's bounds. Recommendations 4 and 6 of the review are the coordinator's, for ARV-124 and ARV-111a.
- Patched dependencies in every image (ARV-184), built 2026-10-15 from the ARV-126 security reviews. Decided by the implementation and accepted by the lead after the security review (PASS in round 2, 2026-10-15): (1) Versions from nuget.org through the nuget MCP: System.Security.Cryptography.Pkcs 10.0.13 and Microsoft.AspNetCore.SignalR.Protocols.MessagePack 10.0.13 (published 2026-10-13), the first fixed versions of GHSA-846f-f764-76mx (High) and GHSA-2fmv-rv2c-59x7 (Moderate) for Pkcs, GHSA-86xp-94pg-8r6r (Moderate) for SignalR MessagePack and GHSA-334f-vvp4-7g6p (High) for Microsoft.AspNetCore.SignalR.Common, which the MessagePack package brings into the integration tests and which every host runs from the ASP.NET Core runtime pack; NuGet's vulnerability data gives each the affected range 10.0.0 to 10.0.12. (2) Pkcs is lifted by a direct reference in Ariva.Infra, the only project whose graph brings it (MailKit's MimeKit asks for 10.0.0 or later), with its version in `Directory.Packages.props`; `CentralPackageTransitivePinningEnabled` was not used, since it would make every entry of that file a floor for the transitive dependencies of the whole solution (the nuget MCP's fix plan proposes the same direct reference). (3) The runtime that ships is the SDK's: a self-contained publish bundles the runtime packs of the SDK's own runtime whatever `runtime-deps` says, so the images had shipped runtime 10.0.3 (SDK 10.0.103) on `runtime-deps` 10.0.12, with Pkcs and SignalR.Common at file version 10.0.326.7603. MCR publishes SDK patches of the newest feature band only (the 1xx tags stop at 10.0.103), so `scripts/base-images.mjs` now accepts any band for the SDK and the band moves with the runtime: the images build with SDK 10.0.402 (runtime 10.0.13) on `runtime-deps` 10.0.13, both pinned by digest. Pinning the runtime packs in MSBuild (`RuntimeFrameworkVersion`) instead was rejected: one more version to move every month, and the property also changes what framework-dependent builds and tests run on. (4) The proof is in every image build: a `checked` stage (the web image's pinned `node:22.23.3-alpine3.24`, so no new base image line) runs `scripts/published-runtime.mjs` on the publish output, and the final stage copies from it only. It fails unless both runtime packs equal the `runtime-deps` tag and are 10.0.13 or later, and System.Security.Cryptography.Pkcs.dll and Microsoft.AspNetCore.SignalR.Common.dll (and SignalR MessagePack where it ships) are at file version 10.0.1326.50504 or later, the 10.0.13 release's; it reads the PE version resource, not a package list. `scripts/base-images.mjs --check` (CI and `verify security`) fails a `runtime-deps` Dockerfile without that stage, with a `--runtime` other than its base's tag, or whose final stage copies anything else; `--resolve --write` moves `--runtime` with the base, and a Dependabot pull request that moves `runtime-deps` alone fails that check by design. Images were not built in this session (disk); the check was proven on local self-contained publishes of Ariva.Api.Ingest: it fails on this container's SDK 10.0.112 (runtime packs 10.0.12, SignalR.Common below the floor) and passes with runtime packs 10.0.13. The lead then restored and published all six hosts inside the pinned SDK 10.0.402 image itself, and the check passed on each; the images.yml build, with Trivy, follows on the next push and must be green before any image is pushed or tagged. (5) The dev container's SDK moves to 10.0.402 with the images, as its own comment and the harness README require; it was not rebuilt here. (6) The floors (`FIXED_RUNTIME`, `FLOORS`) move with the next advisory, raised by the story that answers it.
- Recommended open desks on the immigration screen (ARV-181b1), built 2026-10-15 (the panel of the ARV-181b split; the lead's decisions of the same day, from the plan: the panel and its request only for `ArrivalWaveLanes.View` holders, a summary per lane for the current quarter hour with a 13-row disclosure, read every 60 s, a one-line note on the departures tab, text through allowlists only). Proposed by the implementation, within the lead's design, no backend change: (1) the current quarter hour is the interval holding the answer's `asOfUtc` (the server's clock), the first interval when none does (a stored minute may be up to 3 minutes old across a quarter-hour boundary); its row reads "Now to" the interval's end, the others their start; (2) the difference is recommended now minus the desks open now (desk states) in words: "Short by n" in a warning badge, "Spare n" and "Matches" as plain text (the prototype's forecast wording), "Open count not known" when either number is missing; never colour alone; (3) the target wait and busy cap come per lane in the answer: the panel's header line names them once when every lane with numbers has the same, and each lane's caption always names its own with its time per passenger at a desk and its source (no API change needed); the header's "Computed at" is the latest lane minute, each caption its lane's; (4) a failed read empties the panel and says so (it is read again every minute) rather than keeping older numbers unmarked; a 409 says the site has more arrivals lanes than one answer holds; (5) the first lane short of desks now opens on the panel's first answer only, later answers keep what the user opened or closed; (6) a value that is not known reads "Not known" (words, not a dash), a wait of 120 "Over 120 min", no service with the desks open now "No desk open", arrivals in whole passengers and waits to one decimal; (7) lanes in the screen's order (citizens, residents, visitors, crew, e-gates), any other category after them in the answer's order; times in the browser's time zone as on the other live panels, Latin digits in Arabic; (8) the open-now count is labelled "Open now (desk states)" with a note under the table that the lanes table's Desks open counts AMAN's desk sessions; (9) the E2E planting helpers moved to `tests/support/staffing.ts`, shared with `api/staffing-recommendation.spec.ts`, whose job check now ignores minutes not yet begun (the screen suite plants A-RES for the coming minutes).
- Open desks below recommendation alert (ARV-181b2), built 2026-10-15 (the alert of the ARV-181b split). Decided by the lead on 2026-10-15 (the plan's section 6, recorded in the story's notes): (L1) the value is max(0, recommended minus open now) from `lane_staffing`'s interval 0 (the minute's own current quarter hour, passing the staffing API's read-back check, no gap); comparators `GreaterThan` and `GreaterOrEqual` only; a minute with a degraded open count has no value; (L2) the owner is the Border shift supervisor or the system administrator, never empty (an empty owner means every alert role), even for an administrator; the escalation role is one of them or none; (L3) such rules are hidden from other roles in search and get (404 for get, update and delete), so a terminal duty manager can neither read nor delete a border rule; (L4) create, a change to the metric and a backtest by another role answer 403 (`BorderOnly`, mapped in `TopologyAnswers`), hidden rules and alerts 404; (L5) queue zones only, checked when a rule is saved; a queue zone without a lane simply never has a value; (L6) rules from before the story and rows changed in the database are not evaluated (one warning per tick naming the site and the rule code), every output channel filters by metric anyway, and there is no database check constraint (no new script; script 0051 already accepts the metric); (L7) the two "open" numbers are labelled clearly (desk states on the panel and in the alert, AMAN sessions in the lanes table); (L8) live evaluation is proven in integration (Ariva.Api.Stream is not part of the E2E run), and the E2E suite plants rows the staffing job could have written; (L9) the E2E backtest reads yesterday's minutes; (L10) no demo-seed rule; (L11) out of this story, for the security review: predicted-nowcast rules on lane queues use the lane split of the arrival wave, terminal duty managers may create and backtest them, and their alerts reach airport roles; (L12) the alert email names the metric by its enum name (`Metric: DesksBelowPlan`), acceptable for now. Proposed by the implementation within those decisions: (1) "border" is the permission, not a list kept by hand: `Ariva.Core/Security/BorderAlerts` derives the border roles from the role seed (`RolePermissions`: the alert roles holding `ArrivalWaveLanes.View` that are not airport-side by `BorderDeskAccess.IsAirportSide`) and decides a caller or recipient by the permission its roles hold (an account holding an airport-side role and a border role receives); `BorderAlertsTests` pins the list to the supervisor and the administrator, checks that every holder of the permission is a border role, and that every role holding `SiteConfiguration.Transfer` is one; (2) the border checks live in `AlertRuleValues.Problems` (the entity), with two fixed texts (`BorderRolesOnly` for the owner and escalation roles, `BorderComparator` for the comparator) besides `BorderOnly` (403); the queue-zone rule shares `AlertRuleValues.WatchesQueueZonesOnly` with the health checks (service, import, evaluation) and `QueueZonesOnly` names the metric; (3) the 403 comes first: right after the site check on create and backtest (before the backtest's range check) and after the visibility and site checks on update, so a caller without the permission is answered 403 for the metric whatever else is wrong; a misspelt metric (`desksbelowplan`, a padded name, a number) is not the metric and answers 400 as any unknown name; (4) the read-back check moved unchanged to `Ariva.Infra/Staffing/StaffingReadBack.cs` (one class with the row type nested; the plan named the file `StaffingRows.cs`); the alert reads interval 0 only (`interval_index = 0` in SQL) and checks index and start again per row; (5) reads are bounded: `AlertInputs.MaxStaffingRows` 100,000 rows per read (a day over the 64 zones a rule may watch is 92,160), refused beyond it (backtest `BacktestTooLarge`, the live tick skipped with one warning and resumed), and each lane's latest computed minute is looked for within 90 days (the table's retention); (6) a stored rule failing the checks is skipped before its targets are read (states and open alerts left as they are), the warning reads "not evaluated, it fails the rule checks of its metric (border roles only, fires above)"; the skip warning of a read beyond its bound became generic ("its stored inputs exceed the rows one read takes"); (7) defence in depth beyond the plan: the site configuration export is refused (`BorderOnly`) to a caller without `ArrivalWaveLanes.View` when the site has a border rule, as the import is (`InvalidAlertRules`); only administrators reach either endpoint today; (8) the hub's audience of a border notice keeps the border roles among the owner, escalation and administrator roles (an airport-side owner leaves the administrator alone), and an email is written to border people only, at enqueue (`BorderAlerts.Audience`) and again in the sender's eligibility query (the metric and role lists bound as parameters); the daily report, its CSV, deliveries and the weekly summary add `AND (:border OR metric NOT IN (:borderMetrics))`; (9) the plan's constructed case changed in one place: its implausible row (10:17, 5 recommended of 4 desks) is refused by script 0053's own check, so the integration and E2E cases plant 10:17 to 10:19 as 8 desks short without arrivals (the engine's pairing, which the read-back check refuses), so counting them would raise a second alert; (10) the E2E suite has two accounts of its own with a second factor (`e2e.desksborder`, `e2e.desksterminal`, at DMO) so that a delete reaches the service (204 for the supervisor, 404 for the duty manager) without sharing another suite's TOTP account; it plants A-CRW at 06:00 UTC yesterday (the functional suite of the web part keeps A-CIT); (11) `AlertRuleErrors.NotEvaluatedMetrics` is kept, empty, for a metric added before its inputs exist. Proposed by the web part (2026-10-16): (12) the alert rules screen mirrors the server's border rules as constants in `src/lib/core/alertRules.ts` (`borderOnly`, `borderRoles`, `aboveComparators`, `borderPermission`, `watchesQueueZonesOnly`; no endpoint gives them) and `notEvaluated` is empty, kept for a metric added before its inputs exist; the functional and API suites pin the same values, so a change on one side fails a test; (13) for the metric the owner choices are the border roles the caller may give (their own; both for an administrator, so the administrator may own a border rule although the other metrics do not offer administrators as owner), never "Every role of the site", and the escalation choices the same or none; (14) choosing the metric moves the form's values onto its choices (the owner to the first border role when it is not one, an airport-side escalation role cleared, a comparator below to Above), also when a saved or duplicated border rule opens, and leaving it moves an administrator owner back to the first operational role; a stored border rule with an airport-side owner (from before the story or changed in the database) therefore opens with a border owner, and saving it is the server's decision (a non-administrator taking away a role it does not hold is refused `RoleNotHeld`); (15) the form checks before sending that a border rule has a border owner (`alertRules.form.borderOwner`, like the zones check), the preview has no client check; (16) the server's refusals show as every refusal on the screen does: the problem's title and first problem as the server words them in English ("Not valid: ...", "Not allowed: ..."), not the further items of `problems`, which `Api.ts` does not read for any screen;
