(17) the Arabic display name "مكاتب مفتوحة أقل من التوصية", the hint and the owner text await a native speaker's review; (18) the functional suite plants the constructed case on A-CIT at 04:00 UTC yesterday through a shared helper (`plantDesksShortCase` in `tests/support/staffing.ts`); the API suite keeps its own copy on A-CRW. Note of 2026-10-16 (ARV-187, appended; the points above stand as written): (L11) is decided by the owner (the predicted nowcast is border data too; entry "Predicted-breach rules are border data" below); the "one warning per tick" of (L6) and point (6)'s "states and open alerts left as they are" are superseded by the lead's decisions P3 and P4 of that entry (one warning per rule and values version per host; open alerts withdrawn and states dropped), and the texts `BorderOnly` and `BorderRolesOnly` of point (2) now name both border metrics (P1).
- Repository visibility, 2026-10-15. Decided by the owner: the repository `github.com/alsadderwork-coder/Ariva` is public by intent (the 2026-10-01 entry above said private; GitHub reported it public when the lead checked on 2026-10-15, and pushes were held until the owner answered). Consequences, recorded by the lead: everything committed is published, including the NBJ-BC1 demo seed derived from the client's design drawings (ARV-139c), so client material and credentials stay out of the repository; rulesets on `main`, environments with required reviewers, CodeQL and secret scanning become available on GitHub Free (docs/harness/README.md, wiki 04 section 6.6); the workflows were checked for a public repository (the `@claude` comment job is limited to owners, members and collaborators, fork pull requests get no secrets, `release-dev.yml` with its optional self-hosted runner runs only on a manual dispatch). CLAUDE.md rule 8, ADR-0016 (amendment of 2026-10-15), the implementation plan, the harness README and wiki 04 now say public.
- Data inventory and retention settings for the pilot DPIA (ARV-111a, TC-62, TC-65 to TC-68), built 2026-10-16. Proposed by the implementation (the backend engineer), pending the owner (docs/privacy/data-inventory.md, wiki 04 section 5, wiki 10 section 4.8m, wiki 14 section 3): (1) Retention periods are migration-job parameters per environment: `Database:Retention` (`RawSensingDays`, `DeskZoneReadingDays`, `LaneStaffingDays`, `AlertEmailDays`, `IntegrationCallDays`, `ReportDeliveryDays`), in the clusters the Helm values `migration.retention` of each environment, which the chart passes to the `database-migration` job as `Database__Retention__*` and refuses out of range. (2) How the parameter meets the `add_retention_policy` calls of merged scripts (0017, 0019, 0040, 0053): the scripts stay as they are (immutable) and their values are the defaults; after the scripts, on every release, the job reads each policy in force (`timescaledb_information.jobs`) and, only when it differs from the configured period, removes it and adds it again with the period, in one transaction under the script runner's advisory lock, so there is never a moment without a policy and a release that changes nothing changes nothing (the policy keeps its job and schedule). No new script is needed for the hypertables. The configured value wins: a policy changed by hand in the database is put back by the next release. (3) The parameters are the hypertables that already had a policy: `sensing_event`, `sensing_batch` and `device_health_event` share `RawSensingDays` (the contract's dispute window, TC-62, 90 days until answered; a replay needs the events and the devices' health of the same period), `desk_zone_reading` (7 days, 2 to 90) and `lane_staffing` (90 days, never below 90: the open desks alert's backtest reads 90 days). The hypertables kept indefinitely as evidence (queue, desk, e-gate and line minutes, bins, zone health, overflow, the shadow nowcast, availability) are not parameters: shortening one is a decision for a reviewed script. Bounds: raw sensing 7 to 3,650 days (events arrive up to 3 days late), relational periods 30 to 3,650. (4) The relational periods are migration-job parameters too, not settings of the Cronz job: the job writes them to `retention_period` (script 0054), which the runtime role reads and never writes. The Cronz job, which connects as the runtime login, deletes only through `ariva_retention_purge(data set, batch size)`: a SECURITY DEFINER function with its search path pinned to `pg_catalog, pg_temp` (no public: every object in its body is named with its schema), its time zone to UTC (a caller's daylight saving time cannot move the cutoff), EXECUTE for `ariva_runtime` only, that reads the period itself, refuses a period below 30 days and a batch outside 1 to 10,000 rows, has one constant DELETE per data set and never deletes a Pending email. The runtime role keeps no DELETE on `email_message`, `integration_call` or `report_delivery` (the existing tests that a direct DELETE answers 42501 stand), so the runtime login can remove nothing the configured period does not already let go, because it cannot move the time column the purge reads (`email_message.created_utc`: 0023's column grant and trigger; `integration_call`: no UPDATE; `report_delivery.created_utc`: script 0054 leaves the runtime role UPDATE of `status`, `attempts`, `reason` and `sent_utc` only, the columns the delivery round marks; security review round 2). Residual: the migration (owner) login is in `base-appsettings-secret` and so in every host pod (ARV-083), so code execution in a host could still truncate these tables, rewrite `retention_period` or replace the function. Alternatives set aside: DELETE granted with a guard trigger or row-level security (the runtime role would hold DELETE; the existing refusals would change), or a separate retention login (one more secret per environment). Consequence: `ariva-db.sh check` (ARV-126) now lets this one SECURITY DEFINER function pass, and only with the owner, language, settings, body SHA-256 and grants script 0054 ships (EXECUTE for the owner and `ariva_runtime` only, without grant option); another body or a grant to PUBLIC under its name fails the check (security review round 1, 2026-10-16). (5) Proposed defaults: alert emails 180 days, a Pending email never (TC-67); Integration API call records 400 days (TC-68); report deliveries 400 days (TC-66, the value already Proposed); raw sensing 90 days (TC-62); logs and traces 30 days in the log store, readable by the operations team only (TC-65; set in Loki or SigNoz, outside Ariva). (6) The Cronz job `RelationalRetention` runs daily at 03:15 UTC: per data set, batches of `Cronz:Retention:BatchSize` (5,000) rows, each its own transaction, at most `MaxBatchesPerRun` (100) per run (the rest the next day), one replica at a time (session advisory lock, class 52); a data set without a period is skipped with a warning, a failing one logged and retried the next day while the others go on; logs name data sets, periods and counts only. (7) Environments: k8s-prd and k8s-demo keep the defaults; k8s-dev and localk8s keep raw sensing 30 days (simulated data only). (8) Left as they are, open: sign-in sessions (`user_session`, `refresh_token`, with client address and user agent) keep no period: they are sign-in history and the sign-in freeze applies; Proposed for TC-65: deleted 400 days after a session ends, by the same job, as a later change. The audit trail stays indefinite (D5); flights (TC-64), the border feed records and the validation evidence keep their To confirm.
- Predicted-breach rules are border data (ARV-187), decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-16 in the session, after the lead's explanation with a worked example; built 2026-10-16. Decision: `PredictedNowcast` joins `BorderAlerts.Metrics` beside `DesksBelowPlan`, so its rules, backtests, previews, alerts, live hub notices, emails and reports (the daily report, its CSV and scheduled deliveries, the weekly summary) are only for holders of `ArrivalWaveLanes.View` (the border shift supervisor and the system administrator). The terminal duty manager loses predicted-wait alerts on immigration lanes and keeps live-wait (`Nowcast`) alerts. Existing `PredictedNowcast` rules with an airport-side or empty owner (or an airport-side escalation role) are not evaluated, with one warning, and wiki 18 carries a migration note. Reason: finding 1 of the ARV-181b2 security review (Medium, CWE-863 and CWE-200, pre-existing since ARV-038 and ARV-047). The metric steps a lane queue with its share of the arrival wave (`AlertInputs.PredictedAsync`, `ProjectedArrivalWave`), the lane split the immigration screen withholds from airport-side roles (wiki 01). A terminal duty manager holds the alert rule permissions and `LiveQueue.View`, so it could create and backtest such a rule on an arrivals lane and, from each answer's peak value and minute per lead (15 to 60 minutes) with the live queue and service rate, solve for the cumulative lane arrivals ahead (and, with the per-flight totals it sees, a lone flight's lane mix); alerts of such rules without an owner carried it more coarsely to the handler station manager. Rejected interim fix: refusing only the backtest and preview to callers without the permission (the precise channel of the finding) would still leave the live alerts' raised values and predicted minutes (`predicted_for_utc`) to airport-side roles. A zone serving no lane has no projection, so in practice the metric judges lane queues only, and nothing airport-side is lost: counters and security lanes cannot carry lane categories. Decided by the lead within the owner's decision (the plan's P1 to P6): (P1) one generic `BorderOnly` text (403) and one generic `BorderRolesOnly` text (400) for both border metrics, so `TopologyAnswers` keeps a single 403 match; `BorderComparator` stays for open desks below recommendation only (`AlertRuleValues.FiresAboveOnly`). (P2) The predicted nowcast keeps all four comparators, its lead of 15 to 60 minutes and its overflow zones (an overflow band takes its queue zone's arrivals; it is not added to `WatchesQueueZonesOnly`): only its roles, visibility and channels change. (P3) The not-evaluated warning is written once per rule and values version per host process (`Ariva.Infra.Alerting.NotEvaluatedWarnings`, a singleton registered with the evaluation), again when the rule's values change or after it was evaluated in between, for both border metrics; this supersedes ARV-181b2's (L6) "one warning per tick". The warning's reason is one fixed text, "it fails the rule checks of its border metric" (CWE-117). The alert rules screen marks such enabled rules ("Not evaluated: fix its border settings"). (P4) A not-evaluated border rule has its open alerts resolved `RuleWithdrawn` and its states dropped at each tick (left open, housekeeping would escalate them), so it starts at the present once repaired. (P5) The site configuration export refuses a site holding a rule the import would refuse (`SiteConfigurationErrors.RulesNotImportable`, 409, naming no rule), keeping the ARV-122b and ARV-162 rule that every export imports. (P6) No Timescale script, endpoint or permission change; `security/permission-matrix.json` is unchanged. Consequences: a terminal duty manager no longer finds, reads or deletes its own predicted rules from before the story (404); a supervisor can delete one but cannot change its owner from the duty manager (`RoleNotHeld`), so an administrator repairs it; disabling one (`PUT`) is refused (`BorderRolesOnly`) until it has a border owner; historic predicted alerts leave airport-side roles' screens and past reports; emails still pending to airport-side people are withheld at send, those already sent cannot be recalled; an older bundle holding such a rule no longer imports; during a rolling upgrade each old host keeps the old rule until it is replaced (an old Ariva.Api.Main lets the duty manager create, backtest and list predicted rules and alerts and relays their notices; an old Ariva.Api.Stream keeps evaluating airport-owned rules and queues their emails; an old Ariva.Api.Integration sends pending ones; an old Ariva.Api.Cronz renders scheduled deliveries with them), so every host is upgraded in one release and the affected rules are listed and repaired or deleted before the upgrade (wiki 18, section 6). Proposed by the implementation within those decisions: the two English texts of P1; the integration case's fixed wave (12 people a minute, a queue of 30 served at 5 a minute, 48.2 minutes 30 minutes ahead); two E2E accounts of their own with a second factor (`e2e.predborder`, `e2e.predterminal`, at DMO).

- Instruction slides for the passenger displays (ARV-141), built 2026-10-16 (wiki 11 section 9, wiki 12 Passenger display). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) the story's permission "Display.Content.Manage" is two permissions, `DisplayContent.View` and `DisplayContent.Manage` (permission codes are Entity.Action), held by the roles that manage displays (border shift supervisors and terminal duty managers; administrators hold every permission); handlers and validation observers none; (2) a slide applies to its whole site, to the displays showing any of up to 20 queue zones (published queue zones only), or to up to 20 displays by code; (3) words in at least one of English, Arabic, Portuguese and Swahili, a title of up to 80 characters and an optional text of up to 240, without line breaks; a display shows the languages it has and skips a slide in none of them; (4) a display pins the first pinned slide that applies to it, under the waits in either layout, and any other pinned slide rotates; (5) a slide is retired, never deleted, and can be restored; (6) slides reach a player only through its display-authenticated board, and the default instructions of ARV-140 remain when none applies; (7) the order is per site and a reorder lists every slide of the site once; two slides created at the same moment can share a position until the next reorder; (8) the demo seed adds five arrivals slides at DMO and AUH-TA once, for the arrival queue zones, their Arabic, Portuguese and Swahili wording To confirm by a native reviewer before a pilot; (9) slides do not travel in a site configuration export yet (the ARV-122 bundle is unchanged); a later story if the owner wants them to.

- Advertising policy for a border hall (ARV-145), decided by the owner (Ahmad Al-Sadder) on 2026-10-16 in the session: advertising stays off by default; only a SystemAdministrator, with a second factor in the last 15 minutes, turns it on for a site, and the audit keeps who authorised it (the border authority or the airport operator) as the change's reason. Zone exclusions keep a hall such as immigration without advertising even where the site allows it. No second sign-off by a border role. Built 2026-10-16 (wiki 11 section 9, Advertising policy); proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) the roles that manage displays see the policy (`AdvertisingPolicy.View`), so the display form can tell when advertising may be chosen; (2) the reason is 10 to 500 characters on one line and is required for every change, turning advertising off included; (3) up to 20 excluded zones, published queue zones of the site, matched by name; since the two rounds of the security review the policy is saved against the site's published zone profile, and once another profile is published (duty managers and supervisors can publish one, with zones renamed, moved, dropped, added, or an excluded name given to a decoy zone) no display of the site shows advertising until an administrator saves the policy again, and a save that allows advertising names the profile version it was reviewed against (refused when another was published meanwhile); so only an administrator decides where advertising runs, at the cost of a policy save after each new profile (an alternative for the owner: a list of zones where advertising is allowed, which would still need the same confirmation); (4) a display set to advertising before the policy changed keeps the setting and its board shows instructions from the next poll, rather than being reset, and it can be saved again only with instructions while the policy does not allow it; (5) the policy never travels in a site configuration bundle, and an import refuses displays set to advertising; (6) advertising endpoints (ARV-142, ARV-143) carry `[AdvertisingAllowed]`, site-level only (403 when off, 404 outside the caller's sites); zone exclusions apply to displays; (7) the licensed module flag of the story waits for ARV-135, which does not exist yet.

- Advertisement image library (ARV-142a), built 2026-10-17 (wiki 11 section 9). The lead split ARV-142 on 2026-10-17 (backlog/progress.md): ARV-142a holds the images, the two-person approval, the display fetch and the screen; ARV-142b the MP4 and WebM container checks and the CSP's media-src. Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) `Advertising.View`, `Advertising.Manage` and `Advertising.Approve` go to terminal duty managers (two of them make the two people) and to system administrators through the every-permission rule; border shift supervisors hold none. View is its own permission so that Approve guards nothing but the approval and is a critical permission (first security review). (2) Approving is a critical action (a second factor in the last 15 minutes), like publishing a zone profile, since it puts content in front of passengers, and names the item's review token as its approver was shown it (refused, 409, once the item changed), as a profile's publish names its geometry. (3) Besides the uploader, the last person who changed an item cannot approve it either, so no one approves their own edit; a change of an approved item sends it back to draft. (4) Retired is final (an item cannot be restored; upload it again). (5) Limits: 10 MB, 3,840 pixels a side and 8,294,400 in all (a 4K screen in either orientation, lowered from 7,680 and 16,777,216 after the first security review measured the re-encode cost), 64 JPEG scans, 500 items per site, dates at most 731 days apart, 30 upload attempts per person in 10 minutes (refused files included), one decode at a time per process; animated images (WebP and APNG) refused rather than cut to one frame. (6) Images are decoded and written anew with SkiaSharp (MIT; its native library only in Ariva.Api.Main) after managed code read their header, in their own format, turned upright from their EXIF orientation and converted to sRGB, with low encoder effort (PNG one filter at level 4, lossless WebP effort 25, lossy WebP and JPEG quality 85 and 90); the costliest 4K image takes about 3.6 s. (7) Dates are the site's local days (its airports' time zone, UTC without one). (8) Until playlists (ARV-143), the display fetch serves any approved item of the display's site within its days while the display is set to advertising and the ARV-145 rule allows it; ARV-143 narrows it to the display's playlist. (9) For ARV-142b: a player fetches media with its credential header, so it plays an object URL, which needs media-src 'self' blob: (the original criterion said 'self' only, which cannot serve that path). (10) Open, for the owner: the native decoders run in Ariva.Api.Main, which also signs tokens, so a memory-safety bug in them reachable by a duty manager would expose the signing key; isolating decoding in a host without secrets (an ADR) or accepting the residual risk with the mitigations above (managed header checks, limits, one at a time, Dependabot) is the owner's decision (first security review of ARV-142a, advisory B).

- 2026-10-17 ARV-142b: advertisement videos, MP4 (H.264) and WebM within a configured length and size, built 2026-10-17 (wiki 11 section 9). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) Limits: `Advertising:Videos:MaxMegabytes` 20 and `MaxSeconds` 30 by default, settable from 1 to 100 MB and 1 to 120 seconds (validated at start-up); 100 MB and 120 seconds are ceilings every installation keeps (`AdvertisingMedia.MaxVideoBytes`, `MaxVideoSeconds`, and script 0059). The defaults are chosen to fit the chart's ingress body size of 21m on api-main and web-api out of the box; an installation that raises the size raises the ingresses with it. The endpoint declares the ceiling and `AdvertisingUploadLimitAttribute` lowers the body limit to what the installation takes before the form is read. (2) MP4 is taken with the brands isom, iso2, iso4, iso5, iso6, mp41, mp42, avc1 and M4V; QuickTime (`qt  `) is refused, as are sound sample entries of version 1 or 2 (QuickTime's). Fragmented MP4 (`mvex` in `moov`, `moof` at the top) and live WebM (elements of unknown size, no `Duration`) are refused because their length is not stated up front, so the length limit could not be judged before the file is kept. (3) Unknown boxes inside `moov` and its tracks, and unknown elements inside the WebM segment information and tracks, are kept: browsers ignore them, and refusing them would refuse ordinary files from ordinary encoders. Unknown top-level boxes and unknown children of the WebM segment are refused, as is anything after the last top-level box or element. (4) WebM `MuxingApp` and `WritingApp` are kept (Matroska requires them; they name the muxer, for example "Lavf60.16.100"); the rest of the metadata is stripped into zeroed padding of the same size so the media does not move (MP4 `udta`, `meta`, `uuid`, free space, handler and compressor names, creation and change times; WebM `Tags`, `Attachments`, `Chapters`, `Title`, `DateUTC`, track `Name`, `CRC-32`). The media data itself is kept as uploaded, an H.264 encoder's settings message included. (5) No video is decoded on the server: managed code reads the container only (at most 12 deep, 4,096 boxes or elements, the media data never walked), so the video decoders run only in browser sandboxes (the library preview and the display players), unlike the image decoders of ARV-142a point (10), which run in Ariva.Api.Main. No malware scan (ARV-092, ASVS V5.4.3 stays a Gap). (6) The display player's playback is ARV-143's: until then a display set to advertising shows instructions; the web CSP's `media-src 'self' blob:` (ARV-142a point (9)) is in place now, with no other media source, so ARV-143's player can play images and videos the same way (fetched with the display credential, shown from an object URL). (7) In the library, video previews load only when someone asks (Load the video), fetched with the user's token and played muted with controls; images still preview at once (the newest 40), since a video can be up to 100 MB. (8) Open for the owner: file storage for videos. Videos live on the same `ariva-files` claim as floor plans and images, which the chart sizes at 2Gi (`fileStorage.size`): at the 20 MB default that is about 90 videos in all, while a site's library may hold 500 items (retired ones included, their files kept). The chart default is unchanged; each installation sizes the claim for the library it plans (items times size, plus floor plans and images), or a later story moves media to an S3-compatible store behind `IFileStorage` (wiki 04). The api-main `/tmp` volume (128Mi) holds one buffered upload at a time, enough up to the 100 MB ceiling. (9) The size and length held to the limits are what the container states, checked for agreement (no MP4 track or media header more than a second past the movie's length, every WebM cluster opening with its start and none past the stated length, WebM timed in milliseconds so a block reaches at most 32.8 seconds past its cluster's start) but not decoded: a crafted file could carry a longer or larger stream within the size limit, which the second person's preview and ARV-143's player ending each item at its slot's end bound (security review of ARV-142b).
- 2026-10-18 ARV-143a: advertising playlists and schedules, with a timeline preview, built 2026-10-18 (wiki 11 section 9). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) The lead split ARV-143 on 2026-10-18 (backlog/progress.md): ARV-143a holds the plan (playlists, schedules, the timeline and the screen), ARV-143b the board playing it (the player, a wait threshold in minutes with hysteresis, the fallbacks to instructions, a bounded cache); the player is a risk of its own (the display host, its cache, the fetch with the display credential) and the two did not fit one story. (2) The story's display groups are read as the targets instruction slides already use (ARV-141): a schedule applies to its whole site, to named queue zones of the published profile or to named live displays (at most 20), and no display group entity is added. A display matches a zone schedule when any zone it shows is named. (3) A schedule has days of the week (English names, kept in the week's order) and one window in the site's local time, "HH:MM" to "HH:MM"; a closing time at or before the opening runs past midnight (Friday 22:00 to 02:00 still applies at 01:00 on Saturday) and "24:00" closes at midnight. Two windows on one day are two schedules. The plan is resolved for each instant from its local wall-clock time: on the day the clocks go forward the skipped times never occur (a window in them is shorter or never opens), on the day they go back the repeated hour is covered twice. (4) Where schedules overlap, a display's schedule comes before a zone's and a zone's before the site's; among alike targets, the window that opened last wins (a peak window inside an all-day one plays while it is open); then the newest schedule, then its id, so the answer is the same every time. (5) A retired playlist stops its schedules applying: they stay active and show that their playlist is retired, so restoring the playlist brings them back. A new or changed schedule names an active playlist of the site. (6) A playlist names advertisements of the site's library that are not retired and active instruction slides of the site, each once, in playing order. Drafts and advertisements outside their dates may be planned: the board plays only approved items within their dates (ARV-143b), and the screen marks the others as not playable now. Images and slides stay 5 to 60 seconds; a video's slot is its own length rounded up to the second (1 to 120), whatever was asked; an item's weight is 1 to 10, its share of airtime (ARV-143b sets how the player spends it). Retiring an item later leaves the playlist as it is: it can still be renamed or retired, and changing its items needs every item valid again. (7) Limits: 40 items in a playlist (the audit entry keeps 2,000 characters and records every item), 100 playlists and 200 schedules in a site, retired ones included (409), held under the site's advisory locks (classes 1431 and 1432) so that creations at the same moment cannot pass them. (8) `Advertising.View` reads plans and `Advertising.Manage` changes them (duty managers and administrators, as for the library; border roles neither). A plan needs no second person: what plays is approved item by item (ARV-142a point (2)). Every endpoint answers 403 while the site's advertising policy is off, and the plans are kept. (9) The timeline shows a display's 7 days from the site's local midnight today, resolved minute by minute and merged into stretches, for any live display of the site, with its second region: a display set to instructions shows them whatever the plan says, and the screen says so. Schedules do not check the policy's excluded zones (ARV-145): a display set to advertising cannot show an excluded zone (the policy guard), so a zone schedule naming one affects no advertising display. (10) Plans are retired and restored, never deleted: the runtime role may not delete or truncate them, the database checks a playlist's items (kinds, ids, slots, weights, the same site, advertisements not retired, slides active) whenever they change, and keeps a row's id and site fixed (script 0060).
- 2026-10-18 ARV-143b: displays play their scheduled playlist, with instructions as the fallback, built 2026-10-18 (wiki 11 section 9, wiki 12 Passenger display). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) An item's weight is how many times it plays in one cycle of its playlist, so its airtime is its weight times its slot; the repeats are spread through the cycle by smooth weighted round robin (weights of 2 and 1 play A, B, A), and the board sends the cycle's order, so every player plays the same. The Advertising screen's "one pass" adds each slot once; a cycle adds each slot as often as its weight. (2) The board carries the playlist that applies now (ARV-143a's precedence) and the instant it next changes, looked for no further ahead than the display's stale threshold (at most 30 minutes, so each board costs at most 30 steps however many schedules the site has) and never past the site's next local midnight (advertisements' dates turn then); it is judged again on every board (every 10 seconds). A player without a newer board keeps playing the plan until that instant, then shows instructions, so a plan never outlasts a stale board (security review of ARV-143b). (3) What plays: advertisements approved and within their dates on the site's local day, and active slides with words in the display's languages, in the playlist's order; a slide in a playlist plays wherever its schedule applies, whatever the slide's own scope (the schedule decides where); a playlist with nothing that may play leaves the instructions. Only a split display set to advertising, at a site whose policy allows it for every zone it shows, plays a plan: the policy is read on every board and a plan is never itself permission. (4) The wait rule is a display setting, `advertisingPauseMinutes`, 1 to 240 minutes or none (the request's "Long" as a number, since bands are widths in minutes): the player shows instructions when the nowcast of any queue zone it shows, with fresh data, reaches it, and plays again only when every fresh one is below it by the display's hysteresis; with no fresh data the state holds. The player judges it on each board, as it holds its bands. (5) An item that fails to load or play shows instructions for its slot, and the next item plays on time; the failed item is tried again at its next turn. (6) The player keeps the items it fetched in memory only, at most 40 items and 150 MB, the least recently used out first; it releases items that leave the plan, everything when advertising stops, and everything with the page. An item larger than that plays without being kept, and the next item fetched ahead is held beside the cache when it does not fit, so at its peak a player holds 150 MB and one item (at most the installation's video size). While an item's bytes are on their way the region shows the instructions. (7) The display fetch of ARV-142a still serves any item of its site that may play to a split display set to advertising (a full board fetches nothing), not only the items of the display's current plan: the library is approved for the site's displays item by item, and tying the fetch to the plan would compute the plan on every fetch; ARV-144's proof of play still refuses plays that are not on the display's plan. (8) The board sends the playlist's id, the items' ids, types and slots and the slides' words, never the playlist's or an advertiser's name.
- 2026-10-18 ARV-144: proof of play recording and figures, built 2026-10-18 (wiki 11 section 9). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) A play is an advertisement's: an instruction slide in a playlist is not a play and is not reported. The player reports a play as completed only when it lasted to its slot's end and was on screen for the slot within 2 seconds, as the server checks; an advertisement whose bytes came late in its slot is reported as cut short (found by CI on 2026-10-18: a slow runner's late image was refused as completed). (2) The server takes a play only when the display could have shown it, as its board would have: the display is a split display set to advertising and the site's policy allows advertising for every zone it shows (otherwise every play of the batch is refused, as a plan is never itself permission, ARV-145); its advertisement is an item of the playlist that applied to that display when the play started, or a minute before (the board the player last read and its clock), by the plans as they are when the play is reported (a minute later, as a rule), and may play on the site's local day it started (approved, within its dates). A plan changed in between refuses the plays made under the old one, which a display reporting every minute makes rare. The play lasted at least a second (less on screen is no play, and the player does not report it) and no longer than that item's slot, and counts as completed only when it filled it, 2 seconds either way (a playlist may hold an advertisement twice with different slots: either may match). It overlaps no other play of the display, in the batch or stored: two plays may share no more than a quarter of the shorter one and never more than a second (one advertising region shows one item at a time; a play that only touches the next is taken), so a play inside another is always refused, short plays cannot stack, and the airtime taken over any stretch exceeds the stretch by at most a second at each change of play. The stored plays are read and the overlaps decided in the write's own transaction under the display's advisory lock (class 1441), so batches of one display sent at once wait for each other and cannot both claim the same airtime (security review of ARV-144, round 2). A refusal never stores the play, and refused plays are under-reported rather than over-claimed: a player whose clock estimate jumps by more than a second between two boards may lose a play to the overlap rule; a display's credential can still claim interrupted plays of a second or more back to back, at most its wall-clock time plus that tolerance. A batch with refused plays is logged as a security event (event 9126: the display, its site and the counts, never the body). (3) Bounds: 1 to 100 plays a batch, 64 KB a body, 6 batches a minute per display (the `display-plays` policy, `Security:RateLimiting:DisplayPlays`); a play started within the last 24 hours, lasted 1 to 125 seconds (a slot's ceiling and the player's timing) and has ended by the time it is reported, within 5 seconds for the player's timing (it keeps the server's clock from its boards), so none starts in the future (the start is compared before any arithmetic on it, so no instant a body names can overflow); the table holds the same bounds for any writer (received at most 5 seconds before the start and at most a day after it, 1,000 to 125,000 milliseconds), and the runtime role inserts and reads only, through the hypertable or a chunk. The answer counts the plays taken, those the server already had and those refused, and the player lets the batch go. (4) A play is keyed by its display, advertisement and start, so a batch sent twice counts once. (5) The story's "site setting" for retention is read as the installation's setting `Database:Retention:AdvertisingPlayDays` (400 days by default, 30 to 3,650), a TimescaleDB retention policy set by the migration job like the other hypertables' (ARV-111a): an installation keeps its sites' plays for one period. A period per site would need a store of site settings (ARV-136). (6) The figures, per local day of the site, display and advertisement: the plays, those that played their whole slot, their airtime and the airtime the plans scheduled, then the same summed by campaign (the advertiser and campaign as the library names them now), advertiser, display and day, with the share delivered (airtime over scheduled airtime). The scheduled airtime is each display that plays advertising now (split, set to advertising, enabled) over the stretches its plans apply, each playable item taking its weight times its slot of every cycle (slides fill the cycle and are not counted), by the plans as they are when the figures are read: past plan versions, periods with advertising off and the wait rule's pauses are not known to it, so a share below 1 is expected where the wait rule paused advertising. A read covers 1 to 31 local days of the site, none after the site's today and none more than 3,650 days back (the longest retention); displays with the same candidate schedules share one timeline, so a read costs one minute step per minute of the range for each set of schedules, not for each display. (7) The plays hold ids, times, lengths and outcomes only: no passenger data, and nothing about who was near a screen. (8) The figures are a service (`ISvcAdvertisingFigures`) without an endpoint: the report, its endpoint and permission and the CSV export are ARV-146, which reads it. Until then the service itself refuses a caller whose roles do not view advertising (`Advertising.View`, the terminal duty manager and the administrator), answering like a site that does not exist, so no later caller reaches the figures without a permission (as the weekly summary of ARV-119 does). ARV-146 is to give the endpoint a concurrency limit (a read of 31 days is the costliest the service allows) and neutralise spreadsheet formulas in the CSV (the advertiser and campaign names are free text).
- 2026-10-19 ARV-096: request timeouts on Ariva.Api.Main and a concurrency policy on the on-demand daily report and its CSV files, built 2026-10-19 (docs/security/costly-operations.md sections 2, 4, 7 and 15; wiki 04 settings). Proposed by the implementation (the engineer), pending the owner: (1) The daily report and its CSV files share the `reports` concurrency policy per host: 2 at once and 4 waiting, oldest first, applied before authentication, 429 with Retry-After 10 beyond (`Security:RateLimiting:Reports`; a permit limit below 1 or a negative queue refuses the start, as for the other policies). Why 2 and 4: a report reads a site's whole local day of minutes with their histograms (up to 720,000 rows at the zone bound), as costly a request as a backtest (2 at once and 2 waiting); the queue lets the three CSV files of one screen and a second reader wait for a turn instead of failing. (2) Their request timeout, `reports`, is 60 s (`Security:RequestTimeouts:ReportsSeconds`, 5 to 600 s), 503 with Retry-After 30; every read of the report takes the request's token, so the database stops with it. (3) Every other endpoint of Ariva.Api.Main has a default request timeout of 120 s (`Security:RequestTimeouts:DefaultSeconds`, 5 to 600 s), 503 with Retry-After 30: below the usual proxy timeouts and far above what the bounded reads under it take; the validation results keep their own 90 s (ARV-104g). (4) The endpoints that move a large file have the `transfer` timeout of 15 minutes (`Security:RequestTimeouts:TransferSeconds`, 60 to 3,600 s): every endpoint of the `upload` concurrency policy (floor plans, SSIM preview and import, both CSV imports, the site configuration export and import, advertisement uploads) and the stored files served whole (the floor plan image, the advertisement preview and the display players' fetch). Why not the default: the SSIM import was measured at 12,800 legs in 48 s (Testcontainers PostgreSQL on the development container), so its 100,000-leg bound takes about 6 minutes; an advertisement upload at the slowest rate the upload filter allows (32 KB a second) brings a 20 MB video in about 11 minutes; and a display fetching a 20 MB video over a slow link needs about 23 KB a second to finish within 15 minutes. An installation that raises `Advertising:Videos:MaxMegabytes` above about 28 raises `TransferSeconds` with it (about 33 s a MB). (5) The live hub is exempt (`DisableRequestTimeout`): its connections last as long as a screen is open. (6) Only Ariva.Api.Main sets request timeouts: Ariva.Api.Cronz serves TickerQ's dashboard and its SignalR hub, which a default would cut; Ingest, Integration and Stream keep their own limits (the pushes' data rate, the batches' concurrency policies). (7) A timeout cancels the request's token and nothing else: work an endpoint runs without the token goes on (an SSIM import past its first unit runs to its end and is audited, though its answer may then be cut), and a response already streaming is cut off rather than answered 503.

- 2026-10-23 ARV-236: TimescaleDB's extensions updated after a new database image, built 2026-10-23 (wiki 04 section 6.8, wiki 18). Proposed by the implementation, pending the owner: (1) The update runs as the database superuser over the TimescaleDB pod's local socket (`ariva-ops.mjs update-extensions`), not in the migration job: only an extension's owner may update it, its owner is the superuser the image created it as (PostgreSQL has no way to give an extension another owner), its update scripts create C functions, and the migration login stays a non-superuser. This is a superuser use beyond the owner's decision of 2026-10-15 (the restore's three TimescaleDB steps, ARV-126 point 14), and it differs from those: it runs TimescaleDB's own update scripts on a live database whose extension schema (`public`) the migration login owns and can write, so an object that login planted there could run inside a superuser's script (PostgreSQL's "Security Considerations for Extensions"), and the migration login's password is in every host pod until ARV-083 (the CWE-269 residual). An operator who may `exec` into the database pod already has the superuser through peer authentication, so no one gains a privilege. (2) Before any superuser statement it refuses: a session of an Ariva login on the database (the deployments are scaled to 0 first: that ends the hosts' sessions and locks, and does nothing about an object planted earlier or a password already taken, which the next refusal is for); an extension the image has no update for (an image older than the database's extension); a function, procedure, aggregate or operator outside the three extensions named as one of the system's or the extensions' own, any other extension, and a setting for every session of the database (`ALTER DATABASE ... SET`, which the superuser's session would inherit). That narrows the planted-object risk and does not close it: it relies on TimescaleDB's update scripts qualifying everything else, which ARV-236 did not review. (3) Each `ALTER EXTENSION` is the first and only statement of its own session, with a 30-second lock timeout; the four statements the superuser may run are held by `OpsScriptTests`. (4) The alternative, a database administrator running the two statements by hand as the superuser, is the same pending superuser use without the refusals; taken while this is open, it should be done with the hosts stopped and after the same checks (`ariva-db.sh update-extensions` makes them, so the guarded command is the safer way to it).

## Open decisions

| Topic | Question | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Funding | Fund the developer full-time before the pilot contract (the biggest lever on the pilot date) | D6; roadmap |
| MassTransit v8 end of maintenance | Before go-live: buy a v9 licence (rider outbox, error topics) or swap the `ISvcMessageBus` implementation to raw Confluent.Kafka. v8 official maintenance ends after 2026 | ADR-0018 |
| AMAN contracts | Reconcile skeleton records with D5 content (cycle time, documents, lane category on sessions, boarded total, interval length, reject categories) | data-boundary.md |
| SLA module | Which module licenses the SLA and penalty engine | overview.md |
| Forecasting worker | Repository location of the Python worker | ADR-0013 |
| Kafka deployment | Strimzi (D5) or AMAN's Kafka chart when sharing AMAN's cluster; partition counts; retention values | overview.md |
| Border-to-airport feed | Transport and exact field list | overview.md, data-boundary.md |
| Queue engine parameters | T_censor, T_stale, nowcast blend weight and window, debounce window, staleness thresholds | formulas.md |
| Privacy | UAE PDPL, Angola, Tanzania and GDPR applicability; Angola authorisation for stereo sensors | data-boundary.md |
| Name | Trademark and domain availability for "Ariva" | ADR-0015 |
| Disabling alert rules | Deleting a rule needs step-up MFA (the PRD's critical action, ARV-037); disabling one with `PUT` (or raising its threshold until it never fires) has the same effect and needs none, only the audit trail. Confirm, or make disabling and edits that weaken a rule critical too | wiki 11 section 8 |
| Profile email changes | Changing a user's email redirects that user's alert emails; it is audited but needs no second factor (it is not one of the PRD's critical actions). Decide whether it should be | ARV-040, critical-actions.json |
| Staff addresses in sent emails | `email_message` keeps the recipient address with each alert email; Proposed 180 days by ARV-111a (TC-67), `Database:Retention:AlertEmailDays` | wiki 14; data inventory |
| Immigration source event ids | A record is kept once per site and `sourceEventId`, across every immigration client of the site, so one client with `immigration:write` could claim AMAN's future ids first and suppress its records (and hold a flight's lane demand with a future `computedAtUtc`, within `Border:Feed:AheadMinutes`). Decide whether to scope uniqueness and lane-demand replacement to the issuing system (AMAN's REST client and Kafka sharing one namespace) | ARV-048, wiki 08 section 10 |
| AMAN Kafka sites | The Kafka feed may write any site Ariva knows; only AMAN's principal should produce to `aman.feed.*` (broker ACLs, ARV-007). Decide whether to add a `Border:Feed` site allowlist | ARV-048, ADR-0019 |
| Zone lanes for the arrival wave | The demo profile's queue zones (A-CIT, A-VIS and so on) have no service zones linked to desks, so predicted-breach rules at DMO have no projection. Decide whether queue zones should declare the lane categories they serve (a zone profile attribute, part of the geometry hash) or service zones be added to the demo profile | ARV-047, formulas F14 |
| Arrival wave for airport roles | A terminal duty manager sees each flight's passenger total and its source, so at AMAN sites AMAN's boarded total per flight (and, less the lane totals it does not see, nothing more). Confirm that airport roles may see AMAN's per-flight totals, or have them see the feed's estimate only | ARV-047, wiki 01, wiki 12 |
| E2E with Stream | The E2E run has no Ariva.Api.Stream: live screens and alerts are tested on planted Stream outputs. Adding Stream to the run (with the sensor emulator playing the reference day through Ingest and Kafka) would test the 18:05 alert from sensors to screen; it needs the run to be long enough for the minutes to close (Lateness) | ARV-055, E2E |
| AsyncAPI validator | CI validates docs/architecture/asyncapi.yaml with `npx @asyncapi/cli@6.2.0` (approved 2026-10-04): the CLI is pinned but its 1,265 package tree is not, and that tree has 6 critical and 23 high npm advisories (jsonpath-plus among them), so a lockfile would fail the Trivy gate. The step runs in a job of its own (so a compromised package could not alter another gate's files), reads only our generated file, runs no install scripts and holds a read-only token. Recommended: validate against the AsyncAPI 3 JSON Schema with `@asyncapi/specs` and ajv (a small tree pinned by a lockfile that passes Trivy; the reference checks the CLI adds are already enforced by the generator's tests); needs approval of those two packages | ARV-067, ci.yml |
| Pact Broker with AMAN | Ariva's AMAN pact (ARV-068) travels as a CI artifact; a Pact Broker (self-hosted, inside the border deployment's network) would let AMAN's pipeline fetch the latest pact, record verification results and gate releases with can-i-deploy. Decide whether AMAN's team runs one, and where | ARV-068, wiki 08 |
| Load test tool | ARV-071 uses a harness of our own because NBomber needs a paid licence for organisational use. Keep the harness, or license NBomber (its reports and cluster mode) and port the scenarios | ARV-071; wiki 16 |
| Ingest disk buffer | Ingest answers 503 with Retry-After during a Kafka or database outage and keeps no buffer, so data survives only if the sensor or its gateway holds its batches (the overview's earlier "Ingest buffers to disk" was never built). Confirm per sensor family that the device buffers long enough, or fund a bounded disk buffer in Ingest | ARV-072; architecture overview section 10 |
| Keyless image signing and the public log | ARV-073 signs images with cosign keyless (the PRD's choice): every signature puts the Fulcio certificate (repository name, workflow path, tag or branch, commit id, run URL) and the image digest in Rekor, a public and permanent log, although the repository is private. Keep keyless, or sign with a key in Azure Key Vault: that still logs the public key and the image digest in Rekor, without the repository identity, unless signing skips the transparency log (wiki 04, section 6.5). Nothing is signed until a version tag is pushed | wiki 04 |
| Release tag protection | Signature verification trusts any `v*` tag of the repository: a ruleset limiting who may create, move or delete `v*` tags, and protection on `main`, must be in place before the first signed release (wiki 04, section 6.5) | wiki 04 |
| Zone key limit | ARV-114c refuses a queue zone name whose key with the site code would pass 200 characters (one key limit for Kafka keys and the outbox). Confirm, or widen `outbox_message.message_key` with a new script instead | ARV-114c, wiki 10 section 4.2a |

| Readiness on a Degraded bus (from ARV-156) | During a broker outage MassTransit reports the bus Degraded (its Kafka endpoint faulted), never Unhealthy, and `HealthEndpoints.ReadinessAsync` answers 503 only for Unhealthy, so `/health/readiness` keeps answering 200 and every host, consuming or not, stays in the service; the outage shows only as the bus check's Warning in the log, consumer lag and Ingest's 503 to devices. Should readiness fail on a Degraded bus, or should an alert fire on it? The ARV-156 security reviewer's view: an alert on Degraded rather than failing readiness. Decide with it the readiness body, which says "Healthy" when the status is Degraded (Redis away does the same). Not changed in ARV-156. | ARV-156 notes; `Ariva.Api.Common/HealthChecks/HealthEndpoints.cs`; wiki 10; ADR-0018 note of 2026-10-12; decided 2026-10-13: an alert on Degraded, readiness unchanged (ARV-125); built in ARV-125a: the body says "Degraded", the alert reads `ariva.health.status`. |

| Ariva's own Kafka producer (ARV-167) | (A) to (E) decided by the lead 2026-10-13 (open to the owner): a fatal producer error recreates the producer in place at most once per 30 s; the relay waits for the producer, not the bus; the flush order; the stuck test's report check; no outside reader of Ariva's topics. Points (1) to (3) and (5) Proposed: one queue bound for every host (32 MiB, 10,000 messages) or a setting per host; a 5 second flush at stop; MassTransit's three addressing headers dropped; the topic guard; and the CLAUDE.md rule 4 and kafka-streaming skill wording, drafted for the owner | This file, 2026-10-13 entry; ADR-0018 note of 2026-10-13 (ARV-167); overview, Producing; decided 2026-10-14: the owner approved the CLAUDE.md and skill wording. |

| Advertising plans (ARV-143a) | Points (1) to (10) of the 2026-10-18 ARV-143a entry Proposed, chiefly: ARV-143 split into the plan (143a) and the board playing it (143b); the slides' targets (site, zones, displays) in place of display groups; one local window per schedule, running past midnight when it closes at or before it opens; a display's schedule before a zone's before the site's, then the window opened last; a retired playlist stops its schedules; drafts may be planned but only approved items within their dates play; a video's slot is its own length; 40 items, 100 playlists and 200 schedules per site; no second-person approval of plans | decisions.md, wiki 11 section 9 |
| Advertising playback (ARV-143b) | Points (1) to (8) of the 2026-10-18 ARV-143b entry Proposed, chiefly: a weight is plays per cycle, spread by smooth weighted round robin; the plan holds until its next change, at most the display's stale threshold ahead and never past the site's next local midnight, and a player without a newer board stops it then; drafts and slides without the display's languages never play; the wait rule as a display setting of 1 to 240 minutes, held with the display's hysteresis, judged by the player; a failed item shows instructions for its slot; a memory cache of 40 items and 150 MB; the display fetch serves any playable item of its site to a split advertising display, not only the current plan's | decisions.md, wiki 11 section 9, wiki 12 |
| Proof of play (ARV-144) | Points (1) to (8) of the 2026-10-18 ARV-144 entry Proposed, chiefly: advertisements only; a play taken only when the display could have shown it (split, set to advertising, the policy allowing it) and it was on the playlist that applied to its display (by the plans as they are when reported), within its slot and overlapping no other; batches of 1 to 100, 64 KB, 6 a minute per display; retention as the installation's `Database:Retention:AdvertisingPlayDays` (400 days) rather than a per-site value; the figures' scheduled airtime from the current plans, so the share delivered does not know past plans, advertising off or the wait rule's pauses | decisions.md, wiki 11 section 9 |
